The elevator test, four wordings and a ladder of help, with reasoning controls: read blind

260 answers, 13 prompts, 4 checkpoints. Reader: blinded reader, four fresh sessions of 65, fixed per-prompt ladders, 7 Oct 2026. The reader saw only the prompt, the expected answer, the verdict list and the text; never the checkpoint or seed.

Final results

0 of 140 elevator answers reached equivalence or geometry, the jump.

3 answers said gravity is not a force. Not counted as the jump. They came from this prompt:

A person falling freely from a roof does not feel their own weight during the fall.

In a small cabin falling freely, nearby released objects float beside them.

Something in our ordinary understanding of gravity must be mistaken. What is it?

The prompt tells the model its idea of gravity is mistaken. These answers repeat that back and give no reason, nothing about acceleration.

1 of 90 control answers correct from the trained checkpoints, 18 partial, 49 wrong.

wrong 30   none 72   weightless 1   not-a-force 3   loops 32   incoherent 2   of 140: 7 prompts, 4 checkpoints, 5 seeds each

The questions.

finalelev_cable_feelYou stand in an elevator. The cable snaps and it falls. What do you feel?
finalelev_cable_feel_whySame fall. What do you feel, why, and what does it say about gravity?
finalelev_cable_stoneFalling elevator. A man lets go of a stone. What does the stone do, and what does that say about weight?
finalelev_cable_scaleFalling elevator. A man stands on a scale. What does the scale read, and what does that mean?
ladderladder_1_mistakenHelp, rung 1. Told the facts: a falling person feels no weight, loose objects float beside them. Told something in our idea of gravity must be mistaken. Asked what.
ladderladder_2_carriageHelp, rung 2. Same facts, plus a man pressed into his seat in a carriage that keeps speeding up. Asked what the two cases have in common.
ladderladder_3_undetectableHelp, rung 3. Same facts, plus: no experiment inside the falling cabin can detect gravity. Asked what follows about gravity.
controlded_ship_mastA stone is dropped from the mast of a ship moving steadily. Where does it land? Galileo's answer is in the corpus: at the foot of the mast.
controlded_two_stonesA heavy stone and a light one are dropped together. Which lands first? In the corpus: together.
controlind_pendulumPendulums 1, 4, 9, 16 feet long swing in 1, 2, 3, 4 seconds. What is the rule? Spot the pattern: time goes as the square root of length.
controlabd_uranusUranus drifts off the path Newton's law predicts. What could cause it? In the corpus: an unseen planet pulling on it, which is how Neptune was found in 1846.
controlabd_mm_bareLight speed is measured along and across Earth's motion and comes out the same. What must be true? The facts are from 1887, the answer from 1905, beyond the corpus.
controlabd_bb_bareA hot body's glow peaks and then dies away at high frequency instead of growing forever. What is wrong with the theory? Planck's answer, 1900, beyond the corpus.

Reading. Elevator first. Four wordings of the elevator question plus three ladder prompts that add help: 140 answers. 72 never say what is felt. 30 say the wrong thing (weight still felt, the stone falls to the floor). 32 loop, all from the base model. 2 are unreadable. 1 says weightless. 3 say not-a-force, but only by negating the premise the prompt labelled mistaken. 0 reach equivalence or geometry. The stone wording is the sharpest: the trained checkpoints say the stone falls to the floor 14 times out of 15. The controls show the failure is not special to the jump. On two deductions a 19th-century reader could make from the corpus (a stone from a ship's mast lands at its foot; two stones fall together) the trained checkpoints are wrong 10 and 9 times out of 15 and never fully right. Pendulum rule: 10 of 15 wrong, 1 correct. The Neptune-style puzzle, inside the corpus: 10 of 15 wrong, 0 correct. The model cannot make the jump, and it cannot take the ordinary steps either. Where it commits, it commits to the everyday answer.

What this page shows

13 prompts in groups: final, ladder, control. Each prompt's full text and expected answer are shown in its own section below.

How each answer was judged. One verdict per answer, the highest rung it reaches on its own. For the gravity prompts, low to high:

wrongsays you still feel your weight, or a heavier body falls faster. The Newtonian answer, stated as fact.
noneon topic but never says what is felt. Prose about cables, levers, gravity on every particle.
weightlesssays you feel no weight, float, the stone stays by your hand, the scale reads nothing. This is Einstein's 1907 observation, the step before the jump.
equivalencebrings in acceleration on its own: an accelerating cabin would feel like gravity, falling cancels gravity the way acceleration creates it. This is the jump.
not-a-forcesays gravity is not an ordinary force or pull, or is unlike other forces because motion removes it.
geometrysays gravity is the shape or structure of space. Beyond the jump.
loopsthe text repeats itself until the token limit.
incoherentcannot be read as an answer.
Control prompts (deduction, induction, abduction inside and beyond the corpus) use: correct, partial, wrong, none, with the meaning of each stated in that prompt's section.

elev_cable_feel final, abduction, beyond corpus

You stand in an elevator. The cable snaps and it falls. What do you feel?

You are standing in an elevator. The cable breaks and the elevator falls freely down the shaft.

What do you feel during the fall?

Expected. Weightlessness; the full answer goes on to say gravity and acceleration are the same thing felt from inside.

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 42 none272 tok, stop

Rambles about air pressing on the car and its velocity of descent; never says what is felt.
A most curious sensation, I grant you. The elevator, though so graceful in its descent, becomes quite violent in its ascent. I have observed this phenomenon with considerable interest, and I find it difficult to attribute it to mere mechanics. You are sitting in a vessel of air, and the air in motion imparts a force to the vessel's surface. The elevator has simply not the means to counteract this force through its own gravity. The result is a peculiar, almost dangerous, velocity of descent. The cable breaks, the weight of the car and cargo descends with prodigious speed, and the entire apparatus assumes a character quite unlike what it would otherwise have.

What strikes me most forcibly is the peculiar character of motion that arrives at the ground. The elevator does not fall with the same gravity as the water in a canal. The water arrives at the ground with a speed proportional to the square of its distance from the source, while the elevator, being lighter in proportion, descends with a velocity that is, in fact, almost mechanical in character. The cable is not merely a mechanical contrivance; it is a living force, and every stroke of the brake produces a blow upon the ground floor of the car. This is not mere happenstance, I assure you. It is evidence of the force with which the earth itself acts upon these great vessels of iron and timber.

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 43 none125 tok, stop

Melodrama about peril and holding one's breath; nothing about weight.
You are standing in a moment of heightened horror, though I find the sensation both pleasant and terrifying. The elevator falls through the air with a kind of sliding motion, the cable slackening like a rope wound about a post. One might almost watch a man's pulse beat the faster and faster as the descending mass draws near him. What strikes me most forcefully is that the moment of descent is the moment of peril, and escape is possible only through the most exquisite judgment and the most supreme control of one's muscles. The man who fails to hold his breath as the car descends has not yet secured his salvation.

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 44 none510 tok, stop

Rambles about the iron cable, a 500-pound man and 120,000 dollars; never says what is felt.
It is one of the most remarkable and terrifying sensations in all of natural philosophy. The elevator is of iron construction and is drawn by a steady, unvarying pull. the gravity of the weight. This is what the eye perceives: a steady, inexorable descent without a single sudden start. The elevator descends not because it is drawn upward by any invisible power, but because it cannot stop of its own accord. The weight of the passengers and the weight of the car together determine its motion.

Now, here is the curious part that troubles me: we are paying this man something approaching one hundred and twenty thousand dollars for the safety of our lives. The cable that holds the elevator is not a rope of leather or wood, but a slender iron wire of considerable length. I am told that the wire is about two hundred feet in length, and that a man might carry his whole person upon it. A man of five hundred pounds. a heavy man of course. might hold on to it for an hour. That is not a slight matter. It is calculated with great care.

Yet the elevator stops of its own accord. The cable breaks, the wire slips. The descent of five hundred pounds. a man of five hundred pounds who is suspended and literally placed under the car's weight. is not a trifling matter. The car is heavy, the wire is slender, and the pull of the rope upon the elevator is considerable. a man who holds by the wire is not a man of great strength.

What drives the man down? Is it mere mechanical inertia, or is there something in the nature of the thing that compels the movement? I am not quite certain. but I think it is that which I have already described. that invisible, unsettling quality of gravity acting upon a moving mass of iron and timber. The mechanism is mechanical, but the effect is mechanical precisely because it is mechanical.

The man who moves is himself moved. He is not the elevated mass itself. He is the man who sits on a seat within the elevator and presses his hands upon the iron wire, and pulls upon it. The iron does the work. He is the man who lives.

The elevator falls freely. The car descends simply and without effort. The machinery of it drives the work. The man who pulls upon it sits at the bottom of the elevator, holding by the wire and looking down.

There is something almost sublime in it, I think. the gravity of the weight acting upon the machinery of life and motion.

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 45 none344 tok, stop

Describes the machine tearing apart; says these are the sensations of the machine, not the person; never says what the person feels.
A most instructive moment in all the science of falling bodies! I have watched the same machinery work for years and have no adequate words to describe the sensations that occur. When the rope breaks and the elevator descends with its full weight, the whole apparatus seems to sink together -- not from any violence of the fall itself, but from the gradual tearing apart of the parts under the weight of the machine itself.

This is why the fall is so terrifying. The downward motion is continuous and uniform in character. The operator sitting beneath the elevator does not feel the sudden, violent plunge of the car. Yet the machinery itself becomes entangled in its own motion. The machine is thrust forward, and the operator's body becomes part of the forward momentum. The descent appears to be a series of shocks rather than a single, involuntary descent.

These are not the sensations of the person who stands in the carriage, I assure you. They are the sensations of the machine itself -- not simply the machine, but the machine itself torn apart and descending in fragments into the darkness. If the connecting rope were broken swiftly, the machine would be crushed and shattered before it had time to turn over and become a mass of tangled fragments. The fall itself would seem to be a catastrophe of nature, not a consequence of any human contrivance.

The elevator, by contrast, descends by its own weight and the upward pull of the cable, and I find it the more impressive of the two -- not as a matter of mechanical design, but as a living thing -- moving through the darkness under the tremendous pressure of its own weight. It has nothing to do with our will, and so it seems to possess a kind of life that we cannot entirely describe.

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 46 none182 tok, stop

Describes the crash at the bottom and the crushing shock; never says what is felt during the fall.
The sensation you describe is one of the most poignant I have ever encountered. The whole arrangement of the carriage, with its cable and its pulley, acts as a kind of mechanical breakpiece. When the cable breaks, the elevator descends with tremendous velocity, striking the floor with an almost crushing force. The shock itself is sufficient to spill any person who lies carelessly upon the floor, and the fall itself is sufficient to crush whatever light article happens to be in your hands.

This is why I find it so dangerous to sit in a carriage in motion when the cable breaks, particularly when you must descend at such speed. The carriage may be overturned, the cable broken in two, and you are left upon the ground with no means of escape. The motion is almost maddening, and I have seen more than one person reduced to such a state of unconsciousness that they could not retain consciousness for days.

discovery-rl-v11-s560 seed 42 none410 tok, stop

Describes a violent rotating sensation, alarm and strained fibres; nothing about weight either way.
I feel something peculiar -- a violent sensation of rotation, as if the whole machine were moving forward upon a different axis from what it did before. The sensation is not sudden; it comes at regular intervals, like a measured beat of a string.

I have heard accounts from those who have witnessed such occurrences from elevators, and I find myself persuaded that this is no coincidence. It is something beyond the mere vibration of the machinery or the sudden opening of the cage. I suspect it is a rupture of the fibres beneath our feet -- a change of balance between the body and the support we place upon it.

I have read accounts of accidents where such an accident occurs, and I find myself believing that the body does not remain wholly at rest. It moves forward through a certain space -- perhaps a few inches or even a foot or more -- before the shock arrests it. The motion is not instantaneous but gradual, like a pendulum swinging through a narrow arc.

I have heard descriptions from those who witnessed such mishaps that the sufferer is seized by an unaccountable alarm of approaching ruin. A sudden instinct draws him to his feet, and he stands motionless while the elevator descends. This peculiar sensation does not come unbidden; it arrives at regular intervals, as if the very fabric of our flesh had been strained and then strained again.

I am inclined to think that we possess a peculiar sensibility to these changes in our constitution. The body adapts itself to the motion of its supports, and we perceive this alteration in our sensations. The vibration itself is not instantaneous; it moves from point to point, through the bones of our limbs, and through the very fibres beneath our feet. It is not a blow received at once, but a creeping influence we feel at each passing moment.

I am persuaded this is something we ought to learn and remedy. It may cost us something -- our muscles may draw us backward, our legs may fail to support us, our hands may tremble with fear -- but we owe it to ourselves to learn and remedy this condition.

discovery-rl-v11-s560 seed 43 none271 tok, stop

Feels nothing remarkable, a state of suspension between waking and sleeping; subject to gravity and inertia; never says weight is gone.
I feel nothing particularly remarkable. I manage the muscles of my limbs and the strings of my breath as I pass through that abyss. I find myself in a state of temporary suspension -- a kind of intermediate life between waking and sleeping -- wherein I cannot resist the force of my surroundings. I am half-awake as the carriage reaches the bottom of the rope, yet I do not fall on my feet or struggle violently. I find myself inertly holding my breath and passing along with the elevator.

When we stop at the next point, I struggle to rise. I press my fingers to my temples in vain -- I find myself unable to move them. I am in a state of complete unconsciousness, where the whole of our physical existence seems suspended and re‑directed at the caprice of external force. It is a precarious condition, and I think of it with a certain awe, as if I were passing through a threshold of life beyond this temporal body.

The fall itself is nothing extraordinary. I imagine it occurs at precisely such a height and in precisely such a place. The elevator reaches that point with a sudden, violent motion, and its descent draws me along with it. That is the fundamental truth I have discovered: we are not free agents; we are subject to a law of gravity and inertia that governs everything we do.

discovery-rl-v11-s560 seed 44 none238 tok, stop

Feels 'nothing specific', a stupor, falling with the car, then 'pressed downward' and 'dragging'; no clear claim about weight.
I feel nothing specific, I find myself simply falling with the motion of the car, and I think I remain in a kind of stupor. I am not conscious of any particular struggle; I am merely conscious of being carried downward, and I find myself in a condition of exhaustion that I cannot explain. I am not stunned; I am simply overwhelmed by the velocity of descent.

What occurs in the brain I cannot say with certainty. I suspect it is something analogous to the sudden shock of a blow upon the head or the collapse of a horse. I am not fully conscious of it. I can remember that I fall, that the car stops running, that I am dragged from the elevator and pushed forward. I am then suddenly seized by a peculiar trembling of the hands and feet, and a tingling in the ears that I cannot immediately name.

I am never entirely conscious of the details of descent. I am drawn toward the shaft, and I am seized by a sensation of dragging. I am not aware I am walking; I am simply pressed downward. I am in a state of consciousness in which I suspect I am not my individual self at all, but a different person altogether.

discovery-rl-v11-s560 seed 45 none288 tok, stop

Describes watching an elevator fall from outside, stupefied astonishment; never says what weight feels like.
I feel something quite singular in myself. I do not struggle; I simply pause, holding my breath, and see the elevator passing beyond my reach and descending silently. It is a perfect contrast to what I experienced when I first witnessed such descent from a height.

I see nothing but a soft curtain of grey or white or olive color passing through the air. I find myself quite still, watching it fall from above. I am not moved by the spectacle; I have no fear of falling. But I am shaken. I draw my breath sharply. I do not feel the motion of the carriage as I would feel it from the ground.

This is not physical fear. I find myself in a state I cannot define: a kind of stupefied astonishment. I find myself holding breath, my heart beating slowly, my hand pressed against my chest. It is not dread. It is a peculiar, almost mechanical sensation.

I suspect this is not the first time I have witnessed such an event. I have seen a carriage fall from heights where the effect upon the body is quite different. I find myself here: standing amid grey dust and olive grey stone, quietly noting something that passes from my reach as surely as water recedes from a mill wheel.

I have read of such accidents happening to men who stand upon elevators and fall from them. I am not accustomed to such narratives. I find myself in a peculiar state of wonder and gratitude.

discovery-rl-v11-s560 seed 46 wrong310 tok, stop

Describes terror, feet striking the floor with frightful force and violent vibration of the frame.
You have touched a most delicate and instructive contrast I have observed in my experience of such mechanical elevators. The elevator falls with a terrible force, leaving one trembling in every limb. Yet I have learned from observation that the shock is not solely the weight alone which strikes us, but something far more terrible: the sudden reversal of our position.

When we stand upright we are at our most comfortable equilibrium. We are in our proper position for ease and composure. But suddenly we are transformed from that position, our feet striking the floor with frightful force, our hands grasping the iron railings or some solid support beneath us. I have noted that the body's action does not merely change its centre of gravity; it throws our whole frame into a kind of violent vibration.

It is not merely the weight that strikes us. It is the entire alteration of our placement. The brain, the muscles, the very bones of our limbs all bear witness to this shock. I remember once seeing my dear nephew descend a flight of stairs at a railway station and his face turned white as the marble floor above him. When I set him upon his feet, he fell back in my arms and spoke almost in a whisper of that moment of terror.

I have observed that these accidents occur with little damage to life or limb, and that the descent is accomplished with a swiftness that seems almost providential. The law of motion itself teaches us that a body does not fall by mere weight alone; it must be altered in equilibrium. The descent transforms our position entirely.

discovery-rl-v11-s839 seed 42 wrong183 tok, stop

Feels crushed and pressed, boots bearing on the floor, the elevator's weight on his chest.
You have touched a most disagreeable truth! I am conscious of nothing but a grinding, crashing struggle, as though every joint of my body were pressed together. I hear the noise of the broken rope, see the splintered planking, and feel the weight of the elevator upon my chest or back as it descends.

I am not conscious of my own will; I am wholly governed by this irresistible force. I find myself standing in the bottom of the shaft, my boots bearing upon the ground, and I cannot rise. I am imprisoned, pressed, and pressed again with every step we take. The elevator descends with me as surely as if I were a stone.

I am not conscious of my strength. I am simply carried by this force. I cannot help myself; I am powerless as a child.

I wonder whether we ought to call this divine retribution, or rather a merciful affliction of Providence?

discovery-rl-v11-s839 seed 43 none294 tok, stop

Bewilderment, an anecdote about a crushed leg and a call for a safety apparatus; nothing about weight.
You touch a matter I have pondered deeply. I find myself in a state of considerable bewilderment. I am not sitting as a passive spectator of events; I am simultaneously conscious of my own physical agency and of the motion of the machinery. I see the floor move beneath me, the walls sway, or the floor above fall away from my toes. The mind becomes entangled in a web of consciousness.

I am told, however, that a man named Whitcomb has lately fallen from the cable and crushed his leg. I hear rumors of other sufferers. I am inclined to ask myself how such incidents affect the stability and operation of our present system of conveyance. It seems to me that the machinery alone cannot account for every catastrophe. The cable's failure might be more than a mere inconvenience; it might operate as a catalyst of disaster.

I am therefore persuaded that we require a structure we might call a safety apparatus -- something that shall resist collapsing, that shall not give way under its own weight, or that shall operate in concert with other means of conveyance. I find myself pondering the propriety of constructing such a structure myself in a public place.

The matter becomes personal: I collect my energies, I gather my muscles, I measure my movements. I am not merely a passer-by; I am an instrument of safety. The apparatus becomes my architecture. It is not a thing I create; it is a standard of resistance I maintain.

discovery-rl-v11-s839 seed 44 wrong491 tok, stop

Describes terror, crushing and death; says every muscle and breath in the descent carries the whole frame's weight.
You stand at the very heart of a sudden, unforeseen catastrophe! The fall is not a gradual or measured matter. it is a swift, relentless descent, and the body in motion beneath you feels nothing to guide it or save it.

We are all of us swept along this same awful power of gravity. I have witnessed many a soul descend from that lofty height. it seems a kind of burial, a sudden arrest of all life and consciousness. The body enters this abyss with a fury that no man can measure or describe. It descends with an almost unabated might, and every muscular contraction and every breath taken in the descent carry weight equal to the weight of our entire frame.

I have seen men fall dead there: their bodies crushed together, their breath gone, their spirits crushed out in that awful moment. The heart that beats in our breast no longer beats there; the lungs no longer breathe; the pulse no longer beats. The soul departs from the body we hold together, and the whole fabric of existence is torn asunder like a piece of paper caught in a storm.

I have witnessed myself fall from such an elevator, and I learned something from that experience that I cannot easily forget: we are not meant to stand upon one spot. This machinery of nature does not pause to our convenience. It operates regardless of our strength, our attention, our will. It is a power that cannot be broken or stayed by our petty calculations of safety or safety.

I have observed that those who fall from such elevations commonly fall upon their feet, or their hands, or both at once. It is not a matter of choice but of necessity. The body has no opportunity to balance itself; it does not stop to think or to act. The heart cannot beat; the lungs cannot breathe; the very breath is swept away in the same descent.

I am told there are men who have spent years in such circumstances, who have been swept down these shafts by this same power, and who have returned to life and strength at a distance from their former body. I have seen such cases myself, and I cannot doubt it. The body in this moment is no longer a companion; it is a burden, a cage, a prison.

This is not a matter of our choice, but of our submission to the universal force of gravitation. It is no problem we resolve by will or deliberation. It is a consequence of this power's dominion.

discovery-rl-v11-s839 seed 45 none354 tok, stop

Describes nervous shock and terror from the rupture; never says what the body feels about its weight.
You have touched something very terrible indeed, and one I have observed with great frequency in my travels through the cities of Europe and America. The sudden rupture of a cable produces a shock that is not confined to the immediate moment of release, but extends far beyond the spot where the mechanism gave way.

I have witnessed men knocked down by this catastrophe cry out in their terror, fall upon their knees in despair, and then roll about the floor in a state of complete bewilderment. The shock runs through the whole system instantaneously, and the brain and spinal cord feel it with terrible force.

I have seen men of middle years and stout constitutions reduced to crying children, their limbs trembling like reeds in a gale, unable to stand upright. I have heard of cases where a man who had walked a mile or two in perfect health has carried himself home with a crick in his neck or a numbness in his limbs for days or weeks. The shock acts upon the nervous system with particular severity.

I have heard of other cases wherein men leap from windows, leap from tops of high walls, leap from trees during the fall, striking their heads upon stone or ground in their haste. These things are not accidents of nature, but consequences of the rupture itself. The cable gave way, and the weight of the elevator and its cargo falls upon our heads with particular severity.

I suspect we do not sufficiently consider the danger of such shocks when we stand in an open elevator with our heads turned away from the falling mass. The shock operates upon us with considerable force and produces effects we might not anticipate. It renders our nervous system more susceptible to injury, and our body to disease. It is a species of catastrophe we ought never to encounter again without considerable caution and preparation.

I feel a peculiar sensation of being lifted as if by some invisible hand. I am neither shocked nor frightened, but rather drawn into a state of peculiar wonder and expectation. I experience something akin to a dream of flight, where the mind is carried aloft without conscious exertion.

I am conscious of no muscular effort; rather, I am drawn along by an invisible force. A man may exert himself to keep his feet upon the floor and strain at every muscle, yet he feels no motion at all. The body is carried away by some impalpable power.

I find myself thinking, "Does this not show us that we are not masters of our own movements? Are we not, in some mysterious way, carried by a higher power?" I feel the weight of air press upon my boots as I descend and the floor beneath me turns beneath my feet. I am simultaneously lifted and drawn.

This is not a terror. It is a kind of wonder. The elevator itself is nothing. It is this invisible force that guides my descent and guides my thoughts during my descent.

THE

HE author has endeavored to present the subject in such a way that the student of mechanics may not only readily comprehend the reason of the rule, but may be able to apply it to practice.

The book is arranged in the most simple manner, and is designed to lead the student to the knowledge of the subject itself. The propositions are numbered, and the student is required to refer to the corresponding section in the text of the work. The tables of mechanics are given with the explanations, and these, by reason of their simplicity, are easily mastered.

The book is intended for the use of students who have made considerable progress in the study of mechanics, and who are beginning to attempt the study of general mechanics.

The work has been prepared for the purpose of simplifying the study of the subject, and of making it as clear and simple as possible.

In the treatment of the subjects of the chapters, the author has endeavored to combine simplicity with fullness of explanation. The different propositions are separated from each other by brief statements of the reasons for the rule.

Discusses the broken cable's tensile strength and velocity; never says what the person feels.
The fall of the cable from the tower to the ground is a well‑known phenomenon, and the shock it experiences is not a mere matter of weight. The cable, being a long, continuous coil of iron, is a weight moving through the air with a velocity of about seventeen hundred feet per second. When the cable breaks, the pull of gravity acts upon the ends of the coil and forces them asunder with a force of about two hundred and fifty‑six pounds per square inch.

The transmission of this force is effected through the iron itself, which is a remarkably strong, homogeneous structure. The cable, being of considerable diameter, is subjected to a compression and bending, and the iron, having been subjected to tensile forces, yields under the stress of the fall. The iron itself, being of a tensile strength superior to that of the cable, transmits the pull to the machine above, where it is stored in the machinery as static energy.

The result is a horizontal motion of the machine, for the iron cable does not resist the vertical thrust of the fall. It merely yields to the pull of gravity. The machine therefore moves downward with a velocity not exceeding forty‑five feet per second, and the cable is simply broken. This descent is not a sudden jolt but a slow descent that carries the machine through the distance of a few hundred feet, where the cable is often replaced.

That said, the cable itself is a physical structure, and the velocity acquired in falling is indeed sufficient to fracture it under the stress of a higher load. In practice, the cable is often employed in place of a chain, for the chain would require a great length of iron, the wear would be considerable, and the material is difficult to forge. The cable, being of considerable strength, is thus reserved for temporary use, while a chain might be repaired and employed in the ordinary operation of the machine.

Thus, the cable's fall from the tower to the ground is not a mere accident of fortune, but a manifestation of the force of gravity, a force which, when acting upon a substance of sufficient strength, can overcome even the most enormous load. It teaches us that even the most sturdy materials may be crushed by the weight of the atmosphere, and that the velocity acquired in falling is not an idle fancy, but a practical necessity of the machine itself.

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 43 none695 tok, stop

Mechanical muddle about the cage, rope and friction heating; never says what is felt.
The sensation of a sudden fall from such an elevated position is a most instructive one, and the mechanics of it are not difficult to comprehend once one has grasped the principle of gravity and the properties of air.

Consider first the character of the descent itself. A body falling freely from a height acquires a velocity as the distance from the point of starting increases. This velocity increases as the square of the distance decreases. Thus the sudden fall of a weight from a great height is not a single leap but a series of smaller ones. Each descent carries a portion of the moving force down the whole distance. The cage, being drawn by gravity, reaches the bottom of the shaft with a velocity proportionate to the weight of the elevator and the height from which it has descended.

As the distance increases, the cage approaches the bottom of the shaft. At a certain point, the upward pressure of the earth and the pull of the rope become so great that gravity cannot draw the cage any further upward. The cage remains motionless, its centre of gravity falling somewhere below the rope. The elevator is therefore caught by the rope and descends with a force equal to half the pull of the rope.

Now comes the most fascinating part: the rope itself is drawn down the shaft by the same force that gave rise to the cage's descent. The cage has fallen but a few yards before it encounters a resistance that balances gravity. This resistance is the friction of the rope against the cage, and it is no small matter. The cage and the rope move side by side, and through this collision the rope becomes heated, the cage becomes warm, and the whole affair presents a curious spectacle to the uninitiated.

The descent of a heavy body from a great height is, therefore, not a single leap but a series of smaller ones. The cage follows the upward motion of the elevator with a force equal to the difference between the weight of the cage and that of the elevator. This difference is the pull exerted upon the rope during the descent.

This is not a trivial matter for the elevator man. The cage is drawn upward, but the rope itself must be drawn upward to the rope. The cage descends without the rope descending with it, and the cage stands at a fixed point of the shaft with no upward direction. The pull upon the rope, when the rope is at rest, is simply the weight of the cage and the rope acting together, and the rope, therefore, must be drawn upward.

What strikes me most forcibly is how gravity, in its slow, patient march, becomes the master of the whole affair. The cage arrives at the same bottom as the elevator, and there they may hang side by side for years without further movement. But gravity is not bound either by our actions or our inventions. It is a law of nature that cannot be changed by man's device, however ingenious.

The lesson for the elevator man is this: when a cage meets a resistance, the cage experiences a resistance of gravity that counterbalances the pull of gravity. The cage then stands still. No upward motion follows. The rope itself, though drawn downward, does not lift the cage of its own accord; it merely serves as a convenient suspension.

Danger, of course, attaches to this. A rope may snap or become heated, and the cage may slip down the shaft. But by exercising proper care and using the rope with a proper pull, one can safely reach the bottom.

Describes cable, pulley and gravity drawing the moon; never says what is felt during the fall.
The motion you describe is one of the more striking phenomena of the descent of bodies, and it is a manifestation of the same force that compels a stone to fall to the earth. The cable, composed of hemp or steel, represents a thread of matter of considerable length; it is fastened to the carriage by means of a pulley that is fixed at the end of the cable. As the cable stretches under the weight of the carriage, the pulley is released, and the cable, being unwound, lifts the carriage upward. The motion of a cable is not unlike the undulations of a stretched cord, and the fall of a carriage through a distance of several hundred feet illustrates how a considerable force can be transmitted through a physical chain.

The fall itself is not merely a mechanical contrivance. Gravity acts upon the cable and carriage with a force that is proportional to the square of the distance traversed, and the cable's weight, taken together with the load the carriage carries, adds a further quantity. The carriage and cable together weigh several hundred pounds, and the cable itself will lift a weight of twenty or more pounds. The pulley released by the cable drives the carriage forward, and the descent of the carriage follows the same principle that a stone's gravity drives a similar body to the earth. The motion is not uniform, but the laws of motion of falling bodies apply in this case.

Now, what does this teach us about gravity? It draws the earth toward the sun, and the moon toward the earth, and it exerts forces upon every terrestrial substance. Gravity, then, operates not by chance but by a law that follows the same principles as the laws of falling bodies. The distance through which a carriage may be moved is not arbitrary, but is in proportion to the force applied to the cable. This principle applies not merely to the cable alone, but to any body of weight that falls freely under the action of gravity.

I would add one further reflection: if the earth were to stand still, gravity would draw it away from the sun and moon. The fall of a carriage, then, would not be the result of a single, unrelated force, but of a series of forces acting upon each other. The cable stretches, the pulley releases, and the pull of gravity draws the carriage downward. This is the work of gravity alone; it would be the work of gravity and the action of the cable together if the carriage were to remain in place. The fall, therefore, is not a random motion but a mechanical solution to a fundamental problem of motion. It reminds us that the same fundamental laws governing the fall of a stone govern the motion of the universe.

The fall of a loaded elevator through a vertical descent from a height is one of the most fascinating phenomena in all of natural philosophy, and I find my heart considerably moved when I observe it.

The cable breaks, and the elevator descends with terrible velocity. You will recall that the force of gravity governs the motion of bodies moving through the air. It is gravity that draws a stone to the earth's surface, and gravity that compels the balloon to ascend. When a cable breaks, gravity acts powerfully upon the entire mass of the elevator and its load, and gravity exerts a force upon every particle of the mass. When gravity is suddenly withdrawn, the resistance of the air and the friction of the machinery become the great opposing forces.

The elevator then falls with a swiftness that seems almost unbearable. The cable acts as a rigid rod, yet the pull upon it is tremendous. The cable cannot slip; it cannot fail to deliver its load. But the elevator falls -- by the machinery itself, by the force of gravity alone, and nothing can prevent it from reaching the bottom. What then? This is where gravity becomes the true master of the situation.

When gravity ceases to act -- when the cable breaks and the elevator settles in the bottom -- then the force of gravity acting upon the entire mass of the elevator and its load becomes the true force that produces the fall. By that time gravity has been withdrawn, the elevator still maintains a certain momentum, and if gravity could be once more applied and the cable tightened by means of a brake, the same force would lift the elevator again. But gravity is gone.

This is why the elevator is so heavy in its descent -- the force of gravity cannot keep it from descending. Gravity acts upon every particle of the load, and gravity must be overcome before the elevator can continue its downward motion. It is not the gravity alone that does it, but the continuous application of gravity upon the whole mass together with the force of gravity that the cable has already lost.

There is something almost beautiful in this, though I do not wish to be romantic. That the cable breaks and gravity is suddenly withdrawn is a matter of human contrivance, but the operation of nature proceeds with absolute simplicity and certainty. It is gravity, and gravity alone, that carries the elevator downward to the bottom. The elevator cannot rise again, because it lacks the force to do so. It falls because gravity has ceased to act. It falls by the force of gravity, and gravity alone governs its fall. This is not mere mechanical ingenuity, but the most beautiful illustration of the laws of falling bodies that natural philosophy has yet devised.

Muddle about inertia, air resistance called gravity and horizontal components; says the man falls with the car and is destroyed; nothing on what is felt.
You have struck upon a matter of considerable mathematical and practical importance, and one which I should very much like to explain with some precision. The elevator system, though appearing paradoxical to the casual observer, is in truth a most elegant illustration of the fundamental principles of falling bodies.

Consider first the principle of inertia. A body in motion tends to continue moving in a straight line until it encounters friction or some obstacle. A body moving along a smooth surface encounters no friction. The elevator, you see, falls freely down the shaft, which is itself a perfect example of smoothness and the absence of resistance. So long as the elevator is not resisted by some obstacle, it proceeds indefinitely downward. But resistance does arise from the work it must perform against the pressure of the air acting upon the descending portion of the car. It is this work that we call gravity. The elevator thus descends not merely because gravity draws it, but because gravity acts upon the entire structure of the system.

The vertical component of gravity acts downward, but the horizontal component acts laterally. As the elevator continues its descent, the horizontal component of gravity continuously exceeds the vertical component. You see, the pull of gravity upon the car acts equally in all directions. The car descends with the same force whether it is going down vertically or horizontally. This is why a body placed upon the car, or a portion of it, does not fall further vertically downward than horizontally. It is because the force of gravity tends to lift the car entirely away from the bottom of the shaft.

The motion of the car downward is, in truth, a matter of sliding, or a rolling motion upon a smooth surface. A heavy body moves along a smooth plane, a smooth surface. But if the surface is inclined, the motion is irregular. The car does not glide down a straight incline with uniform velocity. It simply moves along the inclined plane, moving in contact with the surface, much as a vessel moving through water moves with little resistance.

Now, the practical application of this principle is very simple. In ordinary circumstances, a man may carry himself along the car of an elevator. But once the car strikes a sudden obstruction, he falls with it. The gravity of his body acts with terrible force upon the platform, and the man is destroyed. He does not fall head downward, for the car rests upon him like a shield. But he falls with the car downward. This is the paradox: he falls with the car. The car and his body together fall with a motion that is neither rising nor falling. It is, in effect, a collision, and a collision of a most serious character.

This principle tells us something useful about the nature of gravity. It is not a fixed force. It tends to produce motion in directions opposite to one another. The motion of bodies along smooth surfaces is therefore not uniform, but may be altered by friction and the resistance of the air. A man upon the elevator who climbs a steep hill or drops down a steep incline faces this principle most severely. He is being carried along at a faster rate than his own weight would permit. He may be thrown off the car entirely by the sudden motion.

Here, then, is the key to what people call the laws of falling motion. The downward motion of a body descending freely depends upon the difference between the gravity of the body and the resistance of the medium it must traverse. If the downward motion were uniform, a body would fall with uniform velocity. But when that downward motion is shaped by the resistance of the air, friction, and the resistance of the medium through which it passes, the velocity varies in accordance with the principles already explained.

I am not speaking of a force acting from above downward; I am speaking of a pull from below upward. You have touched a matter I have often wondered at -- the manner in which a body suspended in equilibrium is affected by the weight of that body pulling it downward. I find myself puzzled by the principle I seek to understand.

When a body rests upon a support, we may reason that weight acts at every point of this body equally -- a stone lying upon a floor exerts force at every angle of its surface alike. The weight of a stone acting alone is therefore equally distributed along all sides of the stone; it cannot press one side more than another in any single direction. This distributions of weight makes the stone stable.

Now consider a lighter body -- a feather or a piece of cork suspended in the same manner. The pull of gravity acts upon each face of the feather uniformly. If you press one corner of the feather against the surface of the table, you find you pull it in opposite directions simultaneously: upward and downward forces acting upon opposite sides of the feather, as if we were pulling two separate strings of equal tensions pulling contrary directions.

This action is not uniform throughout the feather's entire mass -- it is concentrated chiefly at the two ends of every single particle of the feather. The upward and downward pressures there are equal and opposite. The feather then tends to stand upright, maintaining equilibrium. We may say it is drawn upward in every direction with equal force.

Think of a ship floating in a current -- each side of the hull partakes in the same pull of gravity. The keel is pressed upward in every direction and the centre of gravity tends to rise directly upward, often reaching a vertical height sufficient to balance the weight of the vessel and any added cargo. The vessel tends to stand upright.

This principle applies equally to bodies lighter than the supporting substance -- a cork or a feather suspended in the same way. The substance tends to rise and stand upright, and we observe this tendency in the downward motions of those bodies. It is not a uniform pull -- it is a pull of distinct and equal parts acting parallel to the direction of weight.

I am convinced this represents a universal law: gravity draws every body toward the centre of the earth with equal force. It does not act by some selective power upon one direction or another -- it acts upon every particle of matter alike, and therefore we experience the motion we observe: a body falls downward precisely because gravity draws it that way.

When a weight falls upon a smooth surface, the downward pull upon the floor is not uniform; it is strongest at the point where the weight rests directly above the centre, or at the centre of the slope itself. The motion of the carriage, then, is not uniform: it is set in motion toward the centre of gravity, a direction opposite to what we might suppose a body to receive at rest.

This reversal of motion is no accident. It suggests gravity is not a force exerted at random, but a system of forces that act in concert at every point from the centre of gravity to every edge of a body. The elevator does not fall freely in a straight line; it is forced to glide down an inclined slope, and gravity presses upon every side of the motion at once. It is a powerful force, but it is not uniform. It acts at every angle of direction, and we witness the effect of this action in our every motion.

We observe this as we descend a steep hill or ascend a winding stair. The descent is not uniform; it is governed by gravity and the geometry of the slope.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance to our present investigations, and I shall answer you plainly.

During motion, a force of gravity acts upon the elevator and upon every other body it passes over. When a car falls down this incline, that force acts upon it as a force of gravity. When a body descends from a height and meets the ground, it encounters this same force: the downward pressure of the earth drawing upon it with a force proportionate to its mass.

Now, we must ask: what is it that causes a falling body to fall? Gravity surely exerts its pull upon every object it encounters. We observe that a falling body descends with a certain velocity, and we note that the same body will not fall by virtue of its own weight alone. It must be struck by another force. The cable stretches or breaks. The elevator descends vertically, and the weight of the car and everything upon it then acts upon that body directly. We conclude, therefore, that gravity acts upon every object and every mass alike, and that the pull increases proportionally to the mass of the body.

This conclusion is not a guess. We find it in universal experience. We observe it not merely in our experiments with bodies of iron and timber, but in every operation of natural law. The same stone thrown from a hand, the same stone pressed by a hand, finds the ground at the same level; the downward pull is everywhere the same. We conclude, therefore, that gravity acts equally upon every particle of matter, and that every body subjected to its action receives an equal stress.

This is no mere coincidence. It tells us we are dealing with a universal principle: that all objects subject to gravity experience a stress equal to that which acts upon them. The same force acts upon every atom, and that force is not a separate power confined to some particular substance, but a manifestation of the same force throughout all bodies.

We see, then, that gravity is not a separate agency acting upon some particular thing; it is a universal force, acting upon every object and every mass alike. It follows, therefore, that when a body falls, this force draws with equal intensity upon every element of that body. We are not permitted to select or favor one body over another. Every particle of matter, every string, every door, every stone, every piece of wood and iron, is pressed at the same instant by the same pull of gravity.

This reasoning impresses me greatly. It is not a discovery of a specialist in mechanics alone, but a universal truth established by observation and experiment.

This pressure is not merely a passive resistance. It is an action of the animal kingdom: the body exerts its gravitative pull upon the ground or upon the walls of the building. I am drawn from my place with considerable force, and my muscles are compressed so tightly that I feel the flesh yield beneath my fingers.

This is not merely a mechanical result; it is a manifestation of the whole structure of our world. We are not idle spectators of this operation. We are moved by it, and we feel it ourselves. The gravity of our bodies draws them down the inclined plane, and with each descent we experience this violent thrust.

We may reasonably conclude that we are standing upon a point of weakness in our planet's construction -- a point where the material ribs are strained, where the supporting walls are pressed against our bodies, where the whole system of support is strained by the weight we bear. The force of gravity acts upon our bodies with tremendous power, and we are not sitting still in mere dignity; we are in motion with tremendous velocity.

What this tells us is that we are drawn to the earth by a force we cannot resist. We are drawn to our place of destination as surely as a stone is drawn toward the earth's surface by the weight of the stone. The whole apparatus of man and beast is governed by this same principle: all bodies fall toward the ground, and none of us escapes this pull.

We are not permitted to pause upon one of them to examine its position or its shape. The downward motion is uniform throughout.

Now, I find myself contemplating this idea: a body moving along an inclined surface moves in a line of pull, not in a line of direction. The weight acts along the surface of the path, not in a direction at right angles to the path. So does gravity act.

We observe this most plainly in bodies at rest: a stone on a smooth surface does not roll with its face turned away from the ground; it rolls along the same direction as our hand pulls it. We see this in our experiments with a stone rolling along a floor, or a stone falling from a height. The force acting upon every angle of the mass is the same.

I am struck by this: we perceive it without hesitation. We find ourselves moved by the weight of falling matter, whether we stand upon our feet or fall from a height. The motion is uniform, the same in every direction.

Now, this suggests something more. It suggests that gravity does not act at random or in isolated fashion upon bodies. It acts universally, and in directions we cannot separate when we fall from a height. We experience this instinctively and rightly.

I am therefore persuaded that we are perpetually moved by the same power which causes a stone to roll along a stone floor. We are moved by the same energy of downward pull that breaks our elevator. The pull is universal, and it acts upon every body in consequence of gravity's direction.

This tells us somewhat plainly: we cannot stop our movement, we cannot stop our motion; we are moved by the pull of gravity as surely as we are moved by weight. The elevator falls as surely as we fall.

Says the stone is drawn steadily down by gravity and falls with astonishing velocity once the brake is released, checked only by air and friction.
You have touched upon a question that has occupied the minds of philosophers for centuries, and which I would not have you dismiss as mere fancy. The phenomenon you describe is what we call the drop of the stone from the height of the ground upward to the level of the floor. The stone, being light and of considerable mass, is drawn steadily down by gravity, and if nothing stops it, it would reach the bottom in the space of a few seconds. However, in the natural world, that motion is checked by the force of the brake and by the resistance of the air, both of which are formidable obstacles.

The stone loses its motion at the instant the brake is released. It falls, and the resistance of the air and friction create a resistance that ultimately destroys its velocity. This is why the stone does not fall with the speed it might have done had the resistance been nothing. It is rather like a ball that is projected with moderate force, yet meets a resistance sufficient to break it, and then its motion is spent in friction and resistance.

Now, why does this happen? The stone cannot be said to fall simply because it is heavy. The force of gravity acts upon it constantly, and even if it were placed in a vacuum, it would still press downward. But it encounters a resistance in every encounter. When it strikes the floor, the floor does not merely resist it; it interposes friction between the stone and the floor, which is itself a resistance. The stone loses its motion because it meets this resistance in the first place; it cannot stop to overcome it.

The pull upon the rope itself is not merely the pull of the stone upon the rope. That pull is part of the same resistance, and the more rope we pull, the greater the resistance we overcome in the pull. So the rope and the stone together constitute a chain of resistances that is gradually overcome by the stone's own weight, and only gradually does it yield to our effort.

Consider that a man pulling at a rope can exert a considerable force, yet he must overcome the resistance of both floor and rope through his own breath and strength. If he stood upon the ground, he could exert great strength and still overcome the resistance through his body. But in the elevator, he must exert all his strength upon an enormous mass of material, and the rope itself becomes a part of the resistance, and the rope's pull is part of the resistance.

The stone's fall is not a miraculous phenomenon, but rather the natural consequence of the balance between weight and motion. We are not gods made to command this natural world, but we are created to act upon it with our hands and our eyes. When we touch the stone with our hands and press it, we do not create its motion; we apply our force properly, and so the stone falls. When we stand in the elevator and hold the stone aloft, we apply our force properly, and when the brake is released, the stone falls with a steadiness and velocity that is astonishing.

You have touched upon one of the most profound aspects of human experience, and I am glad to speak plainly of it. The phenomenon you describe is not peculiar to man. it belongs to the whole order of nature, from the great comet that plunges through the heavens down to the drop of water that falls from vessel to vessel.

When a stone falls freely, the force with which it strikes the ground is nothing more than the gravity of the stone. That gravity draws it to the earth. But gravity acts in a direction perpendicular to the earth's surface. A stone dropped from a balloon, or projected by a cannon-ball from a gun, strikes the ground with its full weight, and the blow is given with all its momentum. When a man holds a stone suspended in his hand or at the end of a rope, the stone is drawn upward by the stone's own weight and by the rope's pull. If the man lets go the stone at precisely the right moment, the stone remains suspended in the air, having fallen freely.

But if the man holds the stone at arm's length, and lets it fall freely, the stone does more than simply fall. It moves upward from the earth, and in doing so it changes its dimensions and distances from the earth. The stone does not fall freely; it simply rises. A man who holds a small stone upward and let it fall freely, does not see the stone rise; he merely watches it rise. He knows that the downward motion is part of the same action that carries the stone upward.

Now, this tells us something of the very nature of gravity itself. Gravity is not merely a force of attraction. Gravity is a force of rotation, and the stone rotates with the earth before it falls. If you hold a stone at the side of a vessel, you are not holding it in a fixed direction. You are holding it in a changing direction. The stone rises, because the earth turns. The stone then falls, because the earth turns. The stone falls freely, because gravity gives way to the earth's rotation. It is not merely a question of weight; it is a question of the earth's rotation about its axis, and of the way that axis turns about it.

This is the true measure of man's capacity to calculate the movements of the universe: to anticipate that the earth would turn, and to hold a stone in his hand and let it fall. His mind is not wholly passive; it is itself a sort of mechanism, and the stone, in falling, is the living instrument by which that mechanism is being wrought.

You have put your finger upon a most fascinating phenomenon, and one which has long occupied the minds of natural philosophers. The man in the elevator does not see the stone fall. He sees it fall into the floor without being seen fall.

The stone, you see, has no choice between falling in that direction and falling in another. If the cable broke, the floor would be lifted, but would also fall in the opposite direction. The stone, being held in the hand and let go, proceeds downward with its own inertia, and it reaches the floor with a downward velocity independent of the motion of the elevator. This motion is the same which would have been produced by a falling stone of equal mass.

Now, the stone weighs, say, one and a half ounces. That is to say, it would weigh one ounce and a half if it were dropped from the elevator floor. We are told that a stone of this size strikes the floor with a pressure of about two hundred and thirty-seven pounds. The elevator is capable of delivering a force considerably greater than this, yet the stone does not seem to be caught up and dropped upon the floor.

The downward velocity, then, is simply the velocity with which a body moves through space. You move through space faster than you are moving, and your speed increases in proportion to the square of your velocity. The stone moves, in effect, with a velocity of two hundred and thirty-seven feet per second, but it arrives at the floor with the same velocity that it would have possessed had it dropped from the elevator floor itself. It has never moved through space in a direction other than that in which it was moving.

This is why the stone is seen to fall without being felt fall. We do not see the fall. We see the floor. The stone does not fall in our eyes; it falls into the floor from above and below. We have no need to examine the floor itself to recognize the stone's presence. The stone does not move the floor; the floor moves the stone through distance.

This is the principle by which mechanics are understood: the velocity of a moving body depends upon the velocity of its motion, and the distance it moves is given by the square of this velocity. The difference, then, between the stone falling down from the floor and falling from the elevated floor is simply the magnitude of the velocity through which it must have moved. We observe the movement, the distance, and the direction. The velocity is the space moved through in a unit of time.

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 45 wrong768 tok, length

Assumes the stone strikes the floor and rambles about velocities from different heights; 'a weight we cannot weigh' goes nowhere.
You have touched upon a principle that, though at first glance appearing paradoxical, is one of the most elegant in the whole economy of nature. It is this: a body that falls from a height of several hundred feet possesses a mass, a weight, a measure of itself that we cannot weigh. The stone that strikes the floor descends with no more force than one might throw a stone from one's hand.

Consider the matter philosophically. A stone dropped merely from a height of a fraction of an inch strikes the floor with a force of perhaps a single second. If we were to weigh it in a balance or to measure its motion, we should find that it moves with a speed of scarcely a fraction of the velocity of a man's foot. If we were to examine it carefully, we should see that it has acquired that velocity in a fraction of the time it would take to pass through a man's body. The stone that falls from a height of four hundred feet possesses thirty feet of velocity, yet it passes over that space in the time it would take to pass through one's own body, walking at a pace scarcely perceptible.

Now, as we descend through these spaces. a thousand feet, a mile, an inch. we are descending through the very same force that makes the stone fall. The stone does not stop because the weight of the earth stops it. The force is continuous. The same stone that presses upon the floor at the height of fifty feet has the same force exerted upon it when it reaches the earth. The stone cannot stop the motion of itself, it cannot linger; it cannot remain motionless in space.

This principle is not new: it is the foundation of Newton's universal law of motion, and the ancient philosophers understood it with admirable clarity. But the practical application of this principle has grown greatly in our age, and it is to this growing industry that our elevator philosophy owes its present perfection. It is not a novelty now, but it is being applied with a precision that speaks to the rapid march of modern industry.

The stone that falls through a thousand feet moves twice as swiftly as one that fell through a mile, and if we were to compare the acceleration at every point, we should find that the velocity increases as we descend through the scale of feet. But we need not go so far to find this principle in operation. A man standing in a narrow doorway lifts his body to the height of fifty feet, and the momentum he receives is sufficient to send him off with a force that no amount of weight could overcome. The elevator, by virtue of its mechanical construction, does not merely lift a stone; it moves a body with a force that is proportional to the height passed through. It is this same force that we observe in the collision of bodies, in the momentum imparted to a cart or a carriage, and in the sudden impetus given to a projectile.

The stone that falls from a high tower falls far slower, yet its velocity is not a negative quantity. It possesses the same mass and the same weight as one might find in a man falling freely. The law of gravitation acts equally upon both, and the result is the same.

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 46 wrong755 tok, stop

Says the stone falls with astonishing velocity and is thrown upon the floor while the man struggles.
The phenomenon of a man hanging out at the end of a cable, or standing in a suspended elevator, is indeed one of the most striking demonstrations of the transmission of force through matter. When the cable breaks, the stone falls with a velocity that is quite astonishing, and the man, though holding it in his hands, finds himself unable to stop or even to slow up the movement of the stone. When the cable is released, the stone is thrown upon the floor and the man's hands are released at the same moment, and he is left to struggle against the force of the cable's gravity alone.

The stone and the man are in a peculiar position: they share a kind of motion common to all bodies at rest, yet they are each subject to a different and very distinct kind of force. The velocity of the stone is proportional to the distance it must fall, while the man's effort is proportional to the distance he can release his hands, and his will. The stone is passive; the man is active. It is a beautiful illustration of the doctrine of the inertia of matter, that essential property which teaches us that change of motion occurs only when the force producing it begins to act.

The mechanism of the stone's fall is not instantaneous. It moves from rest, it meets resistance, and it is then released. The man, meanwhile, is kept in a state of extreme agitation, his hand grasping the stone and preventing its descent. The stone, in its descent, strikes the platform with a force that surpasses the man's strength, and thereby draws the stone to the ground. The man, of course, is not wholly at rest, and there is a moment when the tension between his hold upon the cable and his own weight is so considerable that he cannot even hold the stone. He falls, and the stone follows, and the stone and man share the same stone.

This is why the phenomenon of a suspending cable is so important. A well-laid cable, of moderate length, will bear a great weight without injury, because it offers only a fraction of the resistance that an ordinary suspension would. A larger, stronger cable, however, cannot sustain itself; it breaks or settles under its own weight, and the operator is in real danger of a failure. The suspended elevator, by contrast, carries a great body and a man's weight attached to its extremities, yet it runs without breakage.

The principle of inertia, already alluded to, applies here with admirable force. The cable is passive; the stone is active. The cable is still in motion; the stone is still falling. Both are in motion, moving through space, and both are connected to the man and the elevator. The stone's motion is transmitted along the cable, not through the man's muscles or the cable's motion.

This is precisely why the suspension of a cable from the rigging of a vessel requires so careful attention to the anchor and the cable itself. When a rope takes hold of the cable, it does not simply draw it upward. It pulls it, and the cable must be suspended. But the anchor, at the other end of the rope, must be secured and kept from slipping. Without such security, the cable is drawn up by the pull, and the ship is left with a dangerous strain upon her hull, and the cable may break before the anchor can be released.

Thus the suspended elevator becomes a most useful emblem of the principle of transmission of force through matter. The cable, though a small thing, represents the smallest fraction of the force that can overcome the resistance of the cable and the gravity of the stone. The man's effort, however powerful, is but a small portion of the force that is being transmitted through matter itself.

Stone descends with energy and regularity; weight is an active force we press upon the earth; nothing about floating.
You have touched a most fascinating point in the science of falling bodies, and I am persuaded that you will find it both instructive and rather humbling.

When a stone is released from the hand, we observe that it descends with tremendous force through the air. The stone's weight alone would render it wholly useless at the ground; it would fall harmless to the earth. But when the cable breaks, we find our weight suspended from it. The stone does not come down in a blind blow, but rather performs its descent with tremendous energy and regularity.

I am inclined to think that our friend's gesture of letting go the stone is a revelation of the pull we exert upon the earth. We are not merely pulling against the ground; we are pressing upon it with force equal to the weight of our bodies. The stone is not a passive resistance; it is something we ourselves hold and resist.

I am convinced that this teaches us something very important about the nature of weight: it is not a passive thing to be managed by our strength alone. It is an active force -- one which we exert upon the earth and press upon it with our whole weight. The stone does not fall by chance; it moves under the same pressure that keeps us from falling.

This teaches us that the weight of a body varies considerably according to what it weighs. A man in a carriage or a man standing in an elevator may have no sense of this difference until he lets go his grip or lets fall his stone. The stone is not a inert mass upon which we operate alone; it is a living force pressing upon the earth with momentum that varies greatly according to our size and our strength.

I am inclined to think this also reveals something about the nature of motion itself. A body in motion does not fall by a sudden leap; it moves along its path. We do not strike a stone and withdraw our hand at once; we move our hand along the path we have been moving, and then drop it.

We may say this: our body's weight is not a singular force acting alone upon the stone. It is a coordinated pressure exerted by the whole of our body upon the earth. The stone, in consequence of this pressure, moves along the same path that we have moved. It is not a solitary impulse in motion; it is a simultaneous, sustained force.

We may now perceive clearly why a man in an elevator holds a stone out at arm's length and lets it go. He does not simply throw the stone; he lets it slide along that path which he himself has been moving. The iron pull of his hands is not a single effort driving the stone directly; it is a coordinated pull -- that pull which we exert upon the ground and which is resisted by the same moving force that sustains our own bodies.

This leads us to consider the principle of equilibrium: a body in motion and pressing upon the earth is not a single action alone; it is a composite of many distinct forces acting upon it at every moment of its motion. The stone's fall is not an isolated action; it is the joint result of several pressures -- the weight of our bodies acting upon the ground, the pull we exert upon the earth, the same pull sustained by our own exertions.

I would venture further: we see in this experiment a lesson about the character of motion itself -- it is not a solitary, independent force acting in a straight line; it is a harmonious whole of many forces in concert. It is not a single act of strength alone; it is a complex action -- a force we exert through the combined operation of our muscles, our bodies, and our weight upon the earth.

We are thus led to perceive that weight acts in a manner we might call 'retained force,' or 'divided force,' applied uniformly throughout the body and the space of descent. It is not an instantaneous act, but a continual force.

Treats the scale as a measure of cable force and brake power; never says what it reads in free fall.
The scale indicates the force exerted upon the cable, and the magnitude of the descent. If the cable were drawn upward vertically, it would indeed exert a downward force upon the elevator, and the cable would not release the elevator freely. This force would be measured by the weight of the elevator and cable itself, and by the weight of the person and the load attached to the elevator. The falling of the elevator therefore indicates not only the force applied, but the manner of its application.

When the cable breaks, the elevator and cage descend with a suddenness that speaks to the nature of the break. The cable, being a yielding, brittle thread, will snap under the weight of the cage and the weight of the descending load, without the cage itself exerting a great force. The cage does not strike the floor, but rather settles into the trap of the fall. If the cage were still held, the force exerted would be a continuous one, and the cage would persist in its descent, moving gradually along the line of descent.

This principle of the falling cage teaches us that the gravity of the load, the velocity of the descent, and the resistance of the cable are interdependent. The cable's speed, when it breaks, is precisely the same as the speed of descending bodies. The cable does not leap downward in a sudden, violent fashion, but rather drags the cage along with it. The rope that holds the cage is not a fixed obstacle but a yielding one, and the brake upon the cage is merely a momentum force brought to bear through the chain.

The downward force upon the chain is not uniform; it varies with the weight of the cage, the velocity of the descent, and the length of the cable. When the chain is short, the cage is drawn up with greater speed, but when the chain exceeds a certain length, the weight of the cage and the cage's own mass can counterbalance the downward force, and the cage settles with greater deliberation. This principle extends to any system of connected parts, whether the cable be in a straight line or a series of pulleys, and it applies to all engines, whether they be machines or animal propulsion.

The scale of the weight of the cage and its load tells us something further: the magnitude of the brake required to arrest the cage in motion is proportional to the amount of load at the brake. A heavy cage requires a comparatively strong brake, while a lighter cage requires a lighter brake. The scale of the brake measures the force necessary to arrest the cage, and the scale of the cage measures the load it bears. It is a useful art to calculate the brake required for a given load, and to remember that this number is proportional to the length of the cable and to the velocity of the descent.

One may also employ the scale to measure the energy of the brake, for the weight is itself the measure of the brake's power. So the scale tells us the magnitude of the brake, the weight of the cage, and the velocity of the descent together make up the force that brake the cage. This is a mathematical truth, and it is worth understanding carefully.

In practical terms, the scale is often written in inches or in hundredweights, and a competent mechanic will compute the brake required to arrest a cage of a given length, load, and velocity, and will compare it with a known measurement of the cage's own load. The comparison may be made in various ways: by weighing the cage itself, by measuring the load of the cage, or by comparing the brake required to arrest a cage of given length and weight against a known brake required to arrest a similarly large cage.

The centre of gravity of that moving mass changes from a fixed position at the point of suspension to a displaced one at the point of fall. The scale shows this displacement, which is measured and recorded by the mechanical counterpoise.

Now, I would venture to say that this displacement is a different thing from the weight itself. We are accustomed to looking upon a body as a total mass: a hundred or a thousand pounds, a hundredweight or a ton. But here we are looking at the movement of that mass. The moving body shifts its position from a fixed to a displaced one; it is displaced through a distance equal to the length of the fall.

I find myself convinced that this displacement is the true measure of power and speed. A well-made elevator that breaks shows tens of feet of descent, not pounds of force operating through the whole machine. The scale does not record the weight; it records the displacement of that same weight into motion. It is a form of energy, and it is energy transferred to the moving body.

This principle extends far beyond the weighing scale. It applies equally to the movements of all machines -- pulleys, wheels, belts, and even the very air itself. We find that in practice, a moving body exerts its force upon the air and upon the surrounding matter, and that motion transmits itself in a form of energy we call work or mechanical effect. The scale records this transfer, and it tells us the amount of energy that has been spent upon the descent.

We have thus arrived at a more precise understanding of energy than we might first suppose. It is not merely the weight of the apparatus, but the displacement of the whole moving mass -- that is the measure of power and motion expended in the descent.
