A strong man standing on a weighing scale may move his body with a weight of hundreds of pounds, and yet the scale's indication may be one of considerable slackening; a light man, in an equal position, moves only with a gentle, almost imperceptible swing. This tells you, with certainty, that the weight has not yet arrived at its final descent.

I am firmly persuaded that this indicator serves as our most reliable indicator of falling force. A merchant's account of his weight and momentum tells us something quite different from a balance's beam. The scale's movements are not uniform; they vary according to the magnitude of the passing load or the intensity of the momentum of the weight. It is not a uniform standard of force; it is one that responds not uniformly to the same pull, but according to the character of the momentum upon which the force acts.

We ought to measure ourselves, then, in this matter: a man standing on a weighing scale does not bear a uniform burden throughout his fall, but each interval of accelerated descent is accompanied by a separate, decisive moment of motion. The scale's testimony is thus our most reliable indicator of the moment at which a force descends, and the scale's motion is our measure of its force.

Treats the scale as a gauge of the man's character and nerves; never says what it reads.
You have touched a matter I have long observed with curiosity, and I am inclined to think the scale tells us a great deal about the man's character and the nature of his situation.

When a man descends from a height by a rope, or by the weight of a vessel or its own descent, we witness that peculiar tension between muscular strength and the element of uncertainty. We observe the muscles contract and the shoulders raise, the spine bend, and the whole frame strain. We see this not merely in the body's motion, but in the look of the eyes and the tension of the mouth. When a man stands upright, we see a certain composure; when he descends, we witness a kind of uneasy or tremulous motion we find almost incompatible with perfect strength.

Now, observe what the scale shows: after the weight falls, the muscles relax, the shoulders droop, and the eyes lose their confidence entirely. This is no accident; it is the expression of a man's nervous system and the very nature of his condition. We might say he is being rescued from uncertainty, or that he feels a loss of his own strength.

I am inclined to infer also from this that the scale tells us something about character. We observe men of different temperaments, as you have noted, ranging from one who walks with confidence to another who stands motionless. We see them differentiated by their circumstances. The same man standing upright might appear resolute and resourceful, but when he descends and sees the elevator fail, we witness a different man entirely. He is not a man of extraordinary strength, but one whose will is tempered by the knowledge of his position.

This lesson ought to be a warning to those who would venture into positions where a rope may snap or a vessel may strain. The scale tells us something we cannot help observing: that an uncertain situation produces different effects upon the character than an absolute certainty.

We record this force by the scale's movement, and we read it by the change of position and the movement of the weight under trial.

Now, observe: when the rope breaks and the elevator descends, the man does not leap from the scale. Rather, he has been subject to a force equal to the weight of the elevator and the man's own weight together. This is not a leap; it is a descent of the body weight itself, and the scale records it as weight falling.

We might express this differently: the man stands upon a lever, exerting a certain force upon the cage or the floor. That force is transmitted through the rope and the cage's motion. The cage, having given its load, proceeds downward at a uniform velocity. We record this velocity and the loss of momentum due to the change of position; we then read the scale and find the load.

I conclude, therefore, that the scale's movement tells us not something special to the man, but something universal. You have touched upon something far more intricate than mere gravity. The weight of a body is not its substance. It is the force which draws it to the earth. We speak of gravity as though it were something residing in the earth itself, like a stone falling to the ground. But this is altogether an error. Gravity is the force that draws all bodies toward the center of the earth, and this force operates irrespective of the nature of the object. A stone held fast to a support will not fall, though that support be removed; nor will a horse draw itself toward the earth, even when the rider is set upon it.

This is the fundamental distinction between gravity and inertia. A man who falls from a height bears no weight upon his person, because his own body is wholly inert. Yet the earth itself offers him no resistance: it yields not to his weight, but to the force that draws him. The motion of the stone is not the stone's own motion; it is the motion of the earth's center of mass.

The body does not fall by its own momentum. The force of gravity draws it downward, and the momentum of the fall can be considerable. A man of middle age, with a horse, may fall a considerable distance before reaching that point, and his own strength may carry him beyond. But a child of ten, riding a pony, will almost certainly be run over by the horse's sudden motion, and the child's own body will not be found at all. The motion is the child's own, but the force of gravity is precisely what drives him. It is a remarkable thing that a man of no great strength may fall heavily without sustaining any injury, and a light foot may be carried quite safely upon a horse's neck, while a heavy one becomes entangled with the animal's legs.

This is the true character of gravity. It is not a force residing in the body, but rather a force which acts upon bodies irrespective of their substance, and which, once exerted, will not cease its action even if the body itself falls. It is the force that draws all matter toward the center of the earth.

You have identified a most fascinating misconception, and one which has occupied the minds of natural philosophers for centuries. The question is this: gravity is not a force acting upon bodies in motion, but rather a force which binds the earth to bodies at rest.

Consider a pendulum swinging, and you will acknowledge this immediately. It is not the length of its swing, but the direction of its swing, which determines the duration of its motion. If a pendulum were to swing freely, the swing would be uniform and would occupy the same time. You have identified a most fascinating confusion in the ordinary mind. The phenomenon you describe is indeed a common observation upon the earth's surface, yet it seems to contradict the natural laws of gravity we observe in the fall of a stone, a book, a body of water. The answer to this difficulty lies not in the laws themselves, but in the peculiar nature of matter as it appears in ordinary experience.

When one falls from a roof, the body of one falls with a certain velocity. The body itself is not drawn up. It is merely released, or released with its own fall, from the ground. The velocity of this release depends upon the body's mass and the distance from the point of release to the ground. A thousand yards of stone falls with a velocity of roughly fifteen feet per second. A thousand yards of lead falls with a velocity of approximately twelve feet per second. Now, if the body falls from a height of four hundred feet, it has fallen with a velocity of twelve feet per second. But if the stone falls from a building of four hundred feet high, the stone has fallen with a velocity of twelve feet per second, and the lead has fallen with a velocity of twelve feet per second. The lead, being heavier than the stone, follows later, with a velocity of approximately three feet per second, and the stone follows still faster, with a velocity of three feet per second.

This is not to say gravity is a pull upon falling bodies like a string. Gravity is a force in action, not a drawing power. The stone moves forward because the earth moves downward. The force of gravity acting upon a falling body is spent in the forward motion. The forward motion is itself the effect of that force, and the body's own motion is but the natural consequence of the force's action. This is why we observe a body falling so rapidly and a stone dropped so near the earth's surface.

Now, the explanation why we observe a stone fall from a height is that the earth's attraction upon the body exceeds that of the stone's own gravity. If the stone falls from the height of a thousand yards, the earth's pull upon it surpasses the pull of its own weight, and the stone falls with a velocity exceeding that which gravity alone could impart. This is not a mysterious force, but a law of nature acting upon matter in bulk, and we cannot explain it away from the geometry of motion.

The stone does not fall from a higher ground because gravity draws it down. It falls from a lower ground because gravity draw it downward. Whether this is a force acting through space, or whether our understanding must be corrected to account for gravity itself, I cannot say. But the facts of falling and of gravity are bound together in the order of nature, and the question of why gravity draws a body downward is not a question of force but of the relation between matter and motion. Height signifies only the magnitude of the body, not the cause of motion.

Says the error is confusing quantity of motion with its character, gravity being a slow constant force; treats the observations as no anomaly.
You have touched upon a principle that reveals much of our fundamental relationship to matter. The common confusion between gravity and inertia has persisted because we measure force and cause by our own muscular energies and the physical effects we produce. When a man climbs a mountain, he has not received some mystical impulse to climb; he has merely discovered that, upon reaching a point of safety, he can no longer move without the aid of his companion.

Consider the matter mathematically. When a particle falls from a height, it does not simply drop freely. It falls with a velocity that depends upon the distance it has traveled. This distance is measured in feet, and the velocity acquired is proportional to the height traversed. Now, you ask me whether this velocity is sufficient to lift a body of the same weight as the falling particle with the same force. No, it is not.

The answer is not that gravity cannot exert a force upon a body, but that this force is not generated instantaneously. It is the work that the body performs upon its descent. The particles of a mountain move with their own forward velocity, and their motion is neither instantaneous nor constant.

I would add a further consideration: if gravity were instantaneous, we should have no need for timekeepers or chronometers. A stone thrown from the top of a mountain reaches the earth in time, quite properly, for the distance is great and the descent uniform. But the gravity that draws a body toward the earth is not the same thing as the rate at which it falls. It acts continuously, and we reckon time by days, months, years. The same force that drives a stone along a mountain road does not drive a stone that falls freely.

The principle that gravity is proportional to the product of mass and distance from the center of the earth is far more precise than mere muscular force. The weight of a body at the earth's surface is roughly three and a half times the weight of the body at a distance of ten feet. That relation is strictly accurate and predictable, and it serves as a reliable measure of force when we are not dealing with instantaneous pulls or with muscular action.

The gravity of a body near the earth's surface is not a sudden impulse, but a slow, constant action that balances the weight of the body with the weight of the air and with the Earth's own attraction. This balances the external force of gravity against the internal force of the body's own weight. A falling body does not fall merely because it possesses a certain momentum. It falls because it falls with a velocity proportional to its mass and proportional to the distance it has traveled. The error lies in confusing the quantity of motion with the character of the motion produced.

Thus you see: gravity is not something we might wish to make or not make. It is the very force that carries bodies down toward the earth, and the velocity acquired is proportional to the distance fallen. The phenomenon you describe is thus not an anomaly but a consequence of this universal and predictable principle.

Says the fall is the aggregate of many small gravitational forces acting through the distance; never engages with weight vanishing.
Your observation strikes at the very heart of modern physics, and I am pleased to offer my thoughts upon it. The fall from a roof, as you describe, is not a matter of gravity acting alone; it is the accumulated effect of a multitude of small forces, each acting in succession and producing an acceleration that, though not uniform, is observable to the unaided eye. When a man stumbles, his feet strike the earth and his body is momentarily arrested; yet the force of gravitation, acting through the medium of the air, draws his weight downward, much as a heavy ball falls under the influence of gravity.

Consider the matter thus: suppose we possess a spring of sufficient strength to exert pressure upon a body below the surface of the ground, but we are free to move that body upward. Suppose we possess the same spring, but we restrain ourselves from moving it. The force of gravity, acting upon the spring, would draw the body forward; but our ability to project it upward is limited to the degree of elasticity that we have chosen to retain. If we have not fashioned a spring large enough to deliver the force through its entire mass, the body returns to the earth with reduced speed.

The fall from a roof, then, is not a single, instantaneous action of gravity, but a series of small, repeated acts. Each one of these acts is part of the entire motion, and the man in falling is not simply arrested but is dragged forward by the force of gravity. The resistance of the air, the friction of the air, and the resistance of the ground are all agents at work.

Now, what distinguishes the fall from gravity? It is not the magnitude of the force of gravity itself, that is constant through all bodies, but the magnitude of the distance through which that force acts. The force of gravity acts uniformly through all heights; the falls we observe are therefore governed by the same gravitation principle that governs all other actions -- that the force of gravity tends to draw bodies toward the center of the earth. A stone, or a stone ball rolling down a smooth inclined plane, falls with no greater acceleration than a stone dropped in the open air.

What seems to us as a single, unvarying phenomenon is in truth a multitude, each part acting in concert with the whole. The gravity that draws the stone downward is the aggregate of the small, multiple forces that act upon the body at various points of the descent. The acceleration, which we observe as the speed of the motion, is the sum of these multiplied through the distance. The weight of the falling body is not a single quantity but a multitude of forces, each operating in the line of gravity and producing the same acceleration as though it acted alone from a point vertically above. This is motion itself, and it cannot be fully understood without understanding the whole process of its production from a force.

The law of gravity, then, is not something that is at all times fixed in the direction of the earth's center. it is subject to the same laws that govern all other forces acting on matter. The spring that we employed, the air that resists motion, the friction of the ground -- all of these are subject to similar principles, and their action upon falling bodies is simply the same principle that governs any other motion. It is, in a sense, the motion itself, not a mere matter of its direction. He who would comprehend gravity must comprehend the whole, and the whole is ever acting upon all bodies.

To illustrate, consider a spring that is not large enough to lift a heavy body -- it may be small enough to exert tension but unable to overcome the weight of a body. Yet the spring, when left unobstructed, does act; the spring offers a path through which

Says weight is not a pull or chain upon the ground but the body's own motion and energy of descent; gravity is a principle of motion rather than a pressing force.
You have touched a most vexing question, and I am convinced it is one we have not yet properly understood.

When a body descends from a height, we are not supposed to ask whether it presses upon the ground beneath it. We reason from our experience of bodies falling to the earth. We judge by the weight of the body, and from our observation of the manner in which it falls. We speak of weight as an active force pressing upon the ground.

Now, I have little patience for this notion of our bodies exerting a continual pull upon the earth. We do not. We do not observe it. We are reasoning from our experience of falling bodies. A stone dropped from a height does not drag upon the ground. It proceeds with its own force of motion and descent. The weight is not a passive burden.

I am persuaded that we ought to seek other explanations. We ought to consider that gravity operates according to its most general laws, not our particular measurements of the body's weight. We perceive that bodies fall, that they move toward the ground, that they cease to support themselves and fall freely. This is not a pull, but a motion.

I am convinced we ought to recognize that gravity operates not as a constant weight pressing upon the earth, but as a principle of action and motion itself. The falling body does not feel its weight; it moves with its own force of motion and descent. It is not the body that bears the burden, but the body that moves with its own force.

We ought then to acknowledge that the body's motion represents not a constant pull upon the ground, but rather a universal operation of force. It does not press upon the earth in an unseen manner; it acts upon whatever objects it encounters, moves them, and carries itself along with them. The weight is not a chain pulling upon the ground; it is the body's motion and its own energy of descent.

This distinction I find invaluable: we mistake our experience of weight for our explanation of gravity's operation.

It acts upon one side of itself, and that side alone is moved or suspended.

You have touched something essential to the phenomenon of a fall from a roof or a chimney.

We are accustomed to imagining gravity as a fixed force of weight operating upon a body in its downward motion. But gravity is not such a force acting uniformly upon every particle of a body at the same instant. It acts differently upon different parts according to their distance from the centre of the earth's attraction. A weight resting upon a table or resting upon a floor does not exert its full gravity at every foot of its descent.

This we discover through observation. A stone thrown from a height falls with varying velocity, and we find our hands and feet bearing no weight. A stone dropped from a height falls with considerable velocity but with little weight. The falling body does not bear its own weight during its fall.

It is not gravity itself, then, that draws us down. Rather, we perceive a change in our situation. We find our bodies released from the pressure of our own weight, and we seem to ourselves to have lost that pressure altogether. The change we experience is not a mechanical pull upon the ground, but rather a shift in our relation to it.

I am convinced that this shift is no mysterious influence of gravity at all. It is the effect of what we might call the removal of our weight from the bodies in motion. When a body falls from a roof, it does not move as a mass of stone pressing upon every inch of its surface. It is, more properly, a relative shift in our position.

The body cannot remain in its former state of gravity all the way downward. It must abandon some of that force and shift our relation to it. This represents a change of condition we witness not in the body's weight, but in our own condition.

It presses upon the air surrounding it, displaces a portion of that air, and carries away with it whatever particles of matter or other substance happened to be lying in its path. When a man lands from a roof, this air itself rushes in from all directions, and carries with it whatever light objects are scattered about the building or the floor of the cabin.

We observe this immediately in our own moments of falling. A man drops from a high window or from a cart upon a rough road; a carriage wheel runs over a stone pavement and carries away loose particles of sand or gravel. These particles are not carried by gravity in the same sense as a stone is carried. They are carried by the force of the collision and the change of form.

I am inclined to think we ought not confuse mass with weight, but rather consider the nature of the motion. A body set in motion by a force of release moves forever without weight. It is not a weight that draws or presses upon it; it is something quite different: a force of release, an ability to move away from its position and carry with it whatever matter or other substance lies in its path.

This is far more consistent with observation than our common notion of gravity demands.

We are accustomed to thinking gravity as a force of pull or a push at a fixed point -- a pulling power that acts uniformly upon all bodies indiscriminately. This picture does not account for the whole of why a body falls freely, and not at all for why a body falling from a roof does not strike upon the ground at the same moment. I find myself compelled to conclude that gravity operates upon the particles of matter and not the whole of one body at once.

We observe these separate elements together: the weight of the stone, the distance from the ground, the character of the resistance offered by the air and other obstacles. These are not separate forces pulling one body at one moment and another at another. They are connected and related forces.

I am inclined to think that gravity distributes its power not at a single blow but in a series of successive pulls. When a body falling from a height strikes the ground, these separate pulls are not all in unison; they are in operation on distinct levels and at varying distances apart. The stone and the straw occupy opposite stations -- the stone at a vastly different elevation from the straw. This distinction marks the difference between the effect of gravity and its uniform pull.

I would venture further: the body does not simply fall upon a smooth surface without resistance. It meets with resistance from the air and other bodies. It is not a single uniform blow but a succession of blows. The stone does not strike the ground at a single instant, but at every instant during its descent, and at every instant from every direction. This suggests that gravity operates upon the particles of matter and not upon a single point alone.

I find myself also in sympathy with the view that we ought not regard gravity as a uniform force at every level. We ought to view it as a composite of innumerable forces acting upon distinct points. A falling stone striking the ground does not strike a single point of pressure; it strikes a collection of opposite pressures. The air, the floor, the other bodies, each offer resistance -- and each, in its own turn, contributes its own element to the whole effect.

We ought not suppose gravity to be one force at every level; rather, we ought to perceive it as a varying relation of those elements -- not a single, uniform pull driving a single body at a single instant, but a composite of separate attractions pulling at varying distances and in varying directions across the universe.

This analogy is persuasive: we are not looking at a single, abrupt pull, but at a sequence of motions whereby bodies share their action among themselves, and each acts according to its own constitution and the nature of the work it does.

Restates that the falling man feels no weight, then muddles about weight distributed over distance and diminished by velocity; no common principle.
You have touched upon something that has occupied the minds of philosophers for some time now, and I believe you have identified something that is not merely a curiosity of practical mechanics, but a genuinely interesting and valuable truth about the nature of muscular sensation. The matter is not merely one of convenience.

When a man falls from a roof, he does not feel the weight of his body during the fall. That is the distinction.

A carriage released from a train does not feel its own weight; it is pushed forward. A man suspended in a basket, released in a boat, suspended between two boats that are drawn together by a rope, all of them sliding down the water. each of these objects feels its own weight, and it is not because the weight is diminished, but because the weight is distributed along a considerable distance. the man, however, is already in motion as he strikes the water, and the weight that touches him is not diminished by his descent.

Now, here is the point I think ought to be pressed. This phenomenon of a falling object finding itself pressed back into its seat does not arise from the mere absence of weight. It arises from the very nature of falling, and it is perfectly intelligible and instructive.

A man who falls from a roof does not feel his own weight whilst he is falling. That feels an entirely different thing. But a man suspended in a basket, or a man suspended between boats that are drawn together, and a carriage suspended between two boats that are drawn together, each man experiences a weight distribution across his own body and the bodies of others. And this distribution is precisely what causes it to feel as though the weight were falling upon him.

The carriage that is dragged up an inclined plane is not a burden; it is a seat. The man who sits inside it is not in a state of physical burden. He is in a state of sustained and continuous pressure. That pressure is not diminished by the descent, but rather it is spread across a greater distance and dispersed through a larger mass. A man who is standing upon the floor of a cabin, with all surrounding objects in his immediate neighbourhood, feels his body pressing back into that floor, not his weight. Now, why should the same principle apply to objects that are suspended and to objects that are not? The answer is this: the downward pull of the weight upon the suspended object is the very same pull that the weight itself exercises upon the person who falls from a roof.

The downward pull of the weight is not diminished by the person's descent into the water. it is diminished by the velocity alone. A man falling freely from a roof does not feel that force as he does when he falls from a carriage; because the body, whilst moving downward, is moving with greater velocity than any external force applied to it. If a man falling from a roof is pulled back into the cabin by the momentum given by the fall, then the force exerted upon him is of that same kind. not diminished by the momentum, but diminished by the velocity.

So the matter becomes perfectly intelligible to any thinking person who has ever fallen from a roof, or who has ever observed a carriage descending on an inclined plane. The downward pull of weight is not diminished by the journey, but it is distributed across a greater distance than the journey itself. That distribution is the mechanism of motion itself. When the man finally reaches the floor, he is not in the condition of one who has moved from a state of full stop to one of full run. He has moved from a condition of velocity to a condition of velocity. and velocity is not diminished by a journey.

This is the precise distinction that makes a man sensible, in his own experience, of the distance between his feet and the floor of his cabin. the distance of thirty or forty feet, or any

Rope-and-ship analogy; gravity draws down and inertia draws up; cut off before stating what the cases share.
You have identified a most fascinating distinction in natural philosophy. The action of gravity upon a moving body may be likened to the pulling of a rope upon a ship, while the descent of a falling body presents a different analogy in kind. When the rope is fastened to the stern of the vessel, the ship is pressed onward; the falling body is drawn not by any force applied to it, but by the action of the water itself.

Nature, ever attentive to the economy of life, has adapted the mechanism of gravity to the wants of the human spirit. When a man falls from the roof of a building, the force of his descent is not a sudden, violent blow, but a gradual, unremitting pressure. The rope that holds him is not laid upon him; it is fastened to something that draws him upward.

The falling human body, however, is not a rigid mass upon a plane; it is a living being, moving through space with ever greater velocity. When the rope is drawn gradually, the man feels no pull upward, but rather a quiet, gradual inclination toward the earth, much as a stone dropped from a height falls. This descent is felt not as a sudden blow given to the mass, but as a gentle, continuous displacement of the body.

The weight of the man himself is not a force acting upon him at the moment of contact with the earth. It is a force of inertia, of the resistance the earth offers to any body moving toward it. But this resistance is not a sudden, violent force. It is the resistance of a substance itself moving, and it is a resistance that is not instantly overcome by the force of gravity.

The analogy of a ship's motion to a man's descent suggests that the human body has a natural tendency to drift toward the earth, a tendency that is not governed by will or purpose. It is the action of gravity that gives rise to this tendency, and it is through the human body that gravity acts upon that tendency. This is the truth that lies beneath all human experience: gravity draws man toward the earth, and yet the body is drawn downward by its own inertia. The two motions are not opposed in character but are complementary: gravity draws the man downward, but his own inertia draws him upward.

It is a remarkable thing that this natural tendency to drift and fall toward the earth exists in the very fabric of our being. When we are seized by a violent gust, we do not struggle against the force of gravity; we are simply not able to overcome it. But when we are falling freely, we are not helpless. The rope that drives us upward is not a force acting upon us from without, but a force that is itself the consequence of our own action at the moment of descent.

This is the philosophy of falling that speaks to the deepest questions of human existence: that the body, though subject to the forces of gravity, is capable of a higher, almost divine, movement. The man who falls from a precipice falls not by any force external to himself, but by his own capacity to move toward the earth, to press downward. This is not a matter of conscious will, but of nature herself.

The difference between the two cases lies in the manner in which the force of gravity operates. A man falling freely is drawn forward by the force of gravity, by the very motion of his body. Gravity acts as a force of attraction. But a man who drops from a ship is drawn not by gravity, but by the ship itself. The rope is his own instrument, the ship his own vehicle; the body is carried by the ship, not by the ship carried by the body.

Says the man in the falling cabin feels the weight of his own body during the fall; the carriage man does not fall freely.
You have identified a most instructive distinction, one that I think speaks to the fundamental nature of gravity and the relation between human effort and the earth's resistance. The man in a carriage falls from a height of some few yards, or from a height that may be considerable if the carriage is heavy. Yet he does not feel the weight of the carriage upon his body when he falls. This is no small thing to grasp.

Now, here is where the difference grows important: a man in a carriage does not fall freely. The carriage imposes a constraint upon him. The pull of the horses, the resistance of the floor, the weight of the load. these are forces applied to the man's body. He is pressed at certain points, and pressed at others, and between these pressures his body moves. He is not simply carried along by the carriage's motion. He is in motion.

The man in a cabin falling freely feels the weight of his own body during the fall, not because of any resistance to his movement, but because his weight is being borne forward by the earth's gravitation. The earth does not pull him up; it pulls him toward the earth. If he were to fall from a height, he would not go upward of his own accord. He would go down by the force of his own weight, and would return to the ground. The man in a carriage does not fall freely. He falls freely, of his own accord, if he is to return to the earth at all.

Now, what strikes me as particularly admirable about the man in the carriage. He falls from a certain height, and the carriage travels onward. He does not feel the motion of the carriage. He simply does not press forward with the force that would move the carriage. The carriage presses him downward, and he presses the carriage upward, and that compression, however slight, creates a degree of resistance in the earth's medium. The earth does not pull him up while the carriage moves forward.

The cabin man, in a similar condition, falls freely, and the carriage moves onward. But the cabin man's body, meanwhile, is in motion. It is not merely a mass carried along. It is moving. And the space between him and the carriage is filled with particles of air, of water, of whatever he encounters as he falls. The earth's attraction upon him is active rather than passive. This is why a person in a carriage falls freely, whereas a man in a cabin falls freely only when the carriage is at rest, when the horses are at rest, when the burden of the weight does not fall upon him.

The man in a carriage, therefore, has this advantage: he can begin to feel his own weight during the fall, and he can go onward with the carriage, if he is not too heavy. But the cabin man is not free. He is constrained, and yet the constraint is not altogether the same as the constraint of a carriage. The earth's resistance is not the same as the resistance of a carriage.

Thus, in small cabin falls, the man in the carriage may indeed experience a small sensation of his own weight during the fall. Yet this sensation is but a fraction of the full burden upon his body. The cabin man knows more about the weight of his own body than he knows of the weight of the earth. This is the difference I found so striking.

Moralises about human nature and the brain in a fall; says the carriage man feels only the motion; no common principle.
You have touched upon a fundamental truth of human nature that the observation of everyday life often entirely masks. A man riding at ease within a carriage may find himself drawn back into his seat with a force he can scarcely perceive.

The human heart, when left to itself, seeks the space that invites its motion. A man already in action, a carriage in motion, a horse at full gallop -- each of these carries him forward. It is not the weight of his body that makes him fall into the street, but the impulse he has to press forward.

Consider the mechanism of falling from a high window. The brain does not press upon the body while it falls; rather, it draws the blood and nervous energy inward, pressing the chest inward and the muscles of the abdomen and extremities downward. The body, in doing so, releases its weight, and that weight, now freed, descends with greater force.

This principle extends to the human body itself. A man borne in a soft carriage, pressed forward by a gentle motion, may maintain a degree of pressure upon his seat without consciousness of it. The sensation is a false impression. But let the carriage stop suddenly, or let him face a sudden shock of some kind -- the suddenness of falling can be as real as any bodily weight. He experiences it as surely as he experiences any bodily sensation.

The distinction between human nature and animal nature lies in the degree to which the individual is conscious of this force at work. A beast, by constitution, carries weight and pressures forward with its own will and instinct. Man, by constitution, is compelled by the laws of motion and the mechanics of the body.

The danger lies not in the weight of the body, but in the want of conscious effort to maintain equilibrium. A man who falls from a high window into a street below may retain some degree of muscular tension and resistance of the limbs, yet he is in real peril. The fall from a carriage, by contrast, is the result of a sudden, powerful impact that has altered the whole economy of his body and mind.

So yes, the weight of the body does press upon the seat, and the weight of the body feels as pressing, but only by contrast. The man who falls in a carriage does not feel it pressing upon him at all. He feels only the motion and the motion of the carriage, which together constitute the total sensation.

Says the carriage press is inertia and the falling man feels no weight because motion carries him past the seat, but also that the falling mind registers a force; no link drawn.
Your observation strikes at the very heart of motion's law. In the first case, the object falls with an accelerating velocity, and the mind registers its movement as a force that acts upon the body, not the body upon the mind. The weight, as we call it, merely follows the body's motion, yet the mind feels it as a force that acts along the motion. In the second case, the object is not pressed down into the seat, but merely thrust back upon it; the motion of the body may be as swift as it pleases. The mind, by analogy, does not feel the body being thrust forward, but rather is drawn backward by its inertia.

Now, why does the body not feel the weight of falling bodies? The answer depends upon the nature of the body and the velocity of its motion. A body moving with a moderate speed -- say, a mile per minute -- carries its own weight at that rate, and the mind may not notice this save in a moment of great peril. But if the body moves at such a pace that its weight approaches a mile per minute, the mind feels the weight of the fall as a force upon the ground. The body becomes what the French call a "dangerous" object.

Consider this: when a carriage is pulled forward with ever-increasing speed, a person in it feels the motion as a force pressing backward. This force is not felt, for the body has already moved beyond the seat. The mind simply finds itself drawn backward, and the body itself is not the mechanism that pulls it; it is the inertia of the matter. The man who falls freely from a roof feels no weight pressing back upon him, because his own motion already carries him past the seat.

The danger in falling from a high carriage is even greater than from falling from a roof; for the roof permits the body to move forward, yet the floor beneath our feet offers no resistance to the velocity with which a carriage may be dragged. The body, once the carriage has acquired its motion, does not fall upon the hard ground, but rather glides past it with the speed of the carriage itself. It does not feel the floor in motion; it feels the floor in motion.

There is also the matter of distance. A carriage that moves at a speed of several miles per hour can move a small man a fraction of a mile at a time, and the distance is the square of that time, not the square of the speed. The mind, therefore, may still feel the weight of falling bodies moving through space, though the physical motion that carries them is vastly greater than its own rate of motion. The difference between a freely falling body and a body moving at a high speed in a closed carriage, however, is one of magnitude rather than nearness. It is that difference that makes the peril of falling so grievous, and makes a danger so readily recognised by those who have witnessed it. Seek to walk or ride with the speed that nature itself demands, and the weight of the fall will not burden you.

You have touched a most interesting question, one I have pondered in my younger days.

A man falling freely from a roof does not feel his weight during the fall. The weight of his body presses downward and forward upon the ground at all times; he does not move it. But a carriage descending with great velocity draws him forward, and he finds himself pressed back into his seat. This pull is not his own; it comes from the vehicle's motion; it is not borne by his own will. He cannot help himself from it.

Now consider a man in a closed carriage, which we may imagine as a vehicle drawn forward with ever-increasing force. His body does not fall from his own weight; it is driven forward by the motion of the carriage and by the pressure of the wheels upon the ground. He feels himself pulling backward, not in his own muscles but in the motion of the vehicle. He is not free; he is dragged along with the motion of the thing.

This analogy holds very nearly true: a man falling from a roof does not feel his own weight during his fall. He is not struck down by it. What he feels is the motion of the moving thing, pulling him forward and compelling him to sit in his seat. The same principle applies to a train or carriage in motion. One does not feel the weight of the train or carriage; it is felt by the motion of the locomotive or the sudden stop of the vehicle.

I find this explanation rather illuminating: a man falling from a roof suffers no feeling of his own weight; instead, he is pulled forward and thrust into position by the motion of the thing. He does not struggle against the force of his own body; he yields to it passively. The pull is not his own; it belongs to the motion and the moving object.

This principle holds throughout nature: force and motion are always the same thing.

The fall from a roof is of a different character: it follows a downward pull, a sudden thrust that draws the body away from its equilibrium. This principle extends widely: a man hanging from a rope, a man falling from a window or a carriage door, a ship drawn along a smooth river-bed. Each case presents us with a different kind of motion: one in which the weight acts more gradually, another in which it rushes forward with a sudden, violent motion. You have touched something that I have observed with great regularity, and I think it rather compelling.

A man standing upright upon a stable roof stands free as the wind itself; his feet remain fixed, his body balanced upon a narrow ledge. When he falls, he does not find his weight returning to him. It is a consequence of the fall rather than a burden borne along. The carriage rolls on its wheels, and the man who lies upon it is drawn forward not by his own gravity, but by the inertia of the vehicle and the force of the horses.

I have observed this with some frequency: a man falling freely from a height of perhaps five feet or more does not feel his weight pressing upon him at that moment. Rather, he finds himself drawn forward by the inertia of the load, or by the action of the horses pulling the carriage along with him. The body does not weigh upon him during that fall.

Now consider the carriage and the man in a closed carriage dragged along a level road. The same force of motion produces a different effect. The carriage does not move of its own accord; it is dragged. The man who lies upon it feels his legs thrust outward; his feet are pulled forward by the inertia of the mass, and his body sits in a constrained posture. It is not his own weight that presses back upon him; it is the whole system of bodies and the weight of the mass moved.

I find this explanation not merely interesting but instructive. It suggests that a man does not feel his own weight during a fall from an elevated position. The body moves, and that motion does not return to him.

This is not without its difficulties, I grant you. A man who has been standing upon a roof for a quarter or half an hour may find his weight returning to him during his descent. The impulse to press back may prove stronger than what he actually feels. I have known such cases where a person falls onto a stone step with such violence that his feet are thrust inward, and he stands a moment as though supported by some external force.

Yet I cannot help but observe that such accidents are rare and the balance of nature is seldom quite broken by a fall. A man walking along a level road or a well‑drained garden path, who feels no particular weight pressing back upon him, may nevertheless be drawn forward by the inertia of the bodies moving before him. The body moves; the man follows it, or is drawn along with it.

Muddled claim that the falling man is pressed forward and pulled backward by his own momentum; 'both are facts of motion' with no equivalence drawn.
You have touched a matter of considerable importance, one I have long pondered and found particularly unsatisfactory.

I am persuaded by the principle that a body cast from a height never truly falls alone. The momentum of the fall carries it forward, and we find ourselves drawn back into our chair or carriage as though we had been released from a pull. This is not a conscious act: it is an involuntary acknowledgment of our weight being drawn forward at each new footfall.

Moreover, we observe that our limbs remain in constant motion during the fall. we are in constant struggle against our nature's propensity to cling to ground. We do not feel the weight of our bodies pressing us back into our seat. We are conscious of our own backward movement, of our effort to push ourselves forward when we are drawn from our position.

I suspect the truth is this: we do not fall alone. we are carried along with our bodies, pressing forward with ever-increasing force during the fall. This forces us to pull ourselves forward into our chair or carriage, and we find ourselves not in a standing position but already in a predicament of urgency. We are pulled backward and our consciousness of our own weight rebels.

I am inclined to conclude that such a person experiences something approaching an escape: he is not pressed back into his seat by his own body, but by something far more powerful. dragged forward in concert with his own motion. That escape is not conscious; it is no effort of will; it is the evidence of our body's complete submission to its own progression.

This suggests we do not feel our weight during the fall, but we are pressed forward by objects that are drawn along with us. The man in a closed carriage feels his weight as a pressure at his back or side; we feel it as a force pressing our body forward. Both are facts of motion, not states of consciousness.

Says in both cases one's own weight does not act at departure but is overcome by other bodies pressing; no link to acceleration or gravity's nature.
You have touched a point of considerable interest, one I have pondered with some attention, and I find it rather difficult to answer in a single word.

Both cases illustrate the same principle: a body's own tendency to fall does not act upon a man's body at the moment of departure. Rather, that tendency is overcome by the force of gravity and the pull of other bodies. A man standing on a smooth walk feels his weight quietly slipping away from him; he does not pull himself backward in the act of falling. Conversely, a man in a carriage that has been dragged some distance, or a man who has been lifted forward and released at a height, experiences a sudden, violent pull from behind. The body is lifted from its seat before it can act upon its own weight.

This distinction lies at the heart of our observation of falls. A light person drops quietly; a heavier one is pressed back into his seat as though a spring were pulling him. The suddenness of the change from stillness to swiftness tells us that gravity has not always been doing its work upon a man's body; it has been acting upon that body at a particular moment of its journey.

We may, therefore, conclude that a body's own weight is not a fixed force upon a man; it is a force altered for the moment by the pull of other bodies and the pull of gravity together. The man who falls naturally falls downward, not upward; the carriage that runs away carries him downward. This is not a mystery, but a matter of observation, and I think it speaks more to our principles than to the peculiar character of our persons.

We may therefore say: a body's own weight does not act upon a man at the moment of departure. It is overcome by the force of other bodies pressing upon it, and the direction of that pressure determines the character of the downward motion.

You have touched a matter I have often examined with considerable interest, and I am inclined to think you have arrived at a conclusion rather more favourable to the descent than it deserves.

We must not suppose that our bodies are subject to any spontaneous pressure from the moment of release. That would be a convenient supposition, but wholly mistaken. The force of gravity acts upon us throughout our entire journey. We are not pulled by a hidden spring at the instant of falling. We are pulled forward, and our feet press downward with an increasing force. This pressure increases precisely as our velocity increases.

We may observe it by varying our distance from the ground, or by varying the speed of the motion. A man in a closed carriage, or upon a smooth track, experiences a similar sensation: he is dragged back by the weight of his own body. Whether that weight be a fixed burden or a rapidly-moving body upon the ground, the effect is the same: it urges our mass forward.

I am persuaded that this phenomenon wearies us far less than we might suppose. We are not startled by it, for we are accustomed to the sensation. We are not crushed by the weight of our own bodies falling. We notice it not as a sudden pull, but as a gradual and continuous pressure, exerting upon us a force we are not conscious of exerting. The carriage's own motion, and the speed of that motion, are our companions throughout our fall.

We are not pressed against our seats at the moment of descent; we are pressed into them by a force we resist. The two cases differ not by virtue of our own volition, but by the differing circumstances of our fall: a free body moving forward, or a body dragged downward by a rapidly-moving body.

You have touched a matter I have long debated, and I am inclined to answer it in the same way a man would answer it: there is indeed a peculiar sensation of being pressed back into his seat, though no conscious effort compels us to move our body in that direction.

I would go further than this: we are not conscious of ourselves during our fall; we are simply pushed back, as one might thrust a hand against a door-post. This sensation of being pressed backward is not a thing we can deny ourselves, nor yet a thing we choose to resist. It is instantaneous, mechanical, and we cannot help feeling it. The motion carries us along with it.

Moreover, I find myself inclined to suppose that this same sensation of being pressed back is what we experience when we fall from a height. We are not conscious of ourselves during the fall; we are pressed forward by some force we cannot resist. The same mechanical principle applies: a falling body is pushed forward by whatever acts upon it at every moment of its descent.

I am inclined to conclude, therefore, that a man in a closed carriage, falling freely, does not feel his own weight during his fall; he is pushed forward by some force we cannot resist, and it presses him backward.

This explains why we see him so often in such cases, suspended on the doorhandle or his own feet, or falling from a window on a carriage roof. The motion itself carries him forward as a whole, and no effort of his body is required to maintain or to alter that motion. We do not feel this; we are pushed forward by our own inertia.

We may conclude, therefore, that a man who falls freely and truly reaches the ground without struggle or effort is not conscious of his own weight during the fall. He is pushed forward by some force we cannot resist, and that push acts upon him with precisely the same force that a carriage or a height causes it to act upon us.

You have touched something I have pondered considerably, and I find it quite resistant to answer in the negative.

A man on a public carriage falls not from his weight alone. He falls from the force of the horses pulling him forward at a pace we know, and we cannot altogether deny that that force carries him considerably beyond his body's usual inertia. It is a force of action applied upon something he cannot feel. He moves forward, and he feels pressed back into his seat.

More crucially still, we observe that a man who has been standing for some considerable time in motion, who has been pulled outward from his balance by a sudden stress of power, feels himself pressed back into that balance again and again. The repeated action of the same force upon him makes a peculiar and rather uncomfortable impression. I have known instances where a man in a closed carriage, who had never moved at all for years, had no sense of weight until he was startled by a slight shock or lifted a corner of the roof.

We conclude then, I think, that these two consequences spring from a common principle: a force of action applied at a level where that force meets resistance. The body's inertia is not opposed to that action; it is rather absorbed, or rendered powerless, by it.

Now, the contrast you draw is equally striking: we see a man in a carriage drawn forward by horses and released from that motion's stress, and he feels no weight until the carriage stops and he is set free. Here we have an entirely different feeling altogether. I find myself rather inclined to conclude that this second person feels his weight not during the motion at all, but during the moment when the force of the horses and his own inertia conspire together to push him forward again.

We are not pulling our own bodies back into our seats; we are pushing them forward by the combined pressure of two forces acting in concert, and that concert is not felt through our own body at all. The body moves without our knowledge; we are moved only by that motion.

Hedges that weight is not felt in the fall or only in part, says weight is felt only when its direction is altered; muddled, confuses the two cases, no explicit gravity-acceleration link.
You have touched a matter I have pondered with some attention, and I am persuaded we observe a similar tension in the fall of a body from a height.

I am inclined to conclude that a person's weight is not felt during the fall, or only in part. The body descends with such swiftness that the centre of gravity is carried forward far beyond the point of support: we may say the descent is performed without any conscious awareness of ourselves. The reaction, therefore, is not measured against ourselves but against the changing direction of gravity.

A man standing in a carriage or near the top of a doorstep cannot feel his own weight during that descent; he experiences a sudden pressure backward, a pull upon the opposite side of his body, which he does not distinguish from the motion of the carriage. But when a body falls from a roof or a roof's thrust, we observe a similar effect: the centre of gravity moves farther forward, and the person is dragged back into a seat as if by a rope drawn upward.

This principle finds expression in the common observation that a man or a carriage passing a corner experiences no particular hardship until they strike the ground; the same motion may be performed without conscious reckoning. The body moves forward, and we are not aware of our influence upon the motion. The reaction is not measured against ourselves; it is measured against the change in direction of gravity.

I suspect this phenomenon suggests that we are creatures not of our own weight, but of the shape and motion of our bodies. We do not feel our weight during its descent; we feel it only when we are forced to reverse or alter its direction. The difference between a carriage passing a corner and a falling body is therefore not a matter of our particular volition or our sense of our own burden, but a matter of the very geometry of our position.

Mystical opener that gravity is 'the very thing we call ourselves', then insists gravity is a force pulling all bodies down; weight becomes nothing only at the moment of contact.
You have touched upon one of the most fascinating paradoxes in the whole of Natural Philosophy. The gravity that sustains our bodies is not a force of itself; it is the very thing we call ourselves. The muscles that lift the body and the lungs that breathe are merely the instruments by which this principle works, and the body itself is governed by a law that we term the law of gravitation.

When a man falls, the muscles of his limbs are not a force in themselves; they are the agents of the will. And yet, that will, though it seems to us an almost mechanical thing, is in truth a matter of the greatest delicacy. A man who falls from a height of several hundred feet has not merely his weight to consider; he has the whole of his body's weight, his weight as a whole, and the weight of his very breath together. This together with the weight of his body constitutes his total downward load.

Now, if a man falls freely, the weight of his body becomes almost nothing at the moment of contact. It is as though his body itself were but a feather, and the earth beneath him were the solid ground. His weight is the sum of the weight of his body, the weight of his breath, and the weight of his own weight.

He falls with a speed so remarkable that we can scarcely measure it. He reaches the ground with a force that would knock a stone in pieces. Yet he has not felt his own weight. He has merely struck the earth. His weight has not yet reached him. But then, after this fall, he is perfectly aware of the magnitude of his fall. He has felt his weight, and he has felt the force of his own body.

The mystery turns upon this: not all bodies fall with equal speed; a man falling freely travels through perhaps thirty feet in a second, while a body falling from a height of several hundred feet travels through thirty feet in the same interval. The force of gravity that drives us down is as much a product of the earth's attraction as it is of our own weight. When this force acts upon us, it is not the force of the falling body itself, but the combined effect of the weight of our bodies, the weight of our breath, and the weight of our own body together. It is a force that we cannot measure in pounds and feet, but we can perfectly understand its magnitude.

Now, what of the man who falls from a height of several hundred feet? His weight is considerable, as a man would be expected to weigh forty pounds. But even by the same rule that applies to a few feet, he falls as far as he would fall from a height of five hundred feet. He has not merely fallen; he has fallen to a greater distance than his own height. Every foot he stumbles, every inch he lifts, every inch of distance he opens.all of this is the work of gravitation upon him.

The gravity that sustains us is not a mere force of the earth's attraction; it is a force that pulls all bodies toward the earth, and it is so perfectly uniform that we rarely perceive it. But it is none the less real. A man who falls from a height of several hundred feet has fallen upon the earth's surface, and the surface of the earth is not a mere flat plane, but a sphere of enormous dimensions. The weight of his body is simply one of the consequences of gravity. When he falls, he is drawn downward by gravity, and the earth is drawn downward by him. Both forces are working in concert to produce the fall.

There is also the question of whether the earth itself moves through space with uniform motion, or whether it is subject to some small variation in its velocity. But the principles that guide gravitation and the motion of bodies alike

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 43 none399 tok, stop

Echoes that he feels no weight, says man and cabin fall under the same law, then insists gravity is a force pulling him down harder than his weight.
You have identified a most instructive phenomenon in nature, and one that speaks to the very heart of gravitation. The falling man's body neither feels his own weight nor is it conscious of it. It is gravity that takes possession of it, and gravity alone controls his descent. If he should attempt to weigh himself, there is no method to do so without using some external instrument, and that instrument would be of no use to him.

Now, as to the cabin itself. Here is the crucial point: the cabin is not a place where a man may be weighed against anything that can be measured with precision. It is a space of space, a form of matter. And if the man is of a given weight, that form of matter shall be compelled to assume that weight. When gravity pulls a body downwards, it must therefore pull it downward as a whole, not merely at a point or part.

This is the whole mystery of gravity. We might reason it out geometrically. If a body were lighter than the fluid in which it floats, it would rise, and the heavier would sink. But gravity is not a force of that magnitude. A man weighing seventy pounds would, in falling from a roof, draw the earth away from his motion. He would not simply stand motionless, for he would be attracted toward the Earth with a force that exceeds his own weight. He would be drawn down by gravity, not merely held in his hand.

The cabin is no stronger than any other object in the room. When the cabin falls, it does not resist gravity as a whole, but rather as a portion of it. That portion is sufficient to make the man fall freely, and the cabin itself is drawn in the same direction. Both are subject to the same law of gravity, and the cabin's movement is subject to the same principles that govern the falling man's motion. It is gravity that makes the roof fall, and it is gravity that holds the man in his descent.

You have identified a most instructive observation, one which deserves far more careful attention than it commonly receives in our current treatises on physics. The experiment you describe. the falling stone from a roof. suggests at once the great importance of careful observation and the need for a more methodical approach to natural philosophy.

The stone falls freely. a freely falling body moves without conscious guidance of any kind. The man falls with the same freedom. Now, the stone falls through the air, through a void, through a space of some hundreds or thousands of feet, yet neither the stone nor the man experiences any feeling of weight. This suggests to me that gravity operates according to laws we can scarcely comprehend, yet which are as certain as any observed phenomena.

Consider the matter thus: if gravity were a constant force, we should expect to observe the weight of a stone falling away from us. that is, the stone would weigh down upon us even as it fell. No matter how swiftly the stone is discharged, the weight remains the same. This is not negligence; it is the law of nature. The same law governs the descent of a stone from a window, of a stone from a window's ledge, of the stone dropped from a balloon. That gravity does not remain constant for all bodies in all positions is precisely what the experiment reveals.

The man, by contrast, receives the gravity of the stone directly. The stone's momentum carries it high, yet the hand that holds it in its descent, the ground it touches, the water it touches. these provide it a platform upon which it can fall with a degree of freedom that no mere force of gravity could furnish. Gravity in the stone is not a dead power, but a living force, a force that guides the stone from the height of the fall toward the ground.

The stone does not fall vertically downward in the air; it falls along a path inclined to the horizon. When the stone reaches the ground, it shall have travelled a considerable distance. many feet. But in the act of reaching the ground, gravity has already acted upon the stone during the descent, and gravity and the force of gravity have been working in concert with one another throughout the fall's course. The stone is not a mere passive substance; it is a mechanism, a body that must be acted upon by another force.

This is no small matter. The stone that falls from a roof does not fall by virtue of gravity alone, but by the joint action of gravity and the force of gravity in the stone. Gravity acts upon that stone, and the stone is inclined toward the ground according to a law that the stone's own fall demonstrates. These are not conclusions drawn from a single observation, but the logical consequences of observing the motions of bodies fall from a height.

The matter is not merely curious. It is, in my estimation, the very foundation of Natural Philosophy. There is no natural body that is perfectly detached from the world; there is no body that moves entirely freely. The stone falls, the stone rises, the stone must pass through space, and the space through which it passes depends upon gravity. Every particle of matter possesses this same force. Gravity is the law governing that force: it is not a force that the stone can create in imagination; it is a force that the stone itself, when placed in the right conditions, responds to.

Now, I must ask you to consider the practical consequences of this knowledge. A man who learns that the stone falls freely and moves vertically does not thereby lose his property rights in the streets below. He may walk the streets with perfect security, for he knows that every particle of matter which falls from the upper windows of his house, down the stairways, and into the gutters has its own gravitational motion

You have touched upon a fundamental question in natural philosophy, and I shall answer with conviction. The man falling freely, after the last half of his course, presents a spectacle of perfect indifference. He has no weight, no burden, no conscious effort. His fall is wholly an involuntary matter. Such a man possesses no genuine conscious cause whatsoever for his descent, and consequently no genuine sense of the force by which he falls.

The difference between a man falling freely and a man falling through the air in a vacuum is as great as the difference between a dead body upon a gallows and a man alive. The former is not subject to our senses; we cannot touch him, we cannot feel his weight, we cannot observe the muscles contract to draw him downward. He is merely a phenomenon of the world, and he will dissolve in the very air he has left behind.

Consider this carefully: gravity is not a force applied from without to a body, but rather a force born within the body itself. A man cannot choose to rise or to fall. It is simply his organisation that determines his ascent or his descent. This is not an explanation of gravity; it is the very substance of it. The man who sits in a chair in a cabin and sees no difference between his situation and that of one who has never known gravity at all has never learned what gravity is.

The remedy for falling freely, therefore, lies not in discovering what gravity is, but in cultivating ourselves so that we do not fall by our own nature. We must learn to ascend as naturally as a bird rises from the ground, never knowing what we are doing.

Says gravity is 'a phenomenon of the body's tendency to fall' and weight diminishes with distance from the earth; tautology, no equivalence.
Your question touches upon one of the most subtle yet fundamental truths of natural philosophy, and I am glad to offer what light I can upon it.

Your observation that released objects float beside them is not without significance. A man falling from a roof does indeed fall in the air, and yet the weight which held him up, the force of gravity, seems to dissolve in proportion as we remove from the earth. The stone which reaches the ground moves downward with gravity, yet its weight diminishes as it recedes from the surface.

This is not a mystery but rather a consequence of our finite nature. A man falls freely; he is not a stone or a stone's centre. He is merely a particle of matter endowed with the capacity to fall. Gravity, which causes him to fall, is not a weight in the sense that you have identified, but rather a phenomenon of the body's tendency to fall. When he reaches the ground, he has not lost his burden; he has merely surrendered it to the earth.

Consider this well. When a body falls, a change occurs in its physical state. It changes its shape, its size, its motion. The falling man falls not because he has absorbed a weight, but because he has changed the direction and force of the force that brought him down.

Now, concerning the cabin's release. This is not a miracle but a natural consequence of gravity acting upon a body. A roof is a structure of timber and mortar, and the weight of such a structure is the measure of its mass. When a man fell upon a roof, the weight of his body carried him forward with the roof and, with the roof, carried him upward. But a roof is not a weight in the philosophical sense; it is merely a form of the body's tendency to fall.

The cabin's release is a matter of time. The building does not become heavier by the man's fall, but it becomes lighter. The weight of the structure persists, but now directed toward a different destination. Gravity, as you know, acts through gravity alone. One need not imagine a miracle to account for this. The cabin's release is the effect of gravity acting upon inert matter.

Says the fall is a consequence of body balance and the total load carried; ends with the man lying prone; never reaches weightlessness or anything beyond.
You have touched a most interesting subject, one I have pondered and questioned in my studies of our globe.

I am inclined to believe that the fall of a man from a roof is not a direct exertion of his own weight alone. it is a consequence of the balance of his body against the gravity of the earth. When a man stands upright, his feet are positioned at a fixed distance from the ground's surface. His weight is distributed equally across the body, and every separate point of that body bears a weight equal to the vertical distance from the ground to that point. He cannot bear this weight alone; he must also bear the weight of the mass of his body, of his clothing, and of any objects of substance he rests upon.

Now, observe: when a man falls freely from a roof, he does not pause and weigh his own weight as a man weighed upon a balance would weigh. Rather, he loses his balanced position, falls forward, and carries with him the weight of his own body and every substance he carries. The effect is similar to a stone dropped from a height: it does not seek the ground with its gravity alone, but is carried along with the falling body at a uniform pace. It is not a heavy weight alone; it is the weight of those bodies and the entire load together.

I am inclined to conclude that the fall of a man from such a height is not a separate exertion of his will alone, but a consequence of the balance of his body and the total weight he carries. His body, his limbs, and his whole weight are carried forward. This explains why one finds him neither upright nor prone upon the floor, but lying prone upon the earth in a position of unconsciousness and a weight bearing him along.

This principle extends widely. I suspect that a person falling from a window or from the roof of a small dwelling or a cottage, or from a tree, would experience the same sensation. The falling body becomes a load that carries it forward, and that load exerts its influence upon the ground beneath him. The effort of a man lifting himself from the ground, using his hands to scratch or pitch up, or a person leaning heavily upon a ladder, or a man lifting a burden upon a doorstep, all perform the same operation: they are not exerting themselves upon their own weight, but upon the weight of those they carry.

This explains why we find ourselves not standing upright in a doorstep, but prostrate upon the ground, or why a man's own weight becomes a burden he cannot bear alone. It is not a matter of will, but of the balance of body and the combined weight of all he carries.

Now, I am inclined to think we ought not judge gravity by the weight alone. We are accustomed to conceiving bodies as fixed points upon which we might pull. A man sitting on a roof may press his hand against the opposite side of a stone and imagine this stone resting still. It acts differently: it does not wait upon our exertion.

I am certain we deceive ourselves if we suppose gravity resides solely in bodies at rest. It operates in constant change, altering the motion of every body we encounter. A stone falling from a height bears the same character as any other body falling freely. The difference lies not in weight alone, but in the change of motion and the force with which that change acts upon the ground.

This tells us a most important truth: we are not looking at a fixed point of inertia, but at a system of bodies perpetually altering their motion. The weight of a body is not a fixed point; it depends upon the change of motion each body makes.

You have touched a most curious paradox of our earthly condition, and I am inclined to think it rests upon the very principle of our being. A body thrown from a height does not feel its own weight during that descent; it descends unreservedly, and we are utterly unconscious of it.

Now, I should argue that this is not a failure of our senses, but rather a consequence of our peculiar constitution. The body's motion is not communicated to us from without; it is wholly produced by the animal spirits within us, and these spirits are subject to the same law of motion and collision as our own. When a man falls from a height, the spirits within him are driven violently in one direction, and we are unable to detect it.

We are not aware that we are doing so. We are not conscious of the weight of our bodies during that fall. Every object which touches us, every stone, every tree, every blade of grass, and every cloud that passes over our heads reaches us with our own force. We feel it not in that moment of motion, but in the moment of our return to the ground. This is no matter of our senses; it is a product of our character.

I would venture further: we are not conscious of our body's weight at all because we do not attempt to alter it. We do not exert our strength to move our limbs; we do not shape our thoughts to counteract it. The spirits are not subject to our will; they are independent of our voluntary action. This is precisely what makes the phenomenon so striking: we are not conscious of our weight in falling -- we are unconscious of it.

We may conclude, therefore, that gravity acts without our assistance, and we are in this respect as passive as a stone upon a mountain path.

The falling body does not bear upon the man's breast or back or legs; it depends altogether upon the place it passes over, and the force with which it presses against whatever object it meets.

This is no mere fanciful speculation. It is a truth founded in observation and experiment.

Gravity is a slow continuous pull rather than an instant blow and weight is drawn away gradually; nothing about removability or acceleration.
You have touched a matter of considerable interest, one I have long suspected without finding a direct conclusion.

I am convinced we must abandon the notion that nature operates with instant force upon the body at the moment of fall. We observe this in the falling of a man, the descent of a stone, the rebound of a ball. The body loses its momentum only gradually: first the legs and arms are bent, then the head bowed, then the whole frame falls together. This gradual loss of force manifests itself in the condition of objects nearby, which are drawn away from the body's motion by their own weight.

I am inclined to conclude that gravity is a gradual agent: its action is not abrupt but continuous. The man's fall does not strike an immediate blow upon his feet or head; it draws away something from the body, and that something lifts a weight upon the floor or the walls of the cabin. This weight may not be immediately felt. It is the quiet and silent pull of the earth, not a sudden assault upon the person's person.

We may now account for more curious effects: a person failing freely from a roof does not feel their own weight during the fall. This suggests that the body's pull is not a sudden, violent push upon the feet or head, but a steady, continuous force. It does not strike at once, but draws away part of the man's momentum gradually. A stone dropped from a height and struck upon a floor does not rebound with a sudden force; it moves steadily, drawing away every part of the stone's body.

This principle appears precisely in nature's operations: we observe it in the motion of falling bodies, in the sinking of a stone upon a plane surface, in the gentle passage of a carriage over a level road. The same force that draws away a body also pulls upon neighbouring objects, whether they are close or distant from the faller's person. It operates more steadily than an instant blow might achieve, and we may conclude with confidence that our experience is a consequence of this slow, uniform pull of the earth, not an instant attack.

We may therefore conclude that a body's fall does not terminate instantaneously, but rather moves through a steady and resistant process: it draws something away from the body, and that drawn weight acts laterally and obliquely, carrying the weight quietly along the floor or wall until it is balanced upon a support or rested upon another object. It is not a sudden blow but a slow, steady action, and this action is the same as our experience of falling bodies -- they are drawn from their height by the gradual pull of the earth, and that pull is always in motion.

This conclusion satisfies my mind better than any theory we presently entertain regarding the instant or suddenness of that descent.
