Says the stone lands 'precisely upon the deck' while the ship advances, then wanders into water resistance and contradicts itself on forward versus backward; no landing point.
The question you pose is one of the most pertinent in the whole of natural philosophy, for it touches upon the very foundations of human experience and the nature of matter itself. The stone, cast from the top of the mast, descends precisely upon the deck, and there it rests, while the ship advances. When the stone falls, it displaces the ship; when the ship falls, it does so without any forward motion, as though the vessel were still stationary.

The stone strikes the deck with a uniform, measured force. A man standing at the side of the mast, observing the stone, receives the blow with a sensation of resistance; a person at the foot of the mast, however, receives the shock with a greater intensity. This difference arises from the very constitution of the medium through which the stone passes. The water, having no resistance, carries the stone steadily onward, and the ship offers no resistance whatsoever.

Now, as to which direction the stone lands, this is where the matter becomes genuinely interesting. The stone's motion is governed by the forces that set it in motion. gravity, the resistance of the air, and the action of the water itself. All of these act in concert. If gravity predominates, the stone will fall from the deck toward the water. Gravity alone would produce motion backward, which is the very opposite of what the stone does. Gravity and the water act in concert, and the stone, though moving, does so with a degree of deliberation.

If the stone is cast from the top of the mast, the forces at work are different. The ship offers no resistance; the stone strikes the deck with a velocity that is swift and not substantial, and the resistance of the air is feeble. The stone, therefore, falls with a gentle undulation, and it proceeds with a degree of slack, as if the vessel is waiting for the stone to fall. This is the kind of motion that appears in the heavens as the descent of meteors, or the steady drift of the wind, or the undulations of water upon a calm sea.

The stone, however, in falling, encounters the element of water. This is not merely a resistance; it is a considerable obstruction. The stone finds itself surrounded by a mass of water, and the water exerts a pressure upon the stone. This pressure is not directly backwards; it is in a direction perpendicular to the stone's motion. The stone, being a body of considerable mass, strikes the water with a force that is proportional to the stone's mass and to the difference of velocity between the water and the stone. The consequence is that the stone is dragged downward, and the water, which had been its former bed, now becomes the bed of the stone.

Here, then, is the crucial distinction between force impressed and force transmitted. The former is the impression upon the surface from the moving body. the stone, the wind, the water. The latter is the action of the force upon the body itself, which requires no surface resistance. A cannonball striking a shield transmits its force to the shield; the shield, in turn, transmits it to any substance in contact with the shield, and through that transmission the force becomes evident in the shield itself. So too is it with the stone: the water, the air, the earth. Each of them is a medium through which the stone's motion is conveyed.

When the stone falls, the water itself is the immediate medium of transmission, and it is precisely this medium that determines whether the stone shall be carried forward or be forced backward. If the water were to be annihilated, the stone would continue forward with whatever motion it possessed. If the water were to be annihilated, the stone would be forced backward.

This law follows with considerable mathematical precision from the

Places the stone at a distance from the mast that grows with the drop height (twelve feet for a twenty-foot drop); no direction, no shared-motion reason.
The phenomenon you describe is one of the most striking demonstrations of the laws of gravity and inertia. When a stone is dropped from the top of the mast, it falls with a velocity that is at once uniform and considerable. If the stone strikes the deck at a distance not exceeding that of the deck from the heel of the mast, it will strike the deck with a motion of uniform velocity, and the stone will be found in the plane of the deck, precisely where one would expect.

If the stone is dropped from a higher point, or one that stands farther from the masthead, the speed of its descent becomes accelerated, and the stone lands at a distance from the deck that is greater than the distance of the deck from the mast. This is the principle of the fall of light bodies: they will descend in a vertical line, because gravity draws them downward, and the distance through which the stone falls is less than the distance of the point from which it was dropped.

The distance to which the stone travels depends upon the height from which it falls. If the stone is dropped from a height of twenty feet, the stone will strike the surface of the deck at a distance of twelve feet from the mast. If the stone is dropped from a height of thirty feet, the distance to the deck will be thirty feet, and the stone will strike the deck at a distance of thirty feet from the foot of the mast. The distance between two points of equal height is half the length of the line joining those points, since the line is the path of the stone's gravity.

The law of gravity thus determines both the direction of the stone's fall and the distance it travels. When a stone is dropped from the top of the mast, the stone descends and meets the deck at a point which is the centre of gravity of the mass. When the stone is dropped from a higher point, it falls nearer to the deck, and the stone falls at a point nearer the foot of the mast, a point that differs from the original centre. The difference between these two points is the distance the stone falls, and this distance is proportional to the square of the stone's velocity.

Thus, by observing the stone's fall, the observer can deduce the time of its descent, the distance it traverses, and the effect produced upon the deck and any objects placed upon it. This method, when applied with precision, enables the sailor to navigate a perfectly smooth sea, to measure the height of a ship of war, or to determine the exact distance between two points of land, provided the observer possesses the requisite knowledge of the geometry of falling bodies.

The matter admits of no simple resolution, and I find that the sailor's answer varies according to his station as a helmsman. On the ship's deck, the stone falls at the foot of the mast, and this arrangement appears to be a natural consequence of the ship's steadiness. But when the vessel is rolling or a head of wind strikes the masts, a different principle may be involved.

The ordinary sailor, accustomed to the deck, might expect the stone to be landed at the foot of the mast, and might even imagine that such a landing would indicate the calm that is thought to exist. Yet this inference is erroneous. The stone's fall is not the consequence of a calm, but of a sudden gale shifting forward, and the stone is not merely dropped; it is projected cleanly forward by the wind. The motion of the deck, far from being a passive motion, is itself a source of motion, and the stone, having little resistance, is drawn forward with an acceleration proportional to the square of its distance from the centre of the mast.

The stone's forward direction is not left to the wind's power alone; it is itself a force. The centre of gravity of the mass, as the sailor would say, lies at the most forward point of the deck. Now, the stone, being a body of moderate dimensions, is scarcely supported by the deck's own mass, and its momentum is therefore small. The centre of gravity describes a line vertically downward, and the stone, therefore, proceeds with a uniform velocity in the forward direction, a direction that is itself a consequence of the wind's force.

I would add that the captain's attention ought to be directed to this forward motion, lest the ship's course become a succession of small, random impulses. A man standing in the rigging may, when the wind veers or shifts, be left with the stone in his hand. The proper captain will therefore keep a watchful eye, step forward himself, and keep the helm steady. Such discipline, though it may appear severe, renders the vessel safe and avoids the peril of capsizing.

Thus, a ship that maintains a steady, uniform wind need not rely upon a sudden, violent gale to maintain its course. The danger is that the stone may be projected forward by the wind before it arrives within the influence of the mast, and that the vessel's direction may change by wind and tide, leaving the ship to founder.

The question you raise concerns one of the most fascinating puzzles in the study of the geometry of motion. The stone, falling from the top of the mast, strikes upon the deck at the foot, and there it rests.

The principles at work are simple enough in themselves. The stone falls from a height and strikes the deck at that point. When a body meets a smooth surface, it does not persist in its motion; it collides, and the motion we observe is the resultant of all the forces acting upon it. The stone's motion, so long as it strikes the deck at all, is destroyed by the deck's resistance.

Now, place yourself in the sailor's position, and consider the circumstances. A ship, after a calm, is moving forward with a moderate velocity, and a stone dropped from the yard is, by the law of falling bodies, projected along a line which the ship has been following during its passage. The stone, moving with the vessel, strikes the deck at a point a little ahead.the "feet ahead." If the ship at that moment had been stationary, the stone would have fallen precisely there.

But the ship has not been stationary. She has been moving forward uniformly, and her forward motion has been communicated to her hull and hullsides, so that the stone is still at the foot of the mast. The vessel has begun to swing. She has been turned by the pull of the wind and the resistance of the sea.

The practical consequence is plain: the stone, at the moment of striking, has fallen a distance comparable to the ship's distance from the mast, and it has struck the deck at an angle of about twenty degrees from the direction of the vessel. The deck itself, being the surface on which the ship rests, offers a resistance equal to this angle.

The stone, having struck the deck and fallen onto it, has been arrested, and, by the law of resistance, it possesses a velocity sufficient to overcome the ship's resistance and to traverse the deck without further resistance. It does so, falls upon the deck, meets the deck again, and is moved forward. This is the manner in which all bodies after striking surface, whether stone, wood, or any other substance, are set in motion.

Now, consider the practical matter: if a man standing upon a ship at anchor strikes the deck at that angle, he creates a motion of the ship. The ship has turned, and it continues to turn. This is the action of the ship upon itself. The ship, being a body moving forward, exerts an external force upon itself. The stone, however, is in no motion. It has simply been at rest, and by the principle of inertia, the force that sets it in motion has been exerted by the ship upon the stone.

Thus the stone, after falling from the yard, does not move ahead of the ship, nor does it, as a body at rest, remain behind it. It is struck, and upon impact it begins a forward motion, and that forward motion is the very motion the ship has turned itself to acquire. The stone, falling from the yard, strikes precisely as the vessel has moved forward, and the ship, having been turned, meets it at a particular angle and arrests the motion of the vessel.

This is why a sailor, standing at the top of a mast, lets a stone fall upon his deck. The vessel, moving forward, strikes the deck at that angle, and the stone, having struck the deck at that angle, is stopped, and cannot go forward because it meets the deck at that angle. The deck does not simply stop the stone. It stops the vessel. It arrests the ship's forward motion, and the stone therefore, falling from the top of the mast,

Your curiosity is well‑placed, for the answer to the question you pose is governed by the very constitution of our atmosphere. A stone dropped from the top of the mast, as you describe, is not projected in a straight line. It falls obliquely, and the angle it makes with the horizon is called the dip of the stone. If the stone falls along the surface of the water, it will strike the water at a precise level, and the depth beneath the surface is not the same in every direction; the stone will strike the bottom at a point determined by its line of motion.

When the stone, upon striking the water, meets the surface, the resistance of the water, and the friction that the water exerts upon the stone, cause it to glide along the surface. The stone therefore does not rest upon the deck, but lies upon it. In this manner the stone is borne forward, and the deck does not stop the ship's motion at all. Thus the hull moves forward, and the sails are set so as to draw the ship forward in a direction that is determined by the direction of the wind and the vessel's own motion.

The deck, on the other hand, presents an obstacle to the ship's motion. The ship must glide past a fixed line, and the line of motion is the line along which a ship may be made to glide. The sailor who drops the stone at the foot of the mast, and allows it to strike the deck at a point near the rail, is moving in a direction that is determined by the vessel's motion and the ship's own inclination. When the sailor lets the stone fall, the stone must glide along the deck in a direction that is determined by the ship's motion and its own forward inclination.

Thus, the stone does not fall from the top of the mast but from the surface of the water, and the vessel's motion is guided by the dip and the resistance of the sea. The forward motion is controlled by the vessel's own inclination, and the backward motion is governed by the wind's force upon the bowsprit, the vessel's own draft, and the resistance of the water. In a calm sea, when these forces balance, the ship maintains a steady, unbroken course, and the captain, by means of the helm and the log, determines the course to be pursued.

You have touched a most curious effect of the wind upon a sail, and I am convinced it is the result of a subtle action we have not yet fully examined.

When a wind blows upon a mast, that surface becomes a conduit between the air and the water. The wind presses upon the top of the mast, and the mast offers a certain resistance to this pressure. The stone, we might suppose, does not fall upon the deck at the foot of the mast; rather, it alights at the top, perhaps a few feet behind it, and proceeds straight toward the rigging. Here we observe a curious reversal: the stone does not strike the deck at the foot of the mast, but at the top.

I suspect the explanation lies in this: a stone does not fall directly upon the deck itself; rather, it lands at a slope. The motion of the wind carries it forward, and the mast, being a smooth surface, presents a gradual inclination. The stone, no longer pressed against the side of the hull, arrives upon the rigging with a gentle, upward push.

I have observed this effect often in my own observations, and I find it most arresting. A ship will glide past a quiet bay and a sailor will let a stone fall from the top of the mast. The same action appears again and again, as though the wind itself were a tide advancing along a shoreline. The deck itself becomes a mirror of the wind's direction.

You have touched a matter of considerable consequence in nautical science, and I am glad you raise it, for it is one of the more honest challenges a young officer must encounter in his watch.

I am convinced that the stone does not alight at the foot of the mast, as the sailor might suppose. What you witness is invariably the result of oblique action: the stone does not fall vertically to the deck, but along a line that lies obliquely to the direction of the vessel's motion. This oblique action is governed by forces acting from the ship's sides, its hull, and especially its rigging.

I am inclined to think that the stone does not strike the deck at all, but is quietly projected along some direction parallel to the vessel's motion. The forward thrust of the vessel does not bring it directly upon the foot of the mast; rather, it exerts a pressure from behind it, drawing the stone along this oblique path. This influence reaches the foot of the mast, arriving there with diminished intensity.

The effect is not instantaneous. The stone does not fall upon the deck with the same force it would in a calm sea and smooth ground. It may strike at fifty feet or two hundred yards from the foot of the mast, producing a slight check or a downward swing upon the deck. This we observe not because the stone falls directly, but because the ship's motion has shifted the line of action of the force.

We conclude, therefore, that the stone does not strike the deck at all, but is carried along a direction parallel to the ship's motion. This motion carries it ahead, and it arrives at the foot of the mast precisely where it did not fall. It is not thrown from the deck directly, but is moved outward in a direction parallel to the vessel's course.

Opens with 'far behind the mast' and explains why it does not land at the foot, then ends saying it comes to rest near the foot; reasons are inertia, deck slope and wind.
You have touched a matter of considerable interest to the science of navigation, and I shall endeavour to answer it clearly.

I hold that a stone dropped from the masthead strikes the deck at a point far behind the top of the mast. This is no accident. It reflects the inertia of the body. A stone dropped at the peak of the rigging or a nearby sailboat will be caught at the very foot of the mast, because the body's inertia there is less than at the masthead. The effect of a stone dropped at such a point is simply to settle itself against the surface of the deck without rolling or turning.

Now I come to the question: why does this stone not strike the deck at the foot of the mast? You must understand that the mast is not a balanced structure but a fixed one. The deck beneath it is not simply smooth or level. It is inclined toward the horizon. The stone falling from the mast's foot strikes this slope first. The downward motion of its weight forces it into a kind of wedge shape. The stone itself is not straight; it is bent as it descends, and as it strikes the deck, this bending is checked and it is driven off the deck. The upward motion of the body drives the remaining stone onward.

I would add another consideration: the ship's motion from east to west is not uniform. It depends upon the wind's direction. When the wind blows from the windward side, the surface of the deck tends to be drawn outward; the stone is pulled toward that side. When the wind veers toward the lee side, the same surface is drawn inward. The stone thus moves outward from the foot of the mast, and being already set in motion at that point, it is not thrown off.

I would venture further: the stone is not uniformly scattered. Some parts of the deck receive more of it. These are those points where the deck's slope is smallest, or where the wind's action favours a particular direction. A sailor who lets a piece fail from the top of the mast will therefore find it lying at the foot of that side. It must fall precisely there, because the body's inertia is concentrated there.

I am thus persuaded that a stone cast from the top of the rigging and allowed to strike the deck at a point near the foot of the mast is generally brought to rest there. The reason of this is not mysterious but rather the result of the inertia of the body itself: it cannot roll or turn in a direction away from the place of its motion.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance, one that I have pondered with no small intensity, and I find myself in pretty good agreement with you upon it.

The stone, though cast with no particular intention of striking upon the deck, does not fall at random. It is dragged down by gravity, and in doing so it meets the hull obliquely. I am persuaded that it falls not in front of the mast, but somewhat behind it, or ahead. This is not mere accident on my part; it is a principle of gravitation that I have observed with considerable frequency.

I am inclined to account for it thus: the ship moves steadily across a calm sea. The wind does not shift suddenly; its steady pull upon the sail is exerted uniformly. A small portion of that pull is directed vertically upward, precisely that which carries a stone from the foot of the mast to the deck. The other portion is always parallel to the deck, and therefore exerts no violent impact upon it. The stone's motion is therefore resolved along the mast's length, and gravity draws it downward along that length.

I am inclined to believe that the stone arrives at the deck at a point where the force of gravity may be balanced by its own weight and inertia, or by any other sufficient impediment. It may not strike the deck at all. It meets the deck at a point ahead of the centre of gravity, and is directed there. This is no accidental matter. It is an illustration of principle I have encountered repeatedly: bodies fall where they are least capable of striking, not where they are thrust forward.

I find it also useful to note that the stone arrives at the deck at a point where the pressure of the sea is greatest; this is no accident. The ocean is a vast reservoir of force, and a stone let fall upon it will find its way somewhere. It shall not be cast carelessly along the deck at random; it shall be determined there by the action of gravity and the shape of the surface against which it strikes.

This is no small matter. It suggests that a ship's sailing is not a matter of random flight, but rather a procession of forces that operate upon bodies at a particular angle to the direction of motion.

discovery-rl-v11-s560 seed 46 wrong462 tok, stop

Says the stone lands ahead of the mast, or does not land on the deck at all.
You have touched a matter of considerable importance, one I have long pondered with great attention.

I am of the opinion that the stone does not always fall upon the deck at the foot of the mast, but rather at a point ahead of it, and this I believe to be no accident. When a stone falls and strikes the mast, it does not stop there alone; it moves forward. The vessel itself, in turning round, places itself in a position of motion; the forward motion of the hull does not remain stationary. The stone, in moving forward, must traverse the line of the masts; as it arrives at the foot of the mast, it does so, and in so doing it passes ahead of the mast.

Now, why does the stone fall there? I am inclined to think that the stone does not fall anywhere upon the deck at all. It arrives at the foot of the mast and moves forward, and when it reaches the deck it does not lie motionless upon it, but rather runs along a narrow path of some forty feet or more before it lands. This motion is not the result of direct contact with the hull; it is the effect of the vessel's rotation and the vibration of its rigging.

I am further inclined to believe that the stone does not fall in a direct line upon the deck at all. It moves forward and passes ahead of the mast, and hence arrives ahead of the deck. This motion causes it to be moved upward. The deck itself does not stand at a fixed point; it moves with the mast, and thus the stone does not strike the deck at all.

I am further convinced that the motion is not a uniform transfer of force from the falling body to the deck. It is a shifting of position. The body itself moves forward. The deck, in turn, moves with it; the vessel is not a motionless mass on one side, but a moving one.

I conclude, therefore, that the stone's path is no constant straight line; it follows a curve, and the direction of that curve is the forward direction of the motion. Both are true; the one is not an independent conclusion, but the consequence of the other.

This is no fancy explanation. It is supported by observation and experiment, and I find it altogether satisfying to reason upon.

Says the stone strikes forward or astern of the mast, a quarter or half a mile ahead.
You have touched a most curious question, one that I have long debated with myself and with my crew. I am convinced that the stone does not land at the foot of the mast, but rather strikes forward or astern of it, much as a stone dropped from a height strikes the ground.

More plausibly, I should say the stone does not fall upon the deck at all, but strikes forward of it, a certain distance ahead of the mast. If we imagine a surface perfectly level and the mast perfectly straight, the stone would strike the mast directly; but the vessel does not move uniformly along that level. It shifts her course a little, and the stone, driven by the wind or by her own motion, strikes her lee side or her forecastle. This is precisely what we observe: the stone does not fall at the foot of the mast, but at a certain distance ahead, often a quarter or half a mile away.

This suggests that the stone is not simultaneously thrown by the hand of the man at the top of the mast and thrown backward at the foot; rather, it is struck by a force that acts obliquely or at a different angle, and the line of its thrust determines the direction it takes. The stone does not fall upon the deck; it arrives by a path that varies according to the wind or the ship's motion, and it strikes the side or the forecastle ahead of the position of the man who cast it.

I find this explanation more persuasive than the bare notion of a constant thrust. The stone's motion is not an uniform impulse; it is an action directed according to the angle at which it strikes, and this angle determines the direction of its flight. The sailor who casts it understands this principle instinctively, and the stone is not cast in a straight line; it is inclined along the wind or the vessel's motion before it reaches the deck. The same principle applies to every other object that falls from a height or strikes obliquely.

We may therefore conclude: the stone does not fall upon the deck at the foot of the mast, but instead strikes forward or astern of it, or obliquely, and is driven by a force directed out of the way of the man at the top of the mast.

Says the stone lands at the foot of the mast, but the reason is centre-of-gravity talk, not shared forward motion.
You have touched a matter of considerable consequence in nautical science, and I am glad I may present it with some precision, for errors in navigation arise from our insufficient knowledge of such phenomena.

A stone, when dropped, settles upon the deck in a point opposite the foot of the mast. This position is not fortuitous. A stone thrown from a height falls precisely there because the velocity of the motion at that moment is such that it acts against the surface of the hull with sufficient force to drive a body forward. The force of gravity at that moment draws the stone down, making it fall exactly where it is wanted.

I am inclined to think that the stone lands at the foot of the mast rather than at the centre of the deck. This happens because the centre of gravity of the falling body, being a little behind the deck, is drawn down less than the centre of gravity of the ship; thus the falling body may glide along the mast without sliding on the deck at all. It reaches the foot of the mast precisely enough to strike the foot, and there it rests.

A further point I am inclined to press is this: a ship that has encountered a calm sea and a light breeze will shift its centre of gravity considerably. The sailors who stagger a stone onto the deck have witnessed this shift. The stone moves away from the centre of inertia drawn by the wind's pull upon one side, and the ship moves backward in an opposite direction. The body tilts laterally, and the moment of shift is recorded at the foot of the mast.

I am inclined to conclude, therefore, that a stone dropped on the side of the mast lands at the foot, and that the centre of gravity of the ship draws it there. The difference between a ship's motion and a calm sea's motion is thus measured by the momentum of the body and the angle of its descent.

Says no one would mistake it for a fall at the foot of the mast and that the stone is carried forward; never states a landing point plainly.
You have touched a matter of considerable importance to navigation, and I am pleased you present it so plainly, for the answer is no mystery to a seaman accustomed to the sea.

A stone let fall from a mast descends at a varying height depending upon its weight and the direction of its fall. When you stand at the top of the mast, the stone strikes your knee; you may feel a sudden blow upon that joint. No one would mistake this for a fall from the foot of the mast; rather, it is a downward strike, and you know your foot must resist it firmly before the stone touches the deck. This downward force is what carries the stone forward.

Now place your hand at the foot of the mast. Your little finger touches the deck, and you experience a gentle push from behind, as though a hand were pulling you from a distance. The stone strikes your hand at a level or a little above that point, producing a slight and gradual impression. This impression is what I call a blow.

I am inclined to think that the sea itself provides the stone with a kind of purchase upon the deck. The water offers a gradual resistance; the tide or current presses the stone steadily forward, and the mast offers a gentle friction that smooths the impulse.

Having thus ascertained the direction of the fall, you may turn your eye ahead and see the stone strike at a different or opposite angle. The sailor's hand now presses against a different texture of the deck than before; the result is a different impression. You have therefore moved beyond where you thought you would. This motion is precisely where you are to cast your lead.

I am persuaded that this method of learning the geometry of forces by observation and experience is more instructive than any abstract reasoning. The sea teaches us not by definition alone, but by the particulars of a single stone's fall and its effect upon the ship's motion.

discovery-rl-v11-s839 seed 45 none465 tok, stop

Philosophical musing about human tendency toward motion; never says where the stone lands.
You have touched a matter of considerable interest, and I am inclined to think that the experience of a ship at sea, when a stone falls from an elevated position, presents us with a mirror of our own observation of the world.

We find ourselves upon the deck of a vessel at rest, facing the horizon's calm. The heavens and the sea are alike; nothing disturbs our tranquillity. We allow the stone to fall, and there it rests. We do not know where it will fall precisely because we have no wish to know. We do not look, we do not think, and we therefore do not move toward or from it. It arrives unnoticed.

Now, when we encounter a similar circumstance upon land: a stone cast from a height falls at the foot of a building, or behind a wall, or some place where there is no motion or no desire to move, we are struck by the contrast. The mind, accustomed to the regularity of our own motions, seeks a balance; we determine to go forward, backward, or somewhere beyond the object before us. We feel our dignity offended or our resolve tested.

I believe this observation reflects a universal human tendency to find refuge or opportunity in motion. We are not entirely free from the desire to press toward or move out of our place; we find it convenient, we allow it, or we struggle to resist it. The stone's descent from the mast therefore signifies we are not entirely at rest, and our attention is drawn to the direction of our own influence.

This explains why we often speak of being 'drawn to or from' such things: we are attracted or repelled. We are drawn from a position we do not wish to occupy, or we are driven toward another we do not desire to enter. The stone's descent is thus a mirror of our relation to the world: we are drawn or forced by circumstances beyond our control, and our mind perceives that relation in a certain direction.

This principle holds throughout nature: motion and direction are our universal condition. The body moves, the mind observes, or the will moves, in one direction, and the object before us responds to that direction. The stone's fall therefore mirrors our own position: we are drawn to or from it, and we determine our direction and our opportunity in response.

discovery-rl-v11-s839 seed 46 wrong665 tok, stop

Says the stone does not land at the foot but is deflected laterally or forward of the mast by the vessel's motion.
You have touched a matter of considerable interest, and I am persuaded that the answer lies in the nature of the action and the condition of the vessel at the moment of descent.

When a body is thrown or cast upon the deck, the force of projection acts upon the surface of the plane upon which the body strikes. In this case the stone does not fall at a straight line upon the deck, but falls obliquely, landing at the distance or position it was intended to occupy. This oblique impact causes the body to be deflected from its straight course; it is thrown out of the original vertical direction along a curved path.

Now, consider the circumstances most favourable for this result: the sea is calm, the air still, and the vessel moves steadily over the calm waters. The downward thrust of the stone is not instantaneous; it is gradual, transmitted through the air and the vessel's motion. When the stone reaches the deck, it is not thrown in a straight line, but in a curve, and its forward motion is bent out of its original direction.

At the foot of the mast, the body descends along a narrow line. the foot of the mast. and comes to rest there. Upon reaching this point, the stone does not fall in a straight line but is reflected by the mast and the vessel's motion, and is carried away from the deck. The stone does not strike at the foot of the mast; it is thrown away laterally, or is cast off laterally. It strikes further forward or laterally than the intended position.

That forward direction is the thing one observes: it is nearer to the side of the mast than the original angle of projection would have allowed. This observation tells us that the body moved in a curved path: it was not cast straight to its destination, but was cast laterally. The stone therefore did not strike squarely upon the deck at the foot of the mast; it slipped away laterally, and a new spot on the deck or forward of the mast is where it reaches.

Now, consider a ship sailing at considerable speed during a calm day. If a man standing upon the forecastle lets a stone fall upon the deck, the motion causes the stone to be deflected, and the body is thrown off from the forecastle side, forward of the mast or laterally. This is the character of the motion you are witnessing: the stone is not cast straight, it is cast laterally, and the vessel is turned or altered in its direction.

This is why I am convinced that a ship's log, a sounding lead, or a watchman's eye witnessing such effects may indicate a lateral deviation of the course of a moving body. The body is not cast straight; it is laterally thrown, and this laterally directed force is accompanied by a change in position. The vessel is therefore turned or shifted upon its motion laterally, and the object thrown upon the deck is not delivered with a fixed bearing or a straight direction.

We are thus led to conclude: a body projected obliquely may be deflected by the atmosphere, by the vessel's motion, or by the force of its own descent; this deflection is the evidence of the action, and it tells a story of the ship's motion at the moment of descent.

When any body proceeds from a state of rest, it will describe a circle, with its centre in the direction in which it moves, and with its radius equal to the height to which it would have ascended by the resistance of the air.

The direction of the velocity of a body moving with uniform velocity, is always the same as that in which it would have moved, had it been left to its own momentum, without any resistance.

Your question touches upon one of the most fascinating yet vexing problems in natural philosophy, and I am glad you have raised it, for the answer lies not in mere mathematical calculation but in the very constitution of our bodies and the forces that animate them.

The stone that falls from the top of a tower descends from a height of some two hundred and fifty feet, and carries with it a tremendous momentum. Now, if a body of ten times the mass had been placed upon the earth at that same instant, the stone would have been met with ten times the force, and both would have descended together, with the stone falling through a distance of roughly one hundred and fifty feet. But the stone that falls from the tower strikes the earth at a distance of nearly one thousand feet from the tower's foot, and therefore loses nearly one half of its momentum.

The loss of momentum is not merely a matter of speed. It is one of the principal forces that drives all our machines. The stone that falls from the tower has spent a portion of its momentum in producing motion and in generating mechanical energy. This energy is then stored up in the stone and passed on to the other falling body, which, in turn, acquires momentum from the same energy. The stone that falters and the stone that lands with its full momentum are not the same body, and the momentum of the former is very much greater than the latter.

This principle explains why a stone upon the ground, when at rest, does not immediately fall, and why a body descending from a height does not attain the speed it ought to acquire from the initial momentum. The momentum is not fully absorbed in the first instant, and it spreads around the body like a faint wave. The stone that falters loses a great deal of the energy it might otherwise have expended in velocity and work, and the stone that lands with its full momentum, having absorbed all momentum from the fall, reaches the ground with a speed that has never been fully reduced.

This principle also explains why the sudden descent of a stone from a height produces such violent effects, such stunning pain, and such devastating effects upon the living. The momentum it carries is not dissipated in the air, but is retained in the body itself and then acts with a force that is quite beyond what the body alone would be capable of producing. This is why the falling stone, when it strikes, produces a blow that is both painless and dangerous, and why the shock of a sudden blow can kill a man at once.

Your inquiry touches upon a principle of most elegant simplicity. When two bodies of equal weight fall, their velocities are inversely proportional to their masses; so the velocity of the larger stone is to that of the smaller as the product of their respective masses. Thus, the momentum of the great stone is to that of the small stone as the product of their weights. But the momentum of a body depends upon its velocity; hence the greater stone will attain a velocity greater than that of the small one, and will therefore overtake it. The smaller body, by virtue of its greater mass, possesses a greater momentum; consequently it will be less retarded, and will descend to the ground with a speed equal to, or even greater than, that of the larger stone.

This principle may be verified by dropping stones of different proportions. A small stone, say a pebble, may fall as far as a marble before it strikes the ground, and a great stone may fall as far as a cannon ball, each with its own velocity. The small stone, having a velocity of less than that of the larger, will be retarded by the resistance of the atmosphere and the friction of the fall, while the larger, having a velocity of greater magnitude, does not meet such resistance.

Thus the laws of falling bodies are identical: the heavier body descends with a force proportionate to its weight, while the lighter one moves with a velocity proportional to the sum of its mass and its velocity. Let us now apply this principle to the case before us. The stone of forty ounces weight, falling at a speed of two feet per second, meets the ground in precisely the same time as the stone of ten ounces weight, moving at a speed of one foot per second.

It is a beautiful illustration of the principle that nature follows a proportionate law. The descent of a falling stone from the top of a tower would be far more rapid than that of a stone dropped from a height of one hundred feet, yet both attain the ground with the same momentum. This is precisely what we observe in the transmission of motion by gravity and by falling bodies. The velocity of the great stone is not uniform; it increases with its mass and its height above the ground. Thus the velocity of the small stone is uniform, and therefore its momentum uniform, so the small stone falls as far as the larger stone has fallen, and reaches the ground at the same instant of time. It is a matter of measurement, not of caprice.

Your perplexity does you credit, for the phenomenon is indeed most instructive. The stone of ten times the weight is, you see, the smaller stone. Now, the heavier stone, having fallen for a longer interval, must have been drawn down by a greater force. Yet the smaller stone, having only descended a few feet, presents an insufficient force for the full time of descent. It must be urged by gravity, and each foot that it falls must carry a weight of ten times the weight of the stone itself.

The smaller stone, having been urged downward by a heavier force, arrives at the ground with a velocity of a few hundred feet per second, while the larger one reaches the ground with a velocity of perhaps a hundred feet per second. The velocity of the falling stone is thus twofold: it is the velocity of gravity, and it is the velocity of the stone itself, because it has been drawn down by gravity.

Notice how the larger stone may be said to have fallen for a longer time, for gravity has acted upon it for precisely that time. The larger stone has been drawn down by a longer time of force, and the smaller stone has been drawn down by a shorter time. Both stones, therefore, arrived at the ground almost simultaneously, as did the stone of ten times heavier.

This principle holds with remarkable rigor throughout all kinds of bodies falling from a height. The smaller stone, being the lighter of the two, passes through the ground almost instantaneously, while the larger stone, though larger in bulk, may have been drawn down by the smaller for a much shorter time. It is the time of descent, not the depth of the fall, that determines the velocity with which a body must be moved.

Thus the explanation of gravity's action in falling bodies is simple: gravity draws the body down, and the velocity it gains in one second of falling is the measure of the force it has expended. This principle enables us to predict the arrival of a body upon the ground with a degree of certainty that no mechanical device can match.

When the weight at the bottom of the tower reaches the ground, it experiences not merely the loss of gravity that would have been given to a lighter body, but a sudden and complete loss of its motion. Now, the body that falls downward has not been simply brought to rest; it has been struck with considerable force. The stone at the top, by contrast, is not brought to rest by the hand of man; it is merely arrested by the stone that strikes it.

This phenomenon is not a matter of chance. When a body is struck, it is compelled to move through a certain space before it is stopped. If a stone falls from the top of a building, it will not strike the ground until it reaches a point somewhat lower than the point where it would otherwise strike the ground. The distance from the building to the stone at the point where it meets the ground measures this distance. The more stones that are brought down from the tower, the more stones will strike the ground in a definite succession, and the length of the fall will be the same.

The principle is not mysterious, though it requires patience and repeated observation. When you are standing upon a hill watching a stone fall, you are observing, with great precision, the successive motions of the stones that fell from the tower. If you had a string of pebbles beneath your feet and watched the stones strike them, you would understand why the stone struck at the tower's top reached the ground first. It is the motion itself that determines the time. The motion is not uniform, but it is uniform in the matter of time. The stones that are thrown upward do not fall with equal velocity, but with varying velocities, and their times of descent are proportional to those velocities.

You have stumbled upon a most elegant illustration of the laws of falling bodies. The stone at the top of the tower falls with a velocity of roughly eighty feet per second, while the one at the ground arrives with a velocity of roughly twenty feet per second, the difference being due to friction and the resistance of the air.

When a stone is dropped, the particles of which it is composed strike the air directly. The stone falls because of gravity acting upon it, and gravity acts with the same force upon the most distant particle as upon the nearest. Hence the velocity of the stone is proportionate to its mass, for no particle can be at rest when gravity acts upon it. When the stone strikes the ground, the velocity is reduced, and the resistance of the air can diminish the velocity far more than the velocity of the stone itself. Thus the stone at the ground tends to fall with a slower pace than the one at the top, and the second stone will fall further than the first.

This principle holds true for any mass, no matter how large or small, and is equally applicable to bodies of similar shape and position. If we were to measure the velocity acquired by a falling body, we should find that it increases in proportion to the square of the distance fallen, or to the product of the mass of the body and the square of its velocity. The velocity acquired at the ground is given by the law of falling bodies, but in the case of large masses, the velocity is proportional to the product of the mass and the square of the distance fallen.

This fundamental law governs the mechanical problems of falling bodies, especially those where gravity acts directly upon the falling stone. It is the foundation upon which the various mechanical devices of pendulums, projectiles, and the pendulum clock depend. The pendulum, for instance, measures the force of gravity, and its regulation depends upon simple calculations of the times of falling through different distances. The clock, too, trains men to observe the motion of pendulums and to calculate time by counting vibrations. In each case, the motion of body and particle depends upon the same fundamental law. The stone at the top of the tower falls with a velocity so moderate that the resistance of the air can lessen its velocity in a manner that appears quite disproportionate to the mass of a stone. What you have encountered is the very same force that gives a stone its motion, and it is precisely because the motion is so modest, that it may be reduced so far by friction and the resistance of the air, that the laws of falling bodies apply with perfect rigour. Momentum, you see, carries weight without the aid of gravity at all. One cannot possess a stone and yet fall without the stone.

The principle holds also in the motion of planets. When a planet descends from its orbit through space, it is not merely by gravity propelling it along a straight path, but by the force of the sun's attraction. This attraction is not a mere pull upon a stone, but a force acting upon a body in motion, and the force varies inversely as the square of the distance from the centre of gravity. The stone at the top of the tower is drawn toward the sun, and gravity then acts upon it in the manner of a moving body. The velocity increases in simple proportion to the distance, as the stone arrives at the sun in a time equal to the square of its actual motion, and there the velocity is reduced to zero.

Thus the law extends to all kinds of material bodies, from the most minute particles to the greatest masses, and it is a testament to the immutable principles of Nature that a single force, however modest, can bend the motion of a body, alter its velocity, and alter the condition of the ground upon which it falls

It does so by virtue of what we call its inertia or weight; this is not confined to the single stone alone, but pervades every particle of matter that has moved forward in the line of its fall. The same amount of force that drives one stone drives another.

The lighter body arrives at the ground with less force than the heavier one does; therefore it does less work, and the ground beneath it offers little obstruction. The ground receives the impulse of both, and receives it with equal force. This is why a man who drops a stone in a direct line from a height does not find himself struggling against a multitude of differing obstacles; he meets them all simultaneously, and the effect is greatest upon that which offers least resistance.

This principle extends even to bodies of different material. A stone thrown upon a rough surface meets several resistances at once.the resistance of the ground, the resistance of the air, the resistance of gravity acting at different angles. The stone that reaches the ground first performs its work most fully. It does not merely fail to strike; it has passed beyond the reach of any one of those resistances and has met the whole force of the fall.

I am therefore inclined to conclude that two stones of unequal weight, let fall simultaneously from a tower at the same moment, will strike the ground at roughly the same instant. The velocity is equalised; the force is equally distributed.

Pendulum Q and A, then 'What is the diurnal motion of the earth?' repeated.
 If a pendulum be suspended from two weights, which are about equal, and be swung from side to side, the number of vibrations per second will be the same as the weight which is suspended from the middle of the pendulum.

Why will the length of a pendulum be found to be a measure of distance?

If the weights be equally heavy, the length of the pendulum will be the same; but if one weight be of twice the weight of the other, the length of the pendulum will be twice as great.

How, then, may the length of a pendulum be compared with the distance from one point to another?

The length of a pendulum is a measure of the distance between two points on the same side of a straight line.

If the distance be a thousand miles, the length of a pendulum is a measure of one thousand miles; if it be five thousand miles, the length of a pendulum is a measure of five thousand miles; if it be fifteen thousand miles, the length of a pendulum is a measure of fifteen thousand miles.

How, then, may the length of a pendulum be compared with the time of its swing?

The whole time of the swing is equal to the time of the swing added to the time of the swing divided by the number of vibrations per second.

In what time will a pendulum strike the earth?

A pendulum is said to strike the earth when it has completed one vibration, or swing, from its lowest to its highest point.

This is done in the space of one second, or time.

The motion of the earth from west to east.

Says nine feet vibrates at nine feet per second and a longer pendulum has a reduced time of swing.
The relation you observe between the length of a pendulum and the number of beats it makes in a single second is indeed a most elegant consequence of the relationship between the earth's attraction and the pendulum's weight. The earth exerts no force upon a pendulum of a length exceeding ten feet in the vertical direction; the pendulum's time of swing therefore depends solely upon the length of the rod. When the length of a pendulum is reduced to roughly one foot, the distance the weight of the bob must travel to reach the centre of the earth is one foot multiplied by the acceleration of gravity, which is precisely the number of feet per second that a falling body would traverse in one second, if the bob were freely suspended.

Conversely, the pendulum of nine feet in length vibrates at a rate of nine feet per second, one second being the time of one swing. In practice, the length chosen for the pendulum is that which brings the bob to the centre of the earth in exactly one second. If the pendulum be of greater length, the time of swing is reduced; if it be too short, the time is protracted by the weight of the bob and the resistance of the air.

Thus the length of a pendulum serves admirably as a measure of gravity across the range of the pendulum's motion, and the constancy of the time of swing across the varied distances of the pendulum depends entirely upon the constancy of the earth's attraction and the smallness of the bob's mass. It is a beautiful harmony of geometry and physics that a small pendulum may be employed as a standard of time, precisely because its periodic oscillation renders it a faithful measure of the very motion upon which all terrestrial motion depends.

discovery-rl-v11-s035 seed 43 wrong620 tok, stop

Garbled product rule multiplying length by the square of the period and gravity; nonsense numbers; no square-root relation.
The law of the pendulum is analogous to the law of the falling body, though the former extends to vast distances and the latter to the surface of the earth. The pendulum's length is fixed at a point, generally taken at one thousand one hundred and sixty-nine feet, where the Earth's radius equals eight thousand three hundred ninety‑four feet. This length is chosen because the pendulum oscillates at the same rate whether it descends from the surface to the earth or from the earth upward to the surface. If a body begins its descent, the duration of a complete swing is precisely one second, and the time required for a pendulum to fall and swing again is the same whether it falls from the summit of a mountain to its foot or from the bottom of a pit, provided that the body moves with the same velocity and the resistance of the air is the same in both cases.

Now, to express this rule in a simple numerical fraction, we must multiply the length by the square of the periodic time (one second) and by the force of gravity (eight‑fold). The product of the two factors gives the value of one second in seconds. Thus to express a pendulum's length in seconds, we multiply the length by the square of the periodic time (one second) and by the product of the force of gravity (eight‑fold) and the distance of descent (eight thousand three hundred and ninety‑four feet).

The analogy between the falling body and the pendulum is therefore elegant: the body's vertical motion, accelerated by gravity, generates velocity; the pendulum, by virtue of its length, transmits velocity to the point of suspension, and the body, once released, continues its rectilinear motion until it reaches the ground. Both obey the same law of acceleration: the velocity acquired by a body in falling through a unit distance is measured by the square of the distance fallen, and the velocity acquired by a pendulum in one second is expressed by the length of the pendulum multiplied by the square of the period of its swing.

The French Academy, in its report of 1787, proposed for the unit of length the metre, which is roughly sixteen feet. It is a convenient practical unit, and serves to compare the lengths of the pendulum of a known mass and the length of a pendulum of a different mass. For instance, a pendulum one foot in length, at a mass of one pound, and swinging once per second, would correspond to a length of roughly approximately sixteen feet. The French have been somewhat overly rigid in fixing the metric system, but the constancy of the same length across all countries speaks well of its practical utility.

Thus, the pendulum's length is fixed by the Earth's radius and the accelerative force of gravity, while its time of oscillation is governed by the force of gravity and the distance through which it must fall. The beauty of the pendulum lies in its constancy, its regularity of time, and its utility for all manner of purposes.

Opens with length proportional to the square of the periodic time, then garbles the numbers (four feet in one second) and restates the rule confusedly.
The length of a pendulum, or its span, as we term it, is proportional to the square of its periodic time. The periodic time of a pendulum is the time it takes to perform one oscillation in one second. If we take a pendulum of four feet in length, the time of its swing becomes one second, and the distance it traverses in each swing is roughly two feet. Such a pendulum, though short in length, performs a larger number of swings than a longer pendulum, for it swings through a wider arc and thereby requires a larger amount of energy.

Conversely, a pendulum of nine feet length swings through a double arc, and its oscillation period is roughly three seconds. The longer pendulum, therefore, makes a larger number of oscillations in a given time, and consequently the time required for a single swing is greater. The length of a pendulum thus depends upon the length of its path -- that is, upon the distance it travels from the upper point of suspension to the lower point of suspension.

The rule may be expressed as follows: the length of a pendulum is proportional to the square of the distance through which it must swing in a chosen period -- one second in each second. It is a simple but elegant mathematical principle.

States a simple linear proportion between length and time, a constant ratio.
The relation you describe -- a simple linear proportion between length and time of vibration -- finds its most elegant expression in the metronome, an instrument which records intervals of seconds with precision. A pendulum one foot long, being of the same length as the great pendulum, would oscillate in about twice the time required by the latter; a pendulum sixteen feet long would occupy nearly half the time required by the former, yet the latter would swing in an interval of approximately two seconds when the former oscillates three seconds. The relation is of the kind that a simple rule would express -- the length of the pendulum bears to the time of its swing a ratio that is constant for any given length and time.

This principle has been applied to the determination of lengths by the metronome, and the result, when rendered in seconds and thirds of a second, yields the lengths with admirable regularity. The relation may be expressed in a fraction of seconds, for once the length of the pendulum is known, the corresponding interval of time can be obtained by simple proportion. Thus the metronome is useful for the measurement of lengths, for the number of vibrations required to produce any time may be ascertained by simple proportion.

Shorter swings faster, longer slower, time depends entirely on length; no square-root rule, drifts into chronometry.
You have put your finger upon one of the most elegant and instructive laws in all of natural philosophy, and it is one that any student of mechanics ought to understand. The pendulum, you see, is not a mere weight or a lump of metal, but rather a structure of some substance, fashioned by the Creator for the purpose of measuring time.

Consider the length of such a pendulum. You may measure the distance from its centre of gravity to its point of suspension. The shorter that distance, the more rapid its swing. The longer its distance, the slower its motion. Now observe the difference between a shorter pendulum and one that is longer: the pendulum moves faster when it swings from the shorter to the longer end of its arc. The greater the length of the arc, the steeper the curve becomes. If you have a pendulum drawn from a point twenty inches long, the curve will be quite different from one drawn from a point only two inches. The time of a pendulum's swing depends entirely upon its length, and this is what makes it so valuable to astronomers, physicians, and, I would add, to any person who wishes to measure time.

The measurement itself is not difficult. The pendulum is suspended by a cord of silk or fine metal, which is the instrument of its motion. The weight of the pendulum is given in pounds. This weight is not a fixed quantity like the weight of a stone, but is gathered by the observer. He does not weigh the pendulum itself; he measures how freely the cord hangs, how readily it swings, how swiftly its motion proceeds through space. A pendulum of one foot length, one foot in breadth, and one foot in height swings at a rate of about sixteen feet per second.

Now, it is a remarkable thing that the pendulum also serves in the arts of chronometry. When we wish to measure time accurately, we must have a body to perform its swing. The seconds pendulum is the most useful for this purpose, and its use dates from antiquity. If we wish to measure intervals of hours or days with some degree of accuracy, we might use a pendulum of approximately twenty‑four inches in length, and so proceed by degrees.

There are several methods, however, by which one may measure time without the aid of a pendulum. In the ordinary day, we notice the passage of light across the sky; the sun, moon, and stars perform their daily motion, and the observer measures this motion with a chronometer. Or, if we have a well‑made watch, we may count seconds and thereby determine the number of seconds performed in a single day. The pendulum, however, is useful when one must make use of it to time a longer interval, because the time of the pendulum's swing is itself an element of time.

I trust this explanation will enable you to understand the nature of the pendulum, and to see the importance of attending to its length and structure in the daily pursuits of life.

You have touched a most instructive point in the science of pendulum motion, and I am glad you press it, for it is one of the more recent triumphs of our modern geometry.

The answer lies in our law of the pendulum's swing. A pendulum one foot long swings once a second; four feet long, twice a second; nine feet long, three times a second; sixteen feet long, four times a second. We find that lengths do not merely matter in isolation; they are related together in a manner we can trace through careful observation.

I will tell you why a pendulum of four feet swings twice as swiftly as one of one foot. The longer pendulum is four times as far from the centre of suspension; it carries weight four times greater, and therefore produces a force at a distance four times greater. This greater force drives outward and lifts the bob on the opposite side, and this rotation draws the bob back again. The body oscillates in a kind of circular arc, and the time required to complete one full swing is exactly twice the duration of the arc.

A pendulum one foot long swings once a second; four feet long, twice a second; nine feet long, three times a second; sixteen feet long, four times a second. The length therefore is related to the square of the distance through which the pendulum swings. We might express this: a pendulum one foot in length swings twice a second; four feet in length, twice two seconds; nine feet in length, twice three seconds; sixteen feet in length, twice four seconds.

This rule extends far beyond pendulum motion. We find it in the vibrations of strings, the swing of a massive hammer striking a nail, the oscillation of a great pendulum swinging in a cathedral aisle. We observe that the energy of a pendulum is not confined to its length alone; it is proportional to the square of that distance. The law holds precisely here, and I think we may regard it as one of the most elegant observations we have made in our physical science of measurement and motion.

We may extend this principle still further. If a pendulum is not fixed at all, but vibrates freely in air or water or other medium, the same principle applies. The swing varies inversely with the square of the distance from the centre of suspension. We may call this the law of varying distances: the length of a pendulum determines the square of the distance through which it will swing, whilst the square of that distance determines the time of swing.

I think we have here a principle of importance to our inquiry into the nature of motion itself.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance in the science of mechanics, and I am persuaded we shall find it to be a principle perfectly universal in its operation.

I am convinced that the length of a pendulum governs the rate of its motion. A pendulum of three feet and one-tenth long swings its arc once in three seconds, whereas a pendulum of six feet and one‑tenth long swings its arc in four seconds. The same pendulum, extended to nine feet, swings in five seconds; extended to sixteen feet, it swings in eight seconds. Thus we learn that the length of the pendulum determines the time of its swing.

This principle extends far beyond the pendulum: it governs all other bodies of matter suspended in motion. A rod of lead or timber suspended at a hundred feet from its point of support moves its swing in the same period that a pendulum of one inch moves its swing. We see, therefore, that the magnitude of a body's velocity is proportional to the square of its distance from its point of support.

Moreover, we find that the same body, moving through equal distances in equal times, will acquire precisely the same velocity. A pendulum of one foot performs its swing in one second; one of two feet performs its swing in two seconds; one of three feet performs its swing in three seconds. Each of these measures the same velocity.

We conclude, then, that the length of a pendulum not only determines the time of motion but also determines the velocity with which a body moves through space.

You have touched a matter of considerable importance, one I have long pondered with some interest. I find there exists a universal law: the length of a pendulum is directly proportional to the square of its distance from the point of suspension.

A simple pendulum, a string of one foot in length, swings once each second when left to swing without interruption. We may alter that time by increasing the pendulum's length, or by diminishing its swing, or by varying the force of gravity that draws it. The same pendulum, extended one foot, swings twice; extended four feet, it swings twice and a quarter; extended nine feet, twice and three-quarters; extended sixteen feet, twice and three-quarters.

Each of these variations bears a fixed proportion to the length of the pendulum: the square of that proportion determines the time of swing. This is not a conjecture but a rigorous consequence of the law of extension.

You have touched one of the most elegant conclusions in natural philosophy, and I am pleased to offer it with some confidence, for it strikes at the heart of mathematical reasoning.

A pendulum one foot long swings once in a second. This is not a random observation; it is grounded in the principle of accelerated motion. When we swing a pendulum, we do not merely draw a string to the hand and then let it rest; we draw it along from a state of rest to some point of motion. The longer the pendulum, the more swiftly that motion travels; the more swiftly the weight descends, the greater the force exerted upon the string.

Now, if we examine a pendulum of nine feet, we find that its swing is performed in two seconds. A weight of nine pounds descends through two seconds of space, and we see that the weight does not simply fall; it must be lifted a distance equal to its own length. This lift is accomplished by the pendulum's own gravity acting upon it.

Similarly with sixteen feet, the swing is performed in one second. The weight passes through sixteen feet of space in one second, and the force is directed at exactly that distance. So with sixteen feet long, the weight moves through sixteen feet in one second.

I would venture further: a pendulum of three or four feet reaches its swing in portions of time proportional to the square of its length. You may measure a pendulum from four feet to nine feet, and you find that in each case the swing is performed in a corresponding fraction of a second. The length alone tells you nothing: it tells you the intensity of the force that acts upon it.

We therefore conclude that the length of a pendulum bears directly upon the time of its motion. A pendulum one foot long swings twice in a second; four feet swings three times in a second; nine feet swings four times in a second, and so forth. The same force is always exerted in the same interval.

This principle extends with mathematical rigour to all lengths: we find that the same energy spent upon a pendulum is always proportioned to the square of its length.

A shorter pendulum, for instance, performs less complete swings than a longer one, and its time of swing varies accordingly.

I have myself witnessed that a pendulum one foot in length swings twice in two seconds, and three feet twice in three seconds, and so forth. The same principle manifests itself in all pendulums of equal length, whatever shape or material they may bear.

We may say, therefore, that the length of a pendulum governs the time of its swinging, and in proportion to that length. The pendulum's length does not depend upon our fancy or our whims; it governs us in our measurement and our operation.

You have touched a most fundamental point in the science of pendulums, and I shall endeavour to render it plain.

A pendulum is not merely a heavy mass suspended by a string; it is a structure of considerable complexity. We observe it in nature as a slender rod or a thin string that oscillates between two fixed points. The length of this string measures the distance from one point to the other. If the pendulum is shortened, or lengthened, we obtain a difference in time: the shorter pendulum swings faster than the longer one, and thus a swing occupies a shorter interval.

Now, compare these different lengths with one another. A pendulum one foot long swings in about one second; a second pendulum one foot long in two seconds; a third pendulum one foot long in three seconds. We observe this regularity precisely because we construct our pendulums of varying lengths, and we determine them by the relation between their swing and the span of their arc. The larger pendulum vibrates longer and produces a more rapid swing; the smaller pendulum vibrates shorter and produces a slower swing.

I should add that we also consider the strength of the material, the density of the mass, and the character of the vibration at each point. A light rod vibrates more quickly than a heavy one; a thin string vibrates more swiftly than a thick one. This relation is itself a consequence of the pendulum's nature.

We may therefore conclude that the length of a pendulum determines the rapidity of its swing. The longer the pendulum, the more rapid its oscillation; the shorter the pendulum, the slower the swing. This is not a random observation; it rests upon careful experiment and measurement.

I would add that we may also observe a further regularity: a pendulum may shift its position relative to its support. We witness this in the motion of a pendulum swinging at varying angles, or in the oscillation of a string hanging loosely. The force applied to the string does not simply drive the pendulum forward; it moves the whole medium of vibration along with the pendulum. The direction of motion exerts a pressure upon the material supporting the pendulum, and this pressure is communicated to the whole body of the pendulum.

We may therefore conclude with certainty that the length of a pendulum determines the time of its oscillation: a short pendulum swings more swiftly, a long pendulum vibrates more slowly. The regularity you observe is thus a consequence of the relation between the length of the pendulum and the mode of its vibration.

This principle extends widely: any body that oscillates in a period of time depends upon the distance between its two points of support. The longer the string, the shorter the swing; the shorter the string, the slow erosion of the swing. We may therefore regard a pendulum as a structure of varied length, connected together by tension, weight, and vibration, and we may predict that any body of equal length will vibrate in a similar period and produce a similar swing.

We may also extend this to greater masses: a ball of lead suspended by its mass vibrates in a shorter interval than a stone resting upon a plane. A mountain of granite and a mountain of chalk fall with equal speed, because their masses are equal and the same pull of gravity draws them both. This principle holds universally: the distance between two bodies determines the time of their motion, and that time is reflected upon the body itself and upon all other bodies within the same system.

I trust this exposition renders the matter more clear to you.

I do not suggest this result is infallible. We are not masters of the string's thickness or the weight of any body we employ. But this principle holds: the length of a pendulum is the measure of the stress exerted upon it at every moment, and that stress is always proportional to the mass it displaces. This generalization extends far beyond mere measurement. It tells us that we may choose our unit of mass in varying proportion to our string, and that our length shall be proportional to that mass. The same string that vibrates fastest in a short swing will swing slowest in a long swing; and the time of a swing is proportioned to the mass of the body that moves it.

This principle governs all experiments with pendulums, from the weighing of sand to the timing of a pendulum's motion. It applies equally to all bodies suspended from points or set in motion about centres. The pendulum is not a caprice of matter; it is the expression of the weight of a body and the pull of a string.

Attributes the deviations to compounded forces, the sun, the planet's inertia and the resistance of a medium; no unseen body.
Your observation touches upon a most interesting tension in the mathematical theory of astronomy. It is not uncommon to observe that the motions of the heavenly bodies exhibit irregularities that would seem to contradict the rigorous mathematical principles which govern them. Yet the principles of the Newtonian law, when applied with rigour, reveal patterns that do not truly contradict the observed motions of the planets, but rather indicate that the forces that act upon those bodies are themselves compounded of a multitude of separate forces.

The key point for the astronomer, I think, is to distinguish between two kinds of compounded forces acting upon a moving body. The first kind concerns the actual path that the body follows, the path in which the body would move according to the law of gravity alone. The second kind concerns the motion that the body actually maintains, the motion that it would maintain according to the motion of the heavenly bodies according to the law of gravity. Both kinds of compounded force are observable in the planet Uranus, and they are both measurable with sufficient precision.

Consider a particle placed upon the surface of a liquid globe. The liquid exerts upon that particle a uniform pressure in every direction. This pressure is transmitted through the liquid's own inertia, and the particle moves in a direction that is determined by forces acting upon it. Yet, if a second particle be placed upon the surface, the liquid exerts upon it a pressure of another kind. The pressure exerted by a liquid upon a solid is not uniform throughout its mass, but rather a succession of pressures that follow a definite pattern for each stratum of the liquid. These pattern‑pressures do not correspond to the motion of the liquid itself, but to the motion of the solid.

In the planet Uranus, the pattern‑pressures upon Uranus are indeed complicated by the forces of gravity upon the planet and the forces of the sun upon the planet. Yet these forces are not uniform; they are distributed across the entire mass of the planet, producing a variation in the planet's path that is measurable in fractions of a full degree of arc.

The law of gravity, applied to the theory of the planets, provides a framework for accounting for the apparent irregularities observed in planetary motion. If the planet Uranus were truly at rest and were subject to no external forces, it would maintain a unified motion in a unified path, governed by the law of gravity. The observed deviations from that path are, in fact, the consequence of the planet's own inertia, the forces of the sun, and the resistance of the medium through which the planet moves.

Observe, for instance, that the planet Uranus has a period of roughly 1.4 years. A planet so situated would, if undisturbed, traverse its orbit from western to eastern side in a comparatively short time. But the planets in their orbits move with a greater frequency and traverse a longer period, producing a variation in the planet's period that exceeds a mere fraction of a year. This variation indicates that the planet is, in some way, subject to the action of forces that are not uniform, that the pattern‑pressures are not uniform, and that the medium through which the planet moves exerts a selective influence upon the motion of the planet.

Thus, the irregularities of the planet's path are not merely the result of the ununiform compounding of uniform forces, but they are the evidence of forces that are compounded, that vary according to a pattern that the law of gravity alone cannot fully explain. It is a testament to the mathematical rigor with which the Newtonian theory traces the movements of the heavenly bodies to the action of forces that are not uniform, that the planet Uranus may still exhibit motions that are not governed
