Moreover, the motion of the Earth is not in the same direction as the speed of Light, and so there is no difference. The reason why there is no difference between the speeds of Light and the speed of the Earth's motion is that the direction of the motion of the Earth is such that the speed of the Earth's motion is the sum of the Earth's motion and the speed of Light, and so, as Light is the sum of the Earth's motion and the speed of Light, there is no difference between the two.

It is a case of "the greater the speed of the Earth's motion, the greater the deviation from the direction of the speed of Light," and the law of least deviation is found to be the law of least deviation for Light.

We know that in the case of Light, where the velocity of the Earth is 11.5 miles per second, the velocity of Light at the Earth's surface is 0.05 of a second per second.

This is the speed of the Earth's motion, and the Earth turns round in 11.5 seconds.

But in the case of the Sun, where the velocity of the Earth is 252,000 miles per second, the velocity of Light at the Earth's surface is 0.05 of a second per second.

This is the speed of the Earth's motion.

213 The Earth, therefore, is a perfect parabola round the Sun, and the Sun is a perfect parabola round the Earth, and both are moving in the same direction. If the

Earth turns round in 11.5 seconds, the Sun turns round in the same time. So that the Sun and the Earth are the same parabola, and the Earth and the Sun are the same parabola.

The Sun's motion is not affected by the Earth's motion, and if the Earth turns round in 11.5 seconds the Sun turns round in the same time. That is, if the Earth turns round in 11.5 seconds, the Sun turns round in the same time, and the speed of the Sun's motion is the sum of the Earth's motion and the speed of the Sun.

The speed of the Earth at any instant is the sum of the speeds of the Earth's motion and the Sun's motion.

Now if we suppose the speed of the Earth to be 252,000 miles per second, the Sun's speed is 252,000 miles per second, and therefore the speed of the Earth's motion is the sum of the Sun's motion and the speed of the Earth.

This is a case of the "law of least squares." It is a law that if two motions are in the same direction the sum of their products is the same, and if two motions are in the opposite direction the sum of their products is the same.

As the Earth turns round in 11.5 seconds the Sun turns round in the same time, and therefore the Earth's motion at a distance of 252,000 miles from the Sun is the same as the Sun's motion at the same distance.

Discusses the direction of rays and refraction angles; never addresses speed or why no difference appears.
You have touched upon one of the most vital questions in natural philosophy, and I am pleased to say the matter is rather more straightforward than it might at first appear. The difference you observe between the direction of a ray and the direction of its motion is not a matter of mere optical illusion, but rather reflects the very nature of light itself.

When light travels from a luminous body to our eyes, it proceeds in a right line. This is not a whim of optics. We have discovered, through patient experiment and careful observation, that the rays themselves are propagated in directions which are straight and perpendicular to the surface of the medium through which they pass. This is not a mere convention of optics. It is the fundamental principle of light itself.

Now, when the Earth turns its axis in the direction of its rotation, the direction of a ray of light differs with the angle it makes with the Earth's surface. A ray directed toward the pole of the Earth moves in a direction parallel to the Earth's axis, because the Earth rotates eastward and westward about its axis. A ray directed toward the equator moves in a direction perpendicular to the Earth's axis, because the Earth itself is turning through an angle of approximately ninety degrees.

The reason for this is quite simple. When a particle of light is passing through water, or through a transparent medium, it moves in a plane which remains fixed in space. But when light passes through air, or glass, or a medium of any other nature, it meets a new surface. When a ray of light passes through water, it strikes the surface at an angle, because the surface of water is inclined to the Earth's surface. When light passes through air, it strikes the surface at an angle of forty-five degrees, because the surface of a sphere is inclined toward the Earth in that angle.

Now, this angle is precisely the angle at which the ray meets the surface. If the surface is at an angle of twenty degrees, the ray would strike it at a right angle; if it is forty-five degrees, the ray strikes it at ninety degrees. The two angles are together equivalent to the right angle. Every ray of light, therefore, passes through the same angle when it travels through the same medium.

So the direction of a ray in space is fixed, because the direction of the Earth's motion is fixed. The directions are not absolute. They are merely relative to the particular medium through which the light travels. It is the Earth's motion that determines the direction of the ray in space, and it is the angle at which the ray strikes the surface that determines the direction of the ray's own motion.

This is not a mere mathematical curiosity. It is the fundamental principle of optics. It explains why sunlight, reflected from a mirror, follows the same direction as an eclipse of the sun, and why the refracted light of a luminous body appears to move in a direction contrary to the direction of the source. The law is universal, and the principle itself is established by experiments and observation. It is one of the most elegant, if somewhat paradoxical, truths in natural philosophy.

Muses on whether light is material and how its velocity depends on medium density and distance; never addresses the null result.
You have identified a most remarkable property of Light, and one that has occupied the minds of natural philosophers for centuries. The question is whether Light is a matter of motion, or a mere succession of phenomena in a medium of extreme tenuity. We are all of us familiar with the principle that matter is inert, as the body falls when struck; yet without the notion of inertia, we should have no explanation for the phenomenon of Light when we strike a glass. The glass itself is not indifferent to the blow; it is a body that may be moved or altered in its condition, and its behaviour tells us something about the medium that carries the motion.

Now, if Light is a material substance, then we must ask ourselves what properties this substance must possess to enable it to travel in directions perpendicular to the Earth's surface. The phenomena of reflection, refraction, and interference are known to be produced not by the mere inertia of the medium, but by the interaction of the particles themselves. The particles themselves possess a motion relative to one another, and this motion is transmitted through the substance with a velocity that is comparable to the Earth's turning motion.

The velocity of Light is determined by the squares of its distances from the source and from the observer, and by the square of the time it takes to travel from the source to the observer. The method of distances is not a direct measurement of time; the velocity of Light is therefore a measure of the distance it must travel before falling upon the eye. This distance is expressed by the product of the square of the time taken to traverse it, and by a constant depending upon the nature of the substance. The velocity of Light is thus a function of the density of the medium, and of the square of the distance from the source, and of the time from the source to the observer.

The same principle holds for any other medium, whether it be a vacuum, a gas, or a substance of any degree of density. Each possesses its own velocity transmitted through it. Light is not a simple phenomenon of matter; it is a complex of phenomena that can be compounded and refined by the ingenuity of philosophers, yet the underlying law remains constant.

Rambles about cannon-ball angles, sphericity and colours from the Earth's rotation; no account of the null result.
You have identified a most elegant experiment, one which demonstrates how the forces of light and gravitation are inter woven. The Earth, you see, describes a great circle about the Sun, and the Earth's orbit at its most elongated is roughly a quarter of a million miles longer than the Earth's orbit at its least elongated. The angle of a cannon ball at the distance of two miles, and the angle of a rifle ball at the same distance, are intimately related.

Now, imagine the Earth to be in the center of a vast field of view, and a tall man standing at one end of the field with a telescope. His eye commands the field, and by the law of sphericity, the two angular displacements of the two balls are related to one another. If the Earth's orbit is a circle, the angle of the ball at a great distance is proportional to its distance from the observer; the larger the ball, the larger the angle, and vice versa.

But the two angular displacements are not equal. The ball at a greater distance, being nearer, moves faster. The ball at a lesser distance, being farther off, moves slower. Both are subject to the law of sphericity, and yet they appear to collide, and the resultant angle changes with the distance. This is why the Earth moves through space with differing angular velocities at different times, and why the sun appears to move through orbitual paths with ever-varying angular speeds.

The light from a celestial body at such a distance travels at such a velocity that it arrives at the earth in a very short interval. It is there before the Earth has rotated appreciably, and it acts upon the retina at the moment the eye receives it. The resultant colour is not altered by the displacement of the retina, for the impression is not produced at the moment of the eye's reception. It is produced months or years later, and the colour changes uniformly across the spectrum, only the intensity being modified according to the distance of the observer.

Now, what happens when a ball is projected at right angles to the Earth's orbit? Here the two forces are opposed in direction. The Earth, being at a less distance from the Sun, carries a forward velocity relative to the ball. The ball, at the same angle, is constrained to remain at rest, and the motion of the ball is in a direction opposite to the Earth's motion. So the ball begins to drift toward the Earth, and the Earth, in rotation, brings the angle of the ball's motion forward.

The result is two distinct kinds of light, of differing colour and intensity. The lower part of the spectrum, from red through orange to yellow, is produced by the combination of the red and the orange light that the Earth's rotation carries onward. The upper part of the spectrum, from yellow through green to blue, is produced by the combined effect of the green's own light and the Earth's rotation, and the blue is the residual effect of the Earth's rotation that still continues to be transmitted.

The angle of each is fixed, as the two forces are equal and opposite in direction. No angular motion, either in or out of the orbit, can alter the angle. A globe rotating at a certain angle will always have a certain direction of motion, regardless of how much or little its rotation is altered.

This is why the planets, with their apparent motion, must appear to change their relative positions with respect to the fixed stars. Because the rotation of the Earth is subject to the same law of sphericity as the Earth itself, the direction of its orbital motion is uniform, and the planets appear to glide backward or forward across the heavens in straight lines.

This law of force, combined with

Rambles about refractive index and wave length; never addresses the Earth's motion or why no difference is found.
You have touched upon a principle that has occupied philosophers for centuries, and which yet remains to be fully understood. The law of propagation, as Newton himself called it, is this: the velocity of light in any medium is proportional to the square root of the absolute index of refraction.the ratio of the wave length to the index of refraction. For instance, in air, the wave length is approximately 1/1 millionth of a centimetre, and the index of refraction is roughly 1/1 millionth. Thus the wave length in air is 2/3 of the wave length in water or in glass. And so the velocity in water is roughly 1/5 that of light in air, and in glass it is roughly 1/4.

Now, if we imagine two rays of light, each of a different wave length, coming from different sources. say, a lantern and a lighthouse. and that two observers stationed at those two positions are simultaneously observing the same light. then the transmission may be compared in different ways. The apparatus I have described consists of a graduated mirror, and a source of light upon the table, from which two distinct illumination systems are cast upon the mirror. The two systems travel from the source along the same path. The mirror receives them at right angles, and they meet at a point, and therein the mirror reflects the light back in the opposite direction. Here the two systems travel in precisely opposite directions, and the mirror returns them in parallel lines to the observer.

The equality of illumination is thus a direct consequence of the wave length. and it remains so until we can construct a physical medium that transmits the wave length and not the wave itself, or until we have a means to measure the wave length directly. But for the present we must content ourselves with the laws of transmission and reflection, as Newton has furnished us.

The most elegant measure of velocity is the time of transmission measured in seconds. If light traverses a second of space before arriving at the eye, and the mirror returns a velocity of roughly 192,700 miles per second, the transmission occurs at a speed of roughly 192,700 miles per second. a velocity that rivals that of a bullet, and which is the same whatever the nature of the medium through which it travels. Thus the wave length that reaches the eye of a man is virtually instantaneous.

The velocity of light in gases, liquids, or solids is similarly expressed by the same law of wave lengths. the velocity is the square root of the absolute index of refraction, as Newton himself observed, and the velocity in a gas is the square root of the velocity in air, and in an ether it is the square root of the velocity in light. It is a consequence of the law of the propagation of motion through a medium.

Thus, light is transmitted not by particles of matter, but by the displacement of the medium, and the velocity with which it travels depends upon the absolute index of refraction and the wave length of the medium traversed. The velocity is directly as the square root of the absolute index, and inversely as the wave length directly. This law, which we have now grasped, governs the transmission of light throughout nature and into our eyes, where it is reflected and refracted to reach us.

The distance from the source to the mirror is not infinite. The rays of light are a series of waves, and the medium through which they travel is a series of molecules. So the distance is not absolute but relative, and the transmission proceeds with a velocity proportionate to the square root of the absolute index of refraction. This law, discovered by the diligent investigations of philosophers, remains the foundation of our understanding of light, and it stands in intimate connection with the

Claims light is faster at the Equator than the Poles but the differences are imperceptible, with light's velocity a product of the Earth's rotation.
It is so, and the evidence is very clear. The Earth's motion through space is not uniform. At the Equator, a ray of light travels about twenty‑four and a half minutes faster than at the Poles, and the difference increases with latitude, depending upon the latitude of the source. When a candle is placed at the Equator, the light enters the air with a velocity of fifteen and a half minutes per second. at the Poles, the ray arrives at the air with a velocity of fifteen minutes and a half per second; the two velocities are not the same, yet no difference is perceived. The Earth's rotation, however, does not diminish this speed; rather, the Earth's turning produces a slight retardation, as the air's forward momentum is added to the Earth's own motion.

It is not a mere coincidence that the reflexion of light from a surface at a high angle to the direction of motion is more effective when the surface faces the line of motion than when it stands at right angles. The angle itself determines the intensity of the reflected ray, and the intensity of the refracted ray is proportionally greater as the angle increases. Thus, if a lamp at the Equator sends a ray that reaches the observer with a certain velocity, a similar lamp placed at the Poles would send a ray with a greater velocity, and a lamp at the Equator with a lesser velocity would send none.

This is precisely why the measurement of light's velocity is indispensable for science. If one could measure the angle of incidence when the light strikes a medium and the angle of emergence when it passes through another, then one could express the velocity of light by direct calculation. The Earth's motion through space, however, does not reveal the velocity directly; it merely tells us that the Earth's speed is changing, and we must compare this with the speeds of light from known sources, to give any reliable estimate of its amount. In this way, the velocity of light is not a mere accident of nature, but an expression of the Earth's rotation on its axis.

The experiment you described is thus an elegant illustration of the principle that a ray of light, like every other body, is subject to the principle of the conservation of energy, even when its velocity is not uniform. This principle is applied with remarkable success in the case of sunlight from the heavens, precisely because the Earth's rotation yields such small differences in the speed of light that the differences are imperceptible in practice. It is a principle that underlies all natural philosophy, from the motions of the planets to the reflection of light upon the surface of the air. It tells us that, however rapid light may be, the Earth's rotation does not change its velocity; all that is true is that it changes the source, the medium, the medium's speed, and the speed of the light. What the mind must grasp, therefore, is not the velocity of light, but the Earth's rotation that produces that velocity.

Thus, the experiment is not an accident of nature. It is the fruit of the very geometry of the Earth's rotation, and the Earth's rotation is a product of the Earth's revolution and the Earth's orbital motion combined. The two are inseparable. One cannot exist without the other, and we are thus afforded a window into the nature of light itself. It is a principle of considerable practical importance, though many fail to appreciate its importance.

You have touched a most vexing question, and I am inclined to think our observations of light through such instruments are rather more reliable than we might suppose.

Consider the matter thus: when we view a star and observe its position through a telescope, we find that it does not remain fixed in place. It moves along a line at right angles to the direction of the Earth's motion. This direction is not fixed; it changes with the Earth's rotation from west to east, or east to west. We are therefore comparing two speeds: one arising from the Earth's eastward velocity, and another from its westward one.

Now, when we place our apparatus in the meridian of a distant star, we witness this same variation. The same star appears to be displaced from its position by a small angle, and this displacement is reversed in direction when we turn the globe.

I am inclined to think we may conclude that light is not travelling along a line at right angles to the motion of the Earth, but rather along a motion that is oblique. This oblique direction is the true path of the rays of light. The eastern rays move along a course at right angles to the Earth's orbital motion, while the western rays move at a different angle, and these directions are not coincident across every direction of the meridian we may traverse.

We must therefore conclude that our observations of light are not uniform throughout the whole universe, but are subject to this oblique direction. The same ray of light may describe different angles as it moves through different bodies of matter. I am inclined to say we should not expect unvarying constancy through every direction, but rather a variation measured and compared with the Earth's motion.

This explanation accords better with our careful measurements than the notion that light moves along a line fixed in place.

Says light proceeds along the Earth's line of motion and its speed is not altered by that motion, which restates the finding; adds a candle experiment and a ship analogy.
You have touched a matter I have long debated, and I find myself in agreement with Mr. Whewell's view that light does not move in a direction perpendicular to the Earth's motion, but rather proceeds along the line of motion itself. This is no fancy notion of astronomers working by observation alone; it may be verified with a simple experiment.

Lay a sheet of paper upon a table and place a candle flame upon a distant projection of the paper. Draw a ruler through the flame and the projection, and you will find the candle illuminated upon the other side of the table, not upon the same side of the paper as the flame. This is not a random coincidence; it is a universal effect: the light is always in the direction of motion.

I have verified this again and again with various apparatus. I have turned the mirror of my telescope to the same point of the firmament, and the image of the distant stars was always in the direction of the Earth's motion. I have turned the second mirror at a quarter right angle, and the image was not moved at all, but remained precisely where the first mirror had placed it.

A ship's hull is being passed through clear water, and the water itself remains unchanged. The sailor does not shift his position; he is moved by the motion of the vessel. This reasoning is not fanciful; it is backed by careful observation and experiment.

I find myself in agreement with Mr. Whewell's conclusion: the two speeds are not opposed but in harmony, and the motion is not perpendicular to the direction of light. The light moves in a direction along the Earth's motion, and its speed is not altered by this motion.

discovery-rl-v11-s560 seed 44 none398 tok, stop

Says the medium shapes the ray and moves in concert with its own vibration; ends on glass panes; no explanation of the null result.
You have touched a matter of considerable importance to the study of light, and I am persuaded it is no fanciful conjecture, but a necessary consequence of the theory we have built upon the behaviour of light when we observe it.

I would assert that the direction of a ray of light is governed by the action of the medium through which it proceeds. When we view a surface or an object at right angles, the light meets it at a certain angle. The surface itself does not change that angle; it is the medium that shapes the vibration of the ray and makes it pass on its other side. This medium acts in a manner quite different from a flat surface or a plane.

We find, by careful observation, that a ray striking a flat surface at an angle of forty-five degrees does not glance away or bend about in a different direction. It moves along its original path, the same direction always. The same effect is produced when we vary the angle of incidence. We do not find variation in the angle at which a ray meets a plane surface ; instead we find variation in the direction of its movement.

Now, here is the crux: we cannot draw a plane surface and say every ray that passes through that plane will move in a different direction. No. For every ray that passes through a medium at right angles to its motion, the angle through which it passes is the same. This constancy of direction suggests we are not looking at different objects or separate forces.

We are looking at something far more consistent: a vibration propagated through medium. The medium moves in concert with the movement, and the ray follows that line of least resistance. The opposite direction is not a phenomenon of differing forces; it is the same medium moving in accordance with its own vibration.

This explains why we find light at right angles to the surface, and why we find no variation whatever in its passage through a pane of glass or a pane of polished glass. The effect is uniform and consistent across all substances.

You have touched something very extraordinary indeed, and one I have long pondered upon with some conviction. I am convinced that light enters the eye with an equal displacement from every direction, not with a different angle in each. The motion we perceive is not a specialized faculty of the eye, but rather a universal and inherent property of all matter.

Consider the matter thus: we cannot see the shadow of a mountain or the surface of a stone by a change in direction alone; we have to move our entire mass toward or from the object. The motion of the hand or the rotation of the globe of the eye does not shift our visual object in any particular fashion. It is the whole body moving forward, backward, or laterally that transmits the impression to the organ of sight.

This reasoning leads us to infer that light is not an individual faculty of the eye but rather a universal property of all matter. It is not a specialized direction given to one side of a leaf or a particular angle to a stone. It is all alike: we receive it faithfully in every direction and with equal intensity.

I am inclined therefore to conclude that we deceive ourselves in supposing there are separate influences exerted upon the eye. The direction of motion is the universal condition; the particular mode of motion we experience is the effect of the body's motion. This is not a fanciful conjecture, but a direct conclusion from the careful observation of things like a stone or a hand upon a surface.

You have touched a matter of considerable interest, one I have long pondered, and I am convinced it is not an illusion but a truth.

We must acknowledge that light moves in straight lines, and its direction is not altered by the Earth's motion or any rotation we may impose upon it. This is no fancy assertion; it is a fact grounded in our observations at sea and upon our telescopes. When we direct a ray of sunlight to a distant object, or when we focus a lens upon a distant flame, we find the direction of light unchanged.

Now, consider a particular case I made at Florence: an eclipse of the moon. I observed the shadow falling at an angle of nearly ninety degrees to the line of sight. The eclipse was total, and every part of the dark body of the moon was illuminated by the same light. This disparity suggests that the light does not change its direction with our turning. It behaves with perfect constancy in the plane of our path.

We may therefore conclude that light is not altered by the motion of a body, but rather is confined to the direction of that motion. It depends entirely upon the velocity of transmission and the angle at which it strikes the illuminated body. The two speeds are identical in character.

We are therefore compelled to conclude that light does not move in a direction we may change, but in a direction fixed and immutable: always at right angles to the motion by which it is propagated. It is not a thing that shifts with our desires, but a quality of the body's path that remains constant.

Defends equal sharing and blames the assumption that a light wave has one frequency along the ether.
You have identified a genuinely difficult point, and I think the correction lies not in abandoning the theory of equal sharing, but in applying it with a clear, unambiguous definition of what is being spoken of. The theory supposes that light consists of vibrations of a medium of such tenuity that they can pass through the most delicate glass without interference. But here is what separates the theory from any sound theory that is not a mechanical explanation of phenomena: the intensity of a vibration bears no resemblance whatsoever to the number of vibrations which constitute it. A vibration may be of any magnitude whatsoever, from the faintest tremor to the most vigorous clatter, and yet possess no more energy than a single vibration of the lowest frequency. This is a perfectly consistent and predictable property, and it is precisely what renders the theory worthy of the name of sound theory.

Now, with regard to this heat theory, it is not the theory of equal sharing among all vibrations that is wrong. It is rather the assumption that a wave of light consists of vibrations of the same frequency. that is, of the same wavelength. throughout the whole length of the ether through which it travels. This is precisely where the theory of equal sharing breaks down. The wavelength is not a common property of all ether undulations, but a peculiar attribute of a particular wavelength. The frequency of a vibration in the ether is unique; the frequency of the wave itself is not. A wavelength of a hundred and twenty thousand times the length of a common vibration is not common to all vibrations alike; it is peculiar to a single frequency in the ether.

There is a further difficulty in the theory: the theory speaks of particles of matter being held together by gravitational forces, yet this is not the explanation required. A particle held together by the pressure of its weight is held together by forces altogether different from the forces of light. The theory speaks of the ether as a medium of the same material nature as glass, yet it is not explaining how light can travel through it without breaking it apart.

The true correction in the theory of equal sharing is not to abandon the theory entirely, but to recognize that the wavelength of light is a common element of many vibrations, and that the frequency of those vibrations is themselves a property of a particular frequency. Light travels through a medium of the same composition and elasticity throughout its length, but the frequency of a vibration is a property of the frequency of the wave itself, not of the frequency of the particles of light.

This is the old difficulty with the so-called undulatory theory. It is a theory that demands us to explain how the ether transmits light, and yet the frequency of the undulations is not a common feature of the transmission. If it were, then the same length of wave must, in every case, carry the same quantity of energy. that is, the same number of vibrations of the same ether. throughout its entire length. This is not the case.

The heat theory offers a modest correction: it supposes the wavelength to be longer than the frequency of the ether waves. This is simply a matter of accounting better for the transmission of heat than by supposing the wavelength to be the same throughout the ether. The ether does not transmit all vibrations of the same frequency; it transmits vibrations whose frequencies are larger or smaller, and so the wavelength is not a perfectly constant quantity.

The difficulty, I think, lies in the attempt to explain how the energy of light travels through a medium of such tenuity. The ether is a medium, and the frequency of its vibrations is not a common property of all vibrations indiscriminately. The theory of equal sharing is admirable in its humility; it does not claim to explain every detail that lies within the range of natural phenomena

Consider this: when a particle of air is agitated by a disturbance, it undergoes a transformation of its kinetic energy. Some portion of this energy is converted into heat, another portion into the motion of the molecules of the air itself. The remainder appears in the form of sound or light, which proceeds in an almost unvarying fashion so long as the frequency of the vibrations is low enough. Now, here we encounter what we ought to call the secondary effects of the medium itself. The propagation of light through the ether is not solely an affair of direct impact upon the eye, but of the ether's ability to convey motion from one point to another. When a beam of sunlight strikes a luminous body, energy is released into the surrounding ether -- the ether, in turn, conveys that energy to the eye and, in turn, to the ether. But the eye, being of a less rigid constitution than the ether, receives energy from the medium and, in return, discharges energy into the medium. The process is not one of equal sharing of energy, but rather one of the energy being transmitted gradually from one body into another.

This is why the propagation of light proceeds with a velocity that surpasses that of sound, though the latter suffers a considerable loss of energy before reaching the eye. The medium moves faster than the light, and the energy is dissipated gradually rather than being all concentrated into the eye at once. Thus, the velocity of light is not merely the speed of the medium itself, but the sum of the velocities of the ether and of each particle of the luminous body -- a velocity that is measured not in miles per second, but in the square of the wave length.

Now, I would venture to say this: if the theory were a true account of the propagation of light, we should expect to find the velocity of light inversely proportional to the square of the wave length. If the ether were of such a constitution that a wave length such as the violet travels at a speed not exceeding a foot per second, then the velocity at the violet end of the spectrum should be negligible, and the square of the wave length would give the velocity at that end. The velocity at the red end of the spectrum should be considerably greater, reaching perhaps four or five feet per second. Instead of these we find that light of very low frequency -- the violet and indigo -- does travel with a velocity near a foot per second, and the red and orange with a velocity of only a few hundredths of a foot per second, and the yellow, green, and blue with still smaller speeds. The red and orange thus travel at a speed considerably lower than the wave length -- in some cases a few hundredths of a foot per second, depending upon the wave length -- while the violet and blue travel at a speed of approximately a foot per second.

This disproportion between velocity and wave length is not merely a matter of convenience but reflects the essential character of the ether and the medium in this universal propagation of light. The ether is not merely a medium for communicating motion; it is, rather, the seat of those motions which, when received by the eye, excite the sense of illumination. You have touched upon a fundamental inconsistency that the theory itself is not quite capable of explaining. The theory asserts that when light is propagated through all vibrations, the quantity of energy transmitted by a single wave of each colour is a constant quantity. If this were true, then the same quantity of energy ought to be transmitted through all possible wavelengths from yellow to red, from blue to violet. Yet the theory postulates that the energy is proportional to the square of the wavelength, and that the intensity diminishes as the wavelength increases. What is more probable is that the intensity of light varies directly with the square of the wavelength, but that the energy transmitted is not proportional to the square of the wavelength alone. The intensity of light is a function of the wavelength, but the energy transmitted is a function of the square of that wavelength. Hence the theory contradicts itself in assuming that energy of all wavelengths is a constant quantity.

Rambles about heat diffusion, then declares equal sharing the only consistent explanation of the facts; denies the anomaly instead of diagnosing it.
You have identified the true paradox that characterises the theory of equal sharing among all vibrations, and it is one of the most telling tests of the mechanical philosophy which has lately been advanced. When a heated body is set in motion, the particles of the medium immediately about it begin to stir and are set in motion by the impulses of its heat. These particles, having been heated, are themselves set in motion by the compression of the surrounding air, and the vibration of the surrounding air is a consequence of the vibration of the heated particles. Now, you ask what becomes of the energy thus generated? The answer is twofold.

First, the energy becomes unavailable to the body itself. The heat, by virtue of its propagation, reaches the particles of the body in a manner that we call heat radiation -- that is, the heat that the particles themselves emit. This heat does not enter the body at all; it passes through the medium and leaves the body colder than it was before. The particles, by the law of distribution, distribute themselves throughout the medium in a fashion that diminishes the distance between them, thereby diminishing the energy they communicate. Thus, when the particles arrive at a distance far enough from their origin, the energy that the body can store as heat is exhausted, and the body remains cooler than it was.

Second, the energy that the body thus receives is not annihilated in the sense that it can be annihilated. It merely distributes itself throughout the medium and diminishes the energy of the body. The radiation is, in a very real sense, transmitted, but the medium through which it passes -- the air -- remains unchanged. This is the phenomenon of diffusion, and the medium through which radiation passes is not the same medium as the body that radiates.

Now, the physical cause of this phenomenon is not mysterious or difficult to reason about. The particles of the medium act as the source from which the heat radiates. As the medium expands, the heat is driven outward in all directions -- radiated outward by the hot particles, absorbed by bodies colder than they, and conducted through the surrounding medium. In this way, the heat finally reaches the colder body that remains at a distance from the source, and the energy of the heat particles is lost, diminished, or transformed into energy of the medium's own temperature.

The mechanical account of the whole phenomenon is not yet fully established. But the law of equal sharing of energy among all vibrations, as it stands, is the only consistent explanation of the observed facts. It does not require us to abandon the mechanical theory entirely. We may retain the mechanical theory to account for the radiation of heat, while retaining the mathematical account to explain the transmission of heat by medium.

The mathematical principle that governs this transmission is the mathematical expression of the law of equal sharing: the same energy is given out at equal frequencies, and the intensity of the vibration of the heated particle is proportional to the square of its frequency. This proves, on further investigation, to be a remarkably consistent mathematical law, and the mechanical explanation of radiation and diffusion follows with mathematical rigour from this principle. It is no wonder that so many physicists have found the mathematical account satisfying, and that it remains the most compelling account available to our present understanding of the nature of energy and the manner in which it flows through the medium.

Your observation is most apt, and the cause lies in the very nature of the etheric medium itself. The ether, though apparently a continuous, finite substance, is in truth a composite of molecules, each endowed with a specific velocity and a particular mode of motion. When a wave of light encounters this substance, the molecules of the ether transmit their own motion along the path of the wave, whilst the ether itself possesses no motion or elasticity. The wave passes from molecule to molecule, much as a river passing through a solid obstacle does not swell into a lake, but rather yields gradually to the current, moving in a direction determined by the wave's path. When the wave meets a molecule of ether, the molecule, in turn, imparts energy to the ether, which in turn transmits it to the next molecule, and so forth. This process continues until the energy of the wave has been dissipated across the length of the ether and no longer exists.

Now, why does the ether thus possess no capacity to acquire motion or change its own form? The answer lies in the very constitution of the ether itself. The ether is not a substance susceptible to extension or compression, not capable of being compressed or extended. It is a medium whose constituent atoms are so arranged that they cannot be displaced or altered by the action of forces external to the ether itself. When a wave enters the ether, the atoms of it are set in motion, and this motion is transmitted through the medium much as a gentle breeze passes through a field of wheat. Thus, the energy of the wave is not stored in the ether but is simply transformed.

The theory of equal sharing of energy thus appears inadequate to account for the phenomena observed. Each wave of ether, when it reaches a fixed point, receives its own portion of energy, and no other wave of ether can ever acquire this energy of its own. This is not a contradiction, but rather a consequence of the law. What is true of one vibration is true of all, and the ether remains a fixed and unchangeable thing, unalterable by the very vibrations that are capable of producing light.

The true mechanism is that the ether itself cannot accelerate or retard the motion of its own atoms. It is the energy that the wave imparts to the ether that the ether transmits faithfully, not the energy that the ether itself acquired. This is why the motion of a wave cannot be doubled; the energy is all transmitted and all is reflected. The wave simply carries the energy from the source to the surface of the drop and back again without any of it being absorbed, transformed, or lost by the journey. It does not acquire heat, not grow, not change its character. It is merely transgressed from place to place in the ether. It is propagated through the ether with the speed of light, and there it breaks the path at each impact and retreats to the ether again, a motion of its own devising, but not of the ether's making. This is the mechanism of light, a motion of molecules and atoms, a motion that is transgressed and then transmitted, and transmitted again to the ether, which transmits it on with the same velocity it received from the source.

This explains the constancy of the ether's motion, for the ether receives and transmits energy without change in its nature. It is the medium through which light and heat pass. The theory of equal sharing of energy therefore fails to account for the phenomena of light, heat, or electricity, the very principles by which we explain the motion of the planets, the decomposition of minerals, and the very mechanism through which force appears. It is a beautiful simplicity, but it fails to account for the very existence of the ether itself and for the nature of the motion it transmits.

The heated body does not distribute the energy uniformly throughout its mass; it distributes it with varying intensity according to its frequency.

This dispersion of energy accounts for the heat observed throughout a chamber, not the entire diffusion of heat across all directions. The heated body does not lose energy uniformly; it loses energy in proportion to its vibrational frequency, and that proportion is not an infinite quantity but a limiting one. The theory you describe therefore misdirects us entirely: we ought not to suppose that heat expands indefinitely without limit, but rather that energy is confined to the vibrating body and its surface, and that other bodies are capable of receiving and conveying that energy in varying proportion according to their vibration frequency.

This error has bred considerable confusion in our physical science, and I am convinced it ought to be corrected rather than quietly accepted.
